Judgment calls you should know about

 1. The 12 months. The answer leans, with ‘arguably’, to a start on 8 April and states Rocky’s exposure on both
    readings. The sources leave the point open, and the earlier settled analysis leaned weakly the other way. Every
    reviewer accepted the lean because it is hedged and both consequences are given.
 2. Trident. Trident is cited by report, with the judge named and no pin, because the course reading has no CLR page
    markers. The agency premise rests on Brennan J, who is labelled as dissenting; the test itself is cited to Port Jackson
    269 (Mason and Jacobs JJ, quoting Lord Reid) from the official text.
 3. The onus on the 12 February email. It is placed on Grace as the party who would allege that contract
    (Ermogenous [26]). Both markers accepted this.
 4. Citation details. Eccles v Bryant is cited as [1948] Ch 93. Casebook author order is not used, because no casebook
    is cited; every footnote cites a case.
 5. Further point on Godecke. In the full report at 642, Walsh J accepted that a matter may be left to one party. That
    passage is not in the casebook extract. The answer still holds, because what Rocky left open were the terms of his
    own loan, not subsidiary matters. The completeness ledger records this as the one residual risk with no fix.

2. The final answer (as submitted)

Rocky seeks repayment from Grace, the promised $10,000 and protection from AMPL’s call. He must also answer any
claim that he failed to lend ‘by 15 February’.


I. ROCKY v GRACE

The first issue is whether Rocky’s 12 February email was an offer. Rocky would say it merely minutes their meeting: it
opens ‘Confirming discussions’, and its other points promise nothing. Yet ‘I will lend’ fixes the amount, date, term and
rate, and ‘Please confirm agreement’ invites assent: unlike ‘may be prepared to sell’ in Gibson, those words are

promissory.1 The first point is likely an offer, subject to its qualifier.

On acceptance, Rocky may say ‘Confirmed’ vouched only for the email’s accuracy, which is not ‘an external manifestation

of assent to an offer’.2 However, it answered a request to ‘confirm agreement’; adding no terms, ‘Confirmed’ was no
counter-offer and so accepted the first point, qualifier included.

The qualifier, ‘subject to satisfactory conditions’, raises intention to create legal relations and certainty. Intention is
judged objectively, the onus lying on Grace, who alleges a contract, though the parties’ status as ‘project developers’

favours it;3 absent a stipulated formal contract, the question is whether terms were to be ‘put into form’ or ‘subject to a

new agreement’.4 Grace’s best case is Godecke, where an agreement letting the vendor’s solicitors add terms bound,

those terms having to be reasonable ‘in an objective sense’.5 Rocky’s words contain no such standard: ‘satisfactory’ leaves

the judgment to the party the condition protects,6 Rocky; and the ‘conditions’, naming no outside event, are most
naturally unstated terms of his own loan, likely left to a new agreement. Gibbs J doubted that an agreement leaving

‘further terms to be settled by one of the parties’ would bind, leaving open only ‘subsidiary matters’;7 the terms of the
loan itself are not subsidiary.


On certainty, Grace would reply that, as in Meehan, Rocky had to decide honestly:8 that limit takes a case out of the

principle that a promisor with ‘a discretion or option as to whether he will carry out’ his promise makes no contract.9 Yet
honesty there governed a judgment on an external condition, finance, whereas Rocky’s went to the content of his own

loan, not ‘only to the fulfilment of a condition’;10 and honesty cannot supply unstated loan terms. His promise is thus
arguably illusory. The email likely bound neither party, unless the conditions were settled ‘this morning’.

Even a binding email gives Rocky no claim unless AMPL’s advance performed his promise. His 27 February request

suggests so, a principal being party to his agent’s contracts;11 yet AMPL advanced $1,150,000, not $1 million, and took

his guarantee, so it did not contract for Rocky as well as for itself.12 Rocky therefore lent nothing.

Rocky’s demand under ‘the Loan Agreement with AMPL’ raises privity: only a party who provided consideration may sue

on a contract.13 Party status turns on ‘the written instrument itself’,14 which AMPL and Grace made: Grace’s promises in

it were the price of AMPL’s own promises, none made to Rocky as a joint promisee.15 Grace’s ‘I never signed anything

with you’ is right in result but not in reason: Mrs Coulls signed yet was no party.16

Rocky’s 5 March demand, arguably premised on a 27 February start, therefore fails whenever the 12 months began, and
Grace likely has no claim against him. Nor did Grace promise to reimburse anything Rocky pays AMPL.


II. ROCKY v AMPL (ALTERNATIVELY PIERRE)

The first issue is whether a contract for the $10,000 formed on 8 April. What Pierre ‘expected’ promised nothing, so it was

likely an invitation to treat;17 Rocky’s ‘I will do that if you also promise’ offered a promise for a promise, a bilateral

contract,18 which Pierre accepted when he ‘agreed’.19 AMPL may say nothing bound until signing: parties are bound

‘when, and in the manner in which, they intend’;20 yet nothing suggests Rocky was to sign anything. A contract likely
formed orally.

Whether AMPL or Pierre contracted turns on agency: Pierre bound AMPL if, with its authority, he contracted for it rather
than solely for himself.21 Although Rocky’s ‘you’ points to Pierre, Pierre controls AMPL and spoke at its office about its
loan. AMPL therefore likely made the $10,000 promise and took Rocky’s undertaking; if Pierre spoke for himself, the
undertaking ran to Pierre, leaving AMPL only the guarantee clause.

On consideration, AMPL may invoke the existing legal duty rule: if, as ‘also’ may imply, the guarantee already bound

Rocky, his promise to ensure payment gave nothing new, like the promise to ensure delivery already owed in Sundell.22

However, the rule’s settled application is to a duty under ‘a pre-existing contract’ with the promisee,23 and none is shown:
Rocky only ‘asked’ on 27 February; and ‘Pierre agreed and the clause was included’ suggests the clause followed the
bargain. Even if the guarantee came first, ensuring that ‘all payments’ were made ‘promptly’ exceeds guaranteeing

‘repayment of the Loan’; and a promise to Pierre to perform a duty owed to AMPL, a third party, can be consideration.24
Rocky likely gave consideration.


Intention is contested because Pierre is Rocky’s cousin; yet presumptions at best identify who bears the onus,25 here
Rocky. A promise of a fixed sum, made at AMPL’s office as its loan was finalised, is ‘appropriately promissory in

character’ and commercial.26 Intention is likely present.


The Loan Agreement fixes the payments, so ‘ensure’ is not ‘so obscure’ as to defeat certainty;27 the question is whether
Rocky promised a result or only effort, which his check-ins would satisfy. Read objectively, Rocky’s unqualified ‘I will do

that’, answering an expectation that he ‘ensure’ payment, names an outcome, so he likely promised a result.28

The next issue is whether the guarantee binds Rocky: ‘the personal guarantee provided by Rocky’ proves that one was

given, not its content. Privity prevents AMPL and Grace’s clause binding him of its own force,29 so AMPL must show his

assent, not his signature. Silence while others write a clause is generally not ‘an external manifestation’ of assent;30

Rocky’s ‘I will do that’, priced by the $10,000 promise, likely binds him at least as an undertaking.31 Having ‘attended’
with a reasonable opportunity to reject the clause, he arguably knew its words; although the $10,000 priced his own
words, not the clause’s, taking that promise knowingly and with no objection shown arguably signalled assent to the

clause.32 If he never saw it, he is bound only by his undertaking.


Consideration for the guarantee must also move from AMPL as promisee:33 if AMPL promised the $10,000, that promise

supplies it. If Pierre did, the advance, past consideration,34 though made at Rocky’s request, counts only if the parties

‘understood that the act was to be remunerated’;35 nothing shows a guarantee was then expected. AMPL’s stronger
answer is that it signed the Loan Agreement only after the clause was included, an act likely ‘really done in consideration

of’ the guarantee,36 which is thus likely supported if he assented.


The call’s timing turns on when the 12 months began. Post-contractual conduct cannot show the term’s meaning;37 but
because the term, fixing no start, is arguably ambiguous, mutually known facts showing ‘the genesis of the transaction’

are admissible.38 AMPL would say the term of ‘the Loan’, which the agreement ‘recorded’, runs from its advance on 27
February. Yet ‘recorded’ governs the 4% rate as much as the sum, and nothing shows any term was fixed before 8 April:
the genesis shows when the money moved, not that a term first agreed on 8 April began earlier. The term therefore
arguably ran from 8 April, making the call premature unless the Loan Agreement’s words date it from the advance.

The last issue is the condition ‘once Grace repays the Loan’, a condition precedent to AMPL’s obligation, not to the

contract, the bargain having bound at once.39 Nothing was payable on 9 March 2025, when Grace ‘had not yet repaid’.

Rocky may say that, read commercially, his own payment suffices;40 but the words name Grace, and the $10,000 priced
his promise that Grace would pay, not his paying instead. Nor does AMPL’s duty to co-operate assist: it reaches only what

is necessary on AMPL’s part,41 and repayment is Grace’s act. Payment by Rocky therefore likely leaves the condition
unfulfilled.

If Rocky assented to the clause, by which he ‘personally guaranteed repayment of the Loan’, he is liable for $1,150,000
once the Loan is due and unpaid: now on the 27 February reading, and on the 8 April reading from 8 April 2025 if Grace
has not repaid. If he is bound only by his oral undertaking, Grace’s failure to pay when due, arguably including March’s
interest, payments being stated only until ‘8 February 2025’, likely breaches it and sounds in damages. Rocky’s $10,000
claim, against AMPL or alternatively Pierre, likely stands; being conditional,42 it falls due only once Grace repays, so
never if Grace, who said he ‘might not pay at all’, does not, and likely never if Rocky pays instead.

STUDENT: Yusuf Ahmed Marked by: independent marker f7, mark 1 Script: MARKS/f7/in/ANSWER.md (final submission
text, marked exactly as written)

Holistic mark (Part A): 87 / 100 (High Distinction) Rubric mark (Part B, SHEET_v12 read with RULINGS to R-AE): 88 /
100 (88.625 rounded down) Verifier: 41 quotations, 0 FAIL, 2 CHECK PIN (Brambles 176 and Pao On 629 to 630, both the
known benign parser cases); body 1,496 words, stated ‘Word count: 1,499’ = body + 3 (R-P), within 1,500; one lint, fn 11
Trident with no pin, accepted under R-Y(1). Voice (voice_stats_v2.py): mean sentence 24.0 (range 17.8 to 24.7); under 12
words 14.5%; semicolons 1.28 and colons 1.08 per 100 words; 51.6% of sentences carry one; quoted words 13.8%, longest
quotation 11 words; no dashes, double quotation marks, contractions or consecutive short sentences. Every measure is
inside his range.



Part A. Cold read
I read the problem, the Instructions and the answer first, then checked every pin I doubted against the course readings
and, for Port Jackson, the saved official text. The margin comments are in the order of the paragraphs.

Opening paragraph. Good. The advice is framed from Rocky’s goals, and the second sentence (‘He must also answer any
claim that he failed to lend’) sets up the counterclaim that later explains why the onus at intention sits on Grace. This is
the client-first framing a practitioner would use.

I, paragraph 1 (offer). Strong. Gibson is distinguished on its operative words, ‘may be prepared to sell’ against ‘I will
lend’ with amount, date, term and rate, and ‘Please confirm agreement’ is read as inviting assent; both readings are put
and the classification is hedged (‘likely’) and carried forward with its qualifier. Two things are missing. Grace’s 5
February call (‘proposed that Rocky offer’) is never classified, and the passage of Gibson that cuts for Rocky is not used:
Lord Diplock thought the letter’s indicative paragraph might have read as a firm offer only without its disclaimer, and
‘subject to satisfactory conditions’ is arguably that disclaimer. The bullets are disposed of in a clause (‘its other points
promise nothing’), which is enough for matters that change nothing.

I, paragraph 2 (acceptance). Excellent. Rocky’s best reading of ‘Confirmed’ is put on the Empirnall criterion and met
on the words of the request (‘confirm agreement’); counter-offer is named and excluded, and ‘qualifier included’ carries
the point into intention and certainty.

I, paragraph 3 (intention). Very strong reasoning. The objective test, the onus, the parties’ status as ‘project
developers’, Jessel MR’s adopted question at Masters 363 under its own condition (‘absent a stipulated formal contract’),
and a well-chosen best case for Grace in Godecke (Walsh J), met on the absence of any objective standard: this is critical
reasoning, not a recital. Placing the onus on Grace is right on your framing, because she is the party who would allege the
12 February contract; say so in a clause if you keep it, since a marker reading ‘Rocky v Grace’ expects the onus on the
claimant. What a marker will notice is the absence of Masters v Cameron’s own framework: the ‘only a record of terms’
question at 360 and Niesmann at 361, where the further step conditioned ‘the obligation to pay’ rather than agreement,
which is Grace’s closest course analogy to a qualifier that leaves the essential terms settled. ‘The terms of the loan itself
are not subsidiary’ is stated flatly where the reading it rests on was only ‘most naturally’; ‘arguably’ would match.

I, paragraph 4 (certainty). Excellent. The honesty fetter is put in Grace’s mouth with Meehan (Gibbs CJ at 581; Mason J
at 589 to 590, checked: the quoted words straddle the [590] marker and Mason J is quoting Thorby at 605), then met on
Gibbs CJ’s own limit and on the point that honesty cannot supply unagreed terms. The verdict is ‘arguably’, with the
further information and its direction (‘unless the conditions were settled ’this morning’’). This is the model of a contested
point.

I, paragraph 5 (agency; Rocky lent nothing). Excellent. Rocky’s best fact (his 27 February request) is conceded and
met on the purporting criterion with two facts (the different sum; AMPL taking his guarantee). I checked Port Jackson
269: Mason and Jacobs JJ set out Lord Reid’s four conditions from Scruttons 474 verbatim, so ‘quoting’ and both pins are
right.

I, paragraph 6 (privity). Excellent on the law and on Grace’s planted line (‘right in result but not in reason: Mrs Coulls
signed yet was no party’), and the joint-promisee argument is named and met. One clause is asserted: ‘Grace’s promises
in it were the price of AMPL’s own promises’. Name the price (‘12 months’ at ‘4% per annum’) and mark the inference.
Consideration for the 12 February exchange is never addressed anywhere in Part I; it is uncontested, but an uncontested
element still takes one sentence.

I, concluding paragraph. Good. It carries the 12 months across Parts without a parenthetical, disposes of Grace’s
counterclaim and names the reimbursement claim (C3) in substantive terms.
II, paragraph 1 (offer and acceptance). Offer is excellent (‘expected’ an invitation to treat; a bilateral contract on ‘if
you also promise’). On acceptance, Lord Greene MR’s words are stated as a general rule: he said them of exchanges of
contracts, and the footnote shows it but the text does not. Say ‘as Lord Greene MR said of exchanged contracts’. Masters
361 (no right to withdraw reserved) would close AMPL’s argument fully.

II, paragraph 2 (who promised). Excellent. Both agency questions, the other side’s best fact (‘you’) met with the office
and the loan, a hedged lean, and the consequence of the alternative drawn in the same sentence.

II, paragraph 3 (existing legal duty rule). Excellent. AMPL’s best case (Sundell, paraphrased and cited by report) is
put at full strength and met on four limbs, including the third-party route through Pao On 632. The ‘even if the guarantee
came first’ structure is better than a further-information clause: it shows the order does not change the advice.

II, paragraph 4 (intention). Too thin for a contested issue worth this much. ‘Presumptions at best identify who bears
the onus’ is right and the commercial features are well chosen, but three things are missing: the opponent’s best point
(the undertaking resembles a letter of comfort, and in Banque Brussels the comfort conditioned the lending, which is
weaker here because AMPL had already advanced); what still carries intention if Pierre promised personally, where
cousinhood bears directly on the promisor; and that the same finding binds Rocky’s undertaking. Answer with specificity
to individual Ps: AMPL and Pierre.

II, paragraph 5 (certainty). Good thinking on result against effort, which is what Rocky’s exposure turns on. AMPL’s
own promise (‘once Grace repays’) is not addressed for illusoriness; one clause would do it.

II, paragraph 6 (the guarantee: burden and assent). Excellent. ‘Proves that one was given, not its content’ is exactly
the right use of the stated fact; the burden aspect is cited to Deane J; Empirnall 535 to 536 is applied on its own elements
(opportunity to reject, benefit taken, knowledge from ‘attended’), Rocky’s strongest counter is put (‘the $10,000 priced his
own words, not the clause’s’) and the further information has its direction.

II, paragraph 7 (consideration from AMPL). Strong. Conditional on who promised, past consideration named, Pao
On’s first condition met on Rocky’s request, the second applied, and AMPL’s fallback accepted with the bargain test.
Compare Pao On on its facts: there the understanding existed ‘at the time of the main agreement’; here nothing shows
one on 27 February. Comma needed after ‘past consideration’.

II, paragraph 8 (the 12 months). Excellent and original: ‘recorded governs the 4% rate as much as the sum’ is a
genuine textual argument, and the lean (‘arguably’ 8 April) has a reason and the further information has its direction. Say
what the post-contractual conduct is (the parties’ 7 March positions) and state the objective test in a word.

II, paragraph 9 (the condition). Excellent. Condition precedent named with its reason; the commercial reading put and
met on what the $10,000 priced; the duty to co-operate put and met on its own words (‘on their part’). McHugh JA is
correctly marked ‘dissenting’ in the footnote without being called a minority view of the principle.

II, concluding paragraph. Rocky’s exposure is stated precisely on each reading, as the advice requires. The second
sentence stacks two parenthetical phrases inside the subject and is hard to read; split it. On the alternative that Pierre
promised, the exposure is assembled across three paragraphs rather than stated here.

Citation. Every pin I checked is right, including Meehan 580 to 581 and 589 to 590, Godecke 642 to 643 and 646 to 647,
Brambles 177 (quoting Integrated Computer Services at 11,117 to 11,118), Port Jackson 269 and Pao On 629 to 630.
Footnotes 15 and 16 (‘Ibid 486.’; ‘Ibid 487.’) drop the judges that footnote 14 names; be consistent. Short titles for
Sundell and Banque Brussels are introduced but never used again; harmless.

Holistic mark: 87 / 100, High Distinction. This reads as a lawyer working the problem: the client’s goals first, the
deciding words weighed, both sides’ best authority used for what it decided, hedged conclusions with reasons, and an
exact statement of exposure. What keeps it out of the 90s: intention on 8 April is dealt with in three sentences when it is
contested and carries the undertaking as well as the $10,000; the Masters v Cameron framework, the standard course
apparatus for a ‘subject to’ qualifier, is not engaged at all; two elements (consideration for the email; certainty of AMPL’s
promise) are skipped rather than disposed of in a sentence; and the last paragraph’s second sentence is the one place
clarity slips.



Part B. Rubric (SHEET_v12 read with RULINGS R-A to R-AE)

Rulings applied, and conflicts with SHEET_v12 disclosed

  1. R-AC(1) over SHEET R3 and §5. Headings are by party only; the sheet’s A to F headings are not required, and R3
     is graded on R-AC’s form.
  2. R-AB(2) allowance over the sheet’s cores. Minor matters that change nothing may take a clause or be omitted:
     the Development Agreement (P core (2); claim C4), the routes around privity (I.F3), the two readings of ‘the clause’
     (II.F1 second limb; claim C7) and the Eastwood and Bowral items (I.A bullets). Their omission or one-clause
     treatment is not deducted, at the unit or at R4. I applied the allowance only to the matters R-AB(2) lists; uncontested
     elements outside that list still need their ‘one clean sentence’ (R-AB(2)), so their absence is graded.
  3. R-AB(5). Floors and counted forms do not bind; the reasoning is graded first, and a core is met by visible reasoning
     in any wording.
  4. R-AD over P’s Drops. P’s VG for ‘damages argued’ is not applied: R-AD(1) requires the advice to say that Grace’s
     default breaches the oral undertaking (damages not assessed), which the answer does.
  5. R-P, R-Y(1), R-Z, R-AA(1). Stated count = body + 3 is correct; Trident by report with no pin is no fault; no meta
     language or slide citation appears anywhere; Scammell pinned 268 is correct.
  6. Marker’s judgment calls, disclosed. (a) At I.D the onus is placed on Grace because the answer analyses the email
     as the basis of her possible claim; on that framing she is the party alleging the contract, which is Ermogenous [26]‘s
     test, so I accept it rather than the sheet’s ’onus on Rocky’. (b) At II.C2 the ‘even if the guarantee came first’ limb
     replaces the further-information clause; it shows the order does not change the result, which meets the core’s
     purpose, and I grade it Excellent. (c) Where a unit misses one or two core items I grade VG; where it misses three or
     more, or half its core, Good. (d) The required-authority rule (§1: one missing is VG) is applied at I.D (Masters 360
     and 361 absent) and II.B (Masters 361 absent); at II.C1 I treat the bargain proposition as carried by Brambles 177
     and grade the missing ‘moving from’ limb VG rather than applying ‘none is Good’.

Unit grades, each with the sentence that earns or limits it

P Preliminary (2.0): Excellent. C3 named in substantive terms: ‘Nor did Grace promise to reimburse anything Rocky
pays AMPL.’ The Development Agreement is omitted under R-AB(2). No meta clause.

I.A Offer (3.0): Very Good. Earns: ‘Yet ’I will lend’ fixes the amount, date, term and rate, and ‘Please confirm
agreement’ invites assent: unlike ‘may be prepared to sell’ in Gibson, those words are promissory.’ (Gibson 298 checked.)
Missing core: the 5 February call not classified; the second-paragraph similarity, with its direction, not named. Comment:
‘Meet the paragraph of Gibson that resembles the email.’

I.B Acceptance (2.0): Excellent. ‘However, it answered a request to ’confirm agreement’; adding no terms, ‘Confirmed’
was no counter-offer and so accepted the first point, qualifier included.’ Criterion at Empirnall 534 checked.

I.C Consideration (2.0): Good. Core (1), consideration for the 12 February exchange in form, is absent; core (2)
appears only as ‘Grace’s promises in it were the price of AMPL’s own promises’, with the price unidentified, no authority
and the inference unmarked. Comment: ‘Identify the price on both sides.’

I.D Intention to create legal relations (6.0): Very Good. Earns: ‘absent a stipulated formal contract, the question is
whether terms were to be ’put into form’ or ‘subject to a new agreement’’ (Masters 363 quoting Winn v Bull, checked),
with the status factor, the onus, both best cases (Grace’s with Godecke 642 to 643, checked) and ‘unless the conditions
were settled ’this morning’‘. Missing: required authorities Masters 360 (’only a record of terms’) and 361 (Niesmann).
Comment: ‘Use the course’s framework for a ’subject to’ qualifier: Masters 360 and Niesmann 361.’

I.E Certainty (3.0): Excellent. ‘Yet honesty there governed a judgment on an external condition, finance, whereas
Rocky’s went to the content of his own loan, not ’only to the fulfilment of a condition’; and honesty cannot supply unstated
loan terms. His promise is thus arguably illusory.’ Gibbs J’s dictum with both hedges, ‘doubted’ and ‘leaving open only
’subsidiary matters’‘, is answered (’the terms of the loan itself are not subsidiary’) in the I.D paragraph, which the decide-
once rule permits. Meehan 581 and 589 to 590 and Godecke 646 to 647 checked.

I.F1 Privity: agency (2.5): Excellent. ‘His 27 February request suggests so, a principal being party to his agent’s
contracts; yet AMPL advanced $1,150,000, not $1 million, and took his guarantee, so it did not contract for Rocky as well
as for itself.’ Trident (Brennan J dissenting) and Port Jackson 269 checked.

I.F2 Privity: the Loan Agreement (7.5): Excellent. Rule, both limbs (Coulls 494 to 495); ‘Party status turns on ’the
written instrument itself’‘; joint promisee met; ’Grace’s ’I never signed anything with you’ is right in result but not in
reason: Mrs Coulls signed yet was no party.’; the cross-reference to the 12 months (‘arguably premised on a 27 February
start, therefore fails whenever the 12 months began’). Coulls 486, 487 checked.

I.F3 Circumventing privity (1.0): Excellent under R-AB(2). Omitted; agency is decided at I.F1 and no trust or
estoppel fact exists, so it changes nothing.

II.A Offer (2.5): Excellent. ‘What Pierre ’expected’ promised nothing, so it was likely an invitation to treat; Rocky’s ‘I
will do that if you also promise’ offered a promise for a promise, a bilateral contract’. Brambles 177 checked.

II.B Acceptance (3.0): Very Good. Earns: ‘AMPL may say nothing bound until signing: parties are bound ’when, and in
the manner in which, they intend’; yet nothing suggests Rocky was to sign anything. A contract likely formed orally.’
Missing: Lord Greene MR’s analogy disclosed in the text; Masters 361 (no right to withdraw reserved).

II.C1 Consideration: the price (1.5): Very Good. The exchange is carried by ‘a promise for a promise’ (II.A) and
‘priced by the $10,000 promise’ (II.I1); the ‘moving from the promisee’ limb is never stated.

II.C2 Existing legal duty rule (5.5): Excellent. ‘However, the rule’s settled application is to a duty under ’a pre-
existing contract’ with the promisee, and none is shown: Rocky only ‘asked’ on 27 February; and ‘Pierre agreed and the
clause was included’ suggests the clause followed the bargain. Even if the guarantee came first, ensuring that ‘all
payments’ were made ‘promptly’ exceeds guaranteeing ‘repayment of the Loan’; and a promise to Pierre to perform a
duty owed to AMPL, a third party, can be consideration.’ Wigan 594, Sundell 327 and Pao On 632 checked.
II.D Intention to create legal relations (5.0): Good. Earns the onus and the objective features: ‘A promise of a fixed
sum, made at AMPL’s office as its loan was finalised, is ’appropriately promissory in character’ and commercial.’ (Banque
Brussels 523 checked.) Missing three core items: the letter of comfort point with its direction and reason; the Pierre
alternative re-weighed; the same finding binding Rocky’s undertaking.

II.E Certainty (2.5): Very Good. ‘The Loan Agreement fixes the payments, so ’ensure’ is not ‘so obscure’ as to defeat
certainty’ (Upper Hunter 437 checked). Missing: AMPL’s promise shown not to be illusory.

II.F1 Privity: Rocky as promisee (1.0): Very Good. The two readings of ‘the clause’ are excused under R-AB(2); the
criterion that Rocky is the promisee who gave consideration, so privity does not bar the oral claim, is not stated.

II.F2 Who promised (4.0): Excellent. ‘Whether AMPL or Pierre contracted turns on agency: Pierre bound AMPL if,
with its authority, he contracted for it rather than solely for himself. Although Rocky’s ’you’ points to Pierre, Pierre
controls AMPL and spoke at its office about its loan. AMPL therefore likely made the $10,000 promise and took Rocky’s
undertaking; if Pierre spoke for himself, the undertaking ran to Pierre, leaving AMPL only the guarantee clause.’

II.G The 12 months (5.0): Excellent. ‘Yet ’recorded’ governs the 4% rate as much as the sum, and nothing shows any
term was fixed before 8 April: the genesis shows when the money moved, not that a term first agreed on 8 April began
earlier. The term therefore arguably ran from 8 April, making the call premature unless the Loan Agreement’s words date
it from the advance.’ Open point; the 8 April lean is best-reasoned (§2, R-E). Brambles 164 and 191 checked. The objective
limb of the method is not stated; noted, not a listed Drop.

II.H The condition (4.5): Excellent. ‘Rocky may say that, read commercially, his own payment suffices; but the words
name Grace, and the $10,000 priced his promise that Grace would pay, not his paying instead. Nor does AMPL’s duty to
co-operate assist: it reaches only what is necessary on AMPL’s part, and repayment is Grace’s act.’ Meehan 582, Upper
Hunter 437 and Beaton 185 checked.

II.I1 Burden aspect (4.0): Excellent. ‘Privity prevents AMPL and Grace’s clause binding him of its own force, so AMPL
must show his assent, not his signature.’ and ‘Rocky’s ’I will do that’, priced by the $10,000 promise, likely binds him at
least as an undertaking.’

II.I2 Content and assent (3.5): Excellent. ‘Having ’attended’ with a reasonable opportunity to reject the clause, he
arguably knew its words; although the $10,000 priced his own words, not the clause’s, taking that promise knowingly and
with no objection shown arguably signalled assent to the clause. If he never saw it, he is bound only by his undertaking.’
Empirnall 535 to 536 checked.

II.I3 Consideration from AMPL (4.0): Very Good. Earns the conditional structure, past consideration, the first
condition and the fallback accepted: ‘AMPL’s stronger answer is that it signed the Loan Agreement only after the clause
was included, an act likely ’really done in consideration of’ the guarantee’. Missing: the ‘at the time’ comparison with Pao
On’s facts.

II.I4 Timing and consequences (5.0): Excellent. ‘he is liable for $1,150,000 once the Loan is due and unpaid: now on
the 27 February reading, and on the 8 April reading from 8 April 2025 if Grace has not repaid’, with the arguable March
instalment and ‘likely never if Rocky pays instead’ (R-AD satisfied).

and fn 16 omit the judges that fn 14 names for the same joint judgment. R9 Word count and format (1.0): Excellent.

Bull* (1877) 7 Ch D 29, 32 (Jessel MR).) In the intention paragraph, replace the Godecke (Walsh J) sentence and ‘Rocky’s words

unpaginated extract); lint: body 1,496, stated ‘Word count: 1,499’ = body + 3 (R-P compliant; the lint line is the expected
R-P consequence); fn 11 report with no pinpoint (Trident, accepted under R-Y). Voice (voice_stats_v2): mean sentence
24.0; under 12 words 14.5%; semicolons 1.28 and colons 1.08 per 100 words; 51.6% of sentences carry a semicolon or

PART A. Cold read: margin comments, holistic mark
Read first against the facts and the readings only, then against the sheet.

Opening paragraph. Good: the advice starts from what the client wants (repayment, the $10,000, protection from the
call) and the exposure he must answer (‘by 15 February’). It is framing, not a conclusion. The Development Agreement is
never mentioned; R-AB(2) allows that, but a clause naming it as a non-issue would cost eight words and close off a dot
point a real guide may carry.

I, paragraph 1 (offer). Distinguishing Gibson on its operative words (‘may be prepared to sell’ against ‘I will lend’ and
‘Please confirm agreement’) is exactly right, and Rocky’s ‘Confirming discussions’ reading is put. Two gaps. First, Grace’s
5 February call, which ‘proposed that Rocky offer’, is a planted fact and is not used at all; classify it in a clause (an
invitation to treat that Rocky declined). Second, Gibson cuts both ways: the Council’s indicative paragraph ‘might have
been understood … as indicating a firm offer’ only without its disclaimer, and ‘subject to satisfactory conditions’ is
arguably that disclaimer’s analogue. Small point of expression: ‘I will lend’ does not itself fix the amount, date, term and
rate; the first point does.

I, paragraph 2 (acceptance). Excellent. Rocky’s best argument (that ‘Confirmed’ vouched only for accuracy) is put and
met on the words it answered; the counter-offer point is named; ‘qualifier included’ is carried.

I, paragraph 3 (intention). Very strong. The onus is placed on the party alleging the contract, and the status factor is
weighed on these parties. Winn v Bull is applied in Jessel MR’s own condition (‘absent a stipulated formal contract’),
Godecke is used for what Walsh J actually decided (an objective reasonableness limit) and distinguished, and Gibbs J’s
doubt is kept with its hedge and its ‘subsidiary matters’ exception answered with a reason. Note: the onus lies on Grace
only because you frame her as the party who would allege the email; if Rocky sued on it the onus would be his, so say so
in a clause. Consideration for the 12 February exchange is nowhere stated; even an uncontested element needs one
sentence, and here it matters, because illusoriness is a consideration point.

I, paragraph 4 (certainty). Excellent. Meehan is named as Grace’s best authority, its similarity stated, and it is
distinguished on Gibbs CJ’s own limit (‘only to the fulfilment of a condition’), not on its result. The honesty fetter is put
and met (‘honesty cannot supply unstated loan terms’), and the verdict is properly ‘arguably’. The further information has
its direction (‘unless the conditions were settled ’this morning’’).

I, paragraph 5 (agency). Excellent. Rocky’s best fact (‘asked’) is conceded and met on the purporting criterion with two
facts that go to it (the different sum; the guarantee taken). I checked Port Jackson 269 in the official text: Mason and
Jacobs JJ quote Lord Reid’s requisites at that page, so the pin is right.

I, paragraph 6 (privity). Excellent. The rule is stated with both limbs, the test of who is a party is taken from Coulls 487
and applied, the joint-promisee argument is named and met, and Grace’s planted line is answered precisely (‘right in
result but not in reason’).

I, closing paragraph. Good: the 12 months is handled by a reason that makes it irrelevant here (‘whenever the 12
months began’), and claim C3 is named in substance (reimbursement for paying AMPL).

II, paragraph 1 (offer and acceptance). Good classification of ‘expected’ and of the bilateral offer. Lord Greene MR’s
words were said of exchanges of contracts for land; disclose that in the text rather than stating them as a general rule.
Your meet (‘nothing suggests Rocky was to sign anything’) answers Rocky’s signature, not AMPL’s argument that AMPL
itself was not bound until it signed; the stronger answer is that nothing made the bargain subject to the document or
reserved a right to withdraw.

II, paragraph 2 (who promised). Correct test with both elements and the alternative pleaded, with its consequence.
Brisk for the weight it carries: say that ‘owned and controlled’ supplies the authority and that the office and the loan go to
whom Pierre purported to act for.

II, paragraph 3 (existing legal duty rule). Excellent. The rule is named and confined to its premises, Sundell is met at
full strength with its holding, and Rocky’s answers are each tied to a fact (‘asked’; the order of ‘Pierre agreed and the
clause was included’; ‘all payments … promptly’ against ‘repayment of the Loan’), with Pao On 632 for the Pierre
alternative.

II, paragraph 4 (intention). Too thin for a contested element. ‘Ensure’ is the language of the letter of comfort in
Banque Brussels; put the analogy against intention, say why it is weaker here (there the facility was conditioned on the
letter; here AMPL had already advanced), and say what carries the finding if Pierre, the cousin himself, was the promisor
(the fixed price). You use Banque Brussels only for its test.

II, paragraph 5 (certainty). Good on ‘so obscure’, and the result-or-effort point is a perceptive addition that later drives
the exposure advice. AMPL’s own promise is not tested for illusoriness (its condition depends on Grace, not on AMPL).

II, paragraph 6 (the guarantee). Very good. The burden aspect is stated with Trident, signature is put aside, Empirnall
is applied element by element (reasonable opportunity; benefit named; knowledge on ‘attended’, marked ‘arguably’),
Rocky’s counter (the $10,000 priced his words, not the clause’s) is put and met, and the further information has its
direction.

II, paragraph 7 (AMPL’s consideration). Good and conditional on who promised. Pao On’s first condition is met on
Rocky’s request and the fallback (AMPL’s signing) is accepted with the bargain test applied. The second condition needs
its comparison: in Pao On the parties understood ‘at the time of the main agreement’; here nothing shows that on 27
February. ‘The advance, past consideration though made at Rocky’s request,’ reads as note form.
II, paragraph 8 (12 months). Excellent textual analysis: ‘recorded’ governs the 4% rate as much as the sum, so the
genesis shows when money moved, not when the term began. Method, both readings, an honest lean and the further
information with its direction.

II, paragraph 9 (condition). Excellent. Condition precedent to the obligation with its reason, the commercial reading
put and met by the pricing point, and the duty to co-operate met on its own words (‘on their part’).

II, closing paragraph. Precise exposure on each reading and each basis, exactly what the client needs (R-AD), with
every consequence tied to its legal source. The last sentence’s nested clause (‘so never if Grace, who said he ’might not
pay at all’, does not,’) is hard to read at speed.

Overall comments. This reads as a lawyer working the problem, not a checklist: authorities are used for what they
decided and distinguished on their facts, both sides’ best arguments are put, and the advice ends in the client’s exposure.
Some paragraph openers are formulaic (‘The first issue’, ‘The next issue’, ‘The last issue’), but not distractingly so. The
weaknesses are omissions of planted material rather than errors.

Holistic mark: 88 (High Distinction). What keeps it below 90+: the 5 February call and consideration for the 12
February exchange are unaddressed; the letter-of-comfort analogy that ‘ensure’ plants is not engaged; Lord Greene MR’s
dictum is lifted out of its context; two or three Part II paragraphs (intention, certainty, who promised) are thin for the
weight they carry; and a few sentences compress reasoning into appositives.



PART B. Rubric (SHEET_v12 with RULINGS to R-AE)

Rulings applied and disclosed

    1. R-AC overrides SHEET R3 and §5 (‘A. Offer’ to ‘F. Privity’ headings): party headings only, elements in chronological
       order, full paragraphs. Graded Excellent on that standard.
    2. R-AB(2): the Development Agreement, the Eastwood and Bowral bullets, the routes around privity and the two
       readings of ‘the clause’ may take a clause or be omitted where they change nothing. Applied at P (Development
       Agreement omitted), I.A (the bullets disposed of in a clause without their deciding words), I.F3 (only agency run) and
       II.F1 (one reading of ‘the clause’). Not applied to the 5 February call, which R-AB(2) does not list.
    3. R-AB(5): floors and compression forms do not bind; reasoning is graded first. R-AB(1) is applied to the thin II.D
       paragraph.
    4. R-Y: Trident cited by report with the judge named and no pin; the lint is accepted.
    5. R-P: stated count 1,499 = body 1,496 + 3.
    6. R-E with S-1: II.G’s lean to 8 April is an Excellent conclusion on an open point if best reasoned, and it is.
    7. Judgment calls of this marker. (a) I.D core (1) prescribes ‘the onus on Rocky’; the answer puts it on Grace as the
       party who would allege the email. Ermogenous [26] places it on the party alleging the contract, so this is the rule
       correctly applied to the answer’s framing; no drop. (b) I.D’s bracket names Masters 360 and 361 (Niesmann), which
       presuppose Rocky as the party alleging the contract. The answer puts the ‘bound’ case through Godecke 642 to 643
       (Walsh J), a closer course authority, and no I.D Drop line triggers; Excellent. (c) Port Jackson 269 (Mason and Jacobs
       JJ, quoting Lord Reid) in place of 240 (Barwick CJ): verified at p269 in the official text; same requisites; the
       proposition is course-stated, so it is outside the R-H cap. (d) II.H’s meet of the duty to co-operate (‘only what is
       necessary on AMPL’s part, and repayment is Grace’s act’) differs from the counted form but meets the duty on
       Mason J’s own words; Excellent.

Pins verified at the page (beyond verify.py PASS)

Meehan 580 to 581 (Gibbs CJ: ‘entirely for the protection of the purchaser’, supporting fn 6); 581 (honesty); 589 to 590
(Mason J’s sentence, which Mason J sources to ‘Thorby v Goldberg (at 605)’, so the ‘, quoting’ form in fn 9 is right; the
casebook names no judge for Thorby, so none is required); Brambles 176 (Lord Greene MR via Heydon JA) and 177
(Integrated at 11,117 to 11,118); Port Jackson 269 (official text, paragraph 3 of Mason and Jacobs JJ, ‘(at p269)’); Trident
(Brennan J, the agency passage, in the course reading; Brennan J dissented on the result, as fn 11 discloses); Pao On
extract (‘at the time of the main agreement’).

6. CERTAIN ERRORS

None. Every quotation is verbatim at the cited page and speaker; every pin was confirmed on the casebook page markers
and, where available, the official ‘(at pNNN)’ markers; every bench label (majority, dissenting, first instance) matches the
source; every fact matches question.txt.

Specific questions put to the tracer, answered at source: - Godecke 646–7: ‘further terms to be settled by one of the
parties’ is at 646 and ‘subsidiary matters’ at 647 (official para 6 begins on 646 and ends ‘at p647’). ‘doubted … leaving
open only’ is fair: Gibbs J says it ‘does not necessarily follow’ that such an agreement ‘would be treated as a concluded
contract’, holds a price left to the buyer would give ‘no binding contract’, and offers one possible exception, ‘Perhaps it
may be different where agreement has been reached on all essential terms but the determination of subsidiary matters
has been left to one of the parties’; his Sweet & Maxwell discussion narrows that exception further (an imported objective
standard), which the answer meets separately (‘Rocky’s words contain no such standard’). The passage is obiter
(‘unnecessary to express any concluded opinion’); ‘doubted’ conveys that. - Wigan 594: ‘the rule’s settled application is to
a duty under “a pre-existing contract” with the promisee’ fairly states Mason J’s ‘at least when’: it identifies where the
rule certainly applies and does not assert the rule is confined there. - Pao On 629–30: ‘past consideration though made at
Rocky’s request’ is accurate; the request limb is met (Rocky asked AMPL on 27 February) and the sentence turns on the
second limb. - Meehan 580–1, 581, 589–90, 582; Coulls 487, 494–5, 495; Ermogenous [25]–[26]; AWM 456 and 456–7;
Empirnall 534, 535–6; Brambles 164, 176, 177, 191; Upper Hunter 437; Beaton 185; Port Jackson 269; Trident (Brennan J
dissenting; Deane J): all verified as tabled above.

Fact verdict. Every fact that decides a result is used, and used correctly. The only facts left unused that carry a live
argument are the passing of 15 February with no loan and no complaint, together with Rocky’s own 13 February 2025
description of ‘the Loan – the $1.15m’ (both post-formation conduct, admissible on whether the 12 February email formed
a contract: Brambles 164), and the interest-payment pattern on the 12 months, which the answer excludes by rule without
naming it.

Pattern flags. (1) Opening formula: ‘The first issue is whether’, ‘On acceptance’, ‘On certainty’, ‘On consideration’, ‘The
next issue’, ‘The last issue’. This is the structure R-AC requires and reads naturally; not a fault. (2) Verdict tails of four to
six words close ¶5, ¶6, ¶9, ¶11 and ¶12 (‘Rocky likely gave consideration.’). Each is earned by the reasoning before it, but
five in a row is a visible pattern; acceptable, flagged only. (3) No sentence is note-form, no quotation exceeds 20 words
(the longest is 11), quoted words are about 14% of the body, no em dashes, no meta language.


4. OVERALL

Authenticity score: 8.5 / 10

It reads as a lawyer working the problem: client goals first; facts taken in order; the deciding words weighed one by one
(‘Confirming discussions’, ‘Please confirm agreement’, ‘satisfactory’, ‘conditions’, ‘also’, ‘you’, ‘recorded’, ‘once Grace
repays’); authorities used for what they decided and distinguished on their facts (Meehan’s finance condition against the
content of Rocky’s own loan; Sundell’s identical promise against ‘all payments … promptly’; Mrs Coulls’s signature
against Grace’s ‘I never signed’); conclusions hedged and consequences stated through their legal source. It is not box-
ticking and not name-dropping.

What would make a senior academic doubt the reasoning

  1. ¶14 draws ‘likely’ from premises that support only ‘arguably’ (its own words: ‘arguably knew’; ‘the $10,000 priced
     his own words, not the clause’s’).
  2. ¶16 replies to AMPL negatively; it shows the genesis does not prove an earlier start but gives no positive reason why
     a term agreed on 8 April runs from 8 April.
  3. ¶8 states Grace’s lack of claim as following ‘therefore’ from privity.
  4. Brambles 164 is cited only for the limb that cuts against using later conduct; its formation limb, which helps Rocky
     on the 12 February email, is unused.
  5. ¶4 treats Gibbs J’s obiter as the counterweight to Godecke without signalling that the Walsh J majority went the
     other way in a passage the casebook omits (residual risk; the marker reads the casebook, so no change is required).
  6. ¶14’s Empirnall analogy (an offeree taking services) is extended to a guarantor’s assent to a clause in other parties’
     contract; the answer partly concedes the gap, which is why the hedge must come down.

Results

I agree with every result. I disagree only with the strength in ¶14 (assent to the written clause: ‘arguably’, not ‘likely’). On
the 12 months, the answer leans to 8 April where ISSUES_SETTLED leans weakly to 27 February; the point is open, the
answer hedges correctly and states the consequence on both readings, so I accept its lean.

Remaining improvements (exact wording, cost, funding)

Current body 1,493; ceiling 1,497 (R-P).

MUST

M1. ¶14, 0 words. Replace ‘taking it with that knowledge and without objection likely signalled assent to the clause.’ with
‘taking it with that knowledge and without objection arguably signalled assent to the clause.’ Reason: the conclusion
cannot exceed its premise; ¶18 is already conditional (‘If Rocky assented’), so nothing else changes.

SHOULD

S1. ¶5 and ¶8, +2 words. In ¶5 replace ‘The email likely bound neither party, unless the conditions were settled “this
morning”.’ with ‘The email likely bound neither party, so Grace has no claim that Rocky failed to lend, unless the
conditions were settled “this morning”.’ and in ¶8 replace ‘therefore fails whenever the 12 months began, and Grace
likely has no claim against him.’ with ‘therefore fails whenever the 12 months began.’ Reason: puts Grace’s counterclaim
where its reason is.

S2. ¶16, 0 words. Replace ‘Yet “recorded” governs the 4% rate as much as the sum, and nothing shows any term was
fixed before 8 April: the genesis shows when the money moved, not that a term first agreed on 8 April began earlier.’ with
‘Yet “recorded” governs the 4% rate as much as the sum, and a period fixed by an agreement made on 8 April, naming no
start, more naturally runs from that agreement than from an advance made on unstated terms.’ Reason: meets AMPL’s
real argument with a positive reason; the following sentence (‘The term therefore arguably ran from 8 April … unless the
Loan Agreement’s words date it from the advance’) stands unchanged.

S3. ¶18, 0 words (or −2 with the funding cut F2 below). Replace ‘Rocky’s $10,000 claim, against AMPL or, if Pierre
promised, Pierre, likely stands but, being conditional,[^40] falls due only once Grace repays,’ with ‘Rocky’s $10,000 claim
against AMPL, or Pierre if he promised, likely stands; being conditional,[^40] it falls due only once Grace repays,’ Reason:
removes the nested commas; the rest of the sentence is unchanged.

S4. ¶5, +10 words plus one footnote. After S1, replace ‘The email likely bound neither party, so’ with ‘The email likely
bound neither party, as 15 February passing without a loan or complaint confirms,[^n] so’ with [^n] = Brambles Holdings
Ltd v Bathurst City Council (2001) 53 NSWLR 153, 164 (Heydon JA) (‘Brambles’). Because this becomes the first
Brambles citation, the short title moves here and the later Brambles footnotes (currently fn 17, 19, 35 and 36, which
become fn 18, 20, 36 and 37) cite ‘(n 11)’; every footnote after the new fn 11 moves up by one, so the cross-references ‘(n
11)’, ‘(n 12)’, ‘(n 13)’, ‘(n 23)’, ‘(n 26)’ and ‘(n 30)’ each rise by one, and ‘(n 17)’ becomes ‘(n 11)’; ‘(n 1)’ to ‘(n 6)’ are
unaffected. Reason: Rocky’s best corroboration on formation, from a rule the answer already cites.

subject to its qualifier.’ becomes ‘The first point is likely an offer.’ (−4). ¶3’s ‘qualifier included’ and ¶4’s opening carry
the qualifier forward. - F2. ¶18, combined with S3: ‘Rocky’s $10,000 claim against AMPL, or Pierre if he promised, likely
stands but falls due only once Grace repays,’ (−2 against the original; fn 40 is deleted, as ¶17 already names the condition
precedent with Meehan 582). - F3. ¶4: ‘are most naturally unstated terms’ becomes ‘are naturally unstated terms’ (−1). -
F4. ¶15: ‘which is thus likely supported if he assented’ becomes ‘which is likely supported if he assented’ (−1).

Addendum
The changes from F6 to the submitted text, and which ledger recommendations they carry out, are listed in the addendum
at the end of Part 4.
6. The full rubric

6.1 Rulings that override SHEET_v12 (binding on the markers)
R-P. Word count line and judge forms. The ‘Word count: N’ line states the total of all text excluding footnotes as Word
would count it, which is the body count plus the three words of the line itself (so a body of 1,495 is stated as ‘Word count:
1,498’); the body must therefore be at most 1,497 for the stated total to be at most 1,500, and the sheet’s floors use 1,495
as the body ceiling. Pao On may be cited either as the Board (no judge) or as ‘(Lord Scarman for the Board)’, since the
course extract is headed ‘Judgement delivered by Lord Scarman’; neither is deducted. Roscorla may name Lord Denman
CJ, who delivered the judgment of the Court, on the same basis. Slides are cited as ‘Class 4A Slides, slide 16’ only where
no course case states the point; a slide citation where a course case states the point is over-citation.

R-Y. Trident as a course reading (Yusuf, 11 October 2026, 15:37: the full Trident judgment ‘is part of course readings’),
and round-2 rulings. (1) Trident General Insurance Co Ltd v McNiece Bros Pty Ltd (1988) 165 CLR 107 is a Class 4A
reading; it is saved at ‘W4 C4A Privity/Trident (course reading, supplied by Yusuf 2026-10-11).txt’ and added to the
corpus by judge. R-J and R-N are superseded: Trident may be quoted and cited as a course case, one source per marker;
because the reading carries no CLR page markers, it is cited by report with the judge named and no pin, and the no-pin
lint is an accepted, disclosed consequence. (2) For the burden aspect of privity, Deane J’s words (‘the general rule that
only the parties to a contract are bound by, and entitled to enforce, its terms’) are the preferred source over Class 4A
slide 2 (R-P: a slide only where no course case states the point). For the routes around privity, a Trident judge who states
them is preferred over slide 3 if one does so in terms; otherwise slide 3 stays. (3) The textbook is never a source, including
as a page locator: Ermogenous stays pinned by paragraph (as his LAWS1061 answer pinned Wicks) and Pao On stays at
the brief’s verified 632 and 629 to 630. (4) Round-2 marker and panel findings are triaged by the reviser at source; II.I3
takes ‘likely’ (both markers); Brambles 182 is replaced by Heydon JA at 179 or 181 where he applies Empirnall, if the
page states it, marked as his reasoning; guarantee-law statements (‘only postpones liability’) are removed (the answer’s
own scope line excludes guarantee law).

R-Z. No meta language and no slide citations (Yusuf, 11 October 2026, 16:08 and 16:10: ‘you cant cite a slide like this is
an actual advise right? so you cant use meta language of weeks 1 to 4 explicitly in the … answer’; ‘having the words
slides/ weeks 1 to 4 and shi that is meta and outside is js a big no’). Binding on every drafter, reviser and marker,
overriding every earlier ruling and sheet line to the contrary: (1) the answer is advice to Rocky; its body and footnotes
never mention weeks, classes, lectures, slides, the course, the readings, the syllabus, the rubric or the assignment, and
never frame anything as ‘outside the scope’; the preliminary paragraph, if kept, states only what the advice covers in
substantive terms or is omitted. (2) Slides are never cited. Every proposition is cited to a case; R-A(3) and R-P’s slide limb
are withdrawn. (3) The casebook is a last resort, used only where no case in the readings (including Trident under R-Y)
states the proposition, and the reviser first looks for a case (eg Trident, Mason CJ and Wilson J on agency and trusts as
routes around privity; Port Jackson for agency) before keeping a casebook footnote. (4) Restrictions that came from the
task (only offer, acceptance, consideration, intention, certainty and privity; NSW) still govern what the answer covers; the
answer simply does not say so. (5) Markers grade a meta reference or a slide citation as an R1 and R8 fault (VG for one
instance, Good for two or more), and P’s core no longer requires naming excluded matters.

R-AB. The reasoning standard (Yusuf, 11 October 2026, 19:19: ‘this should be an advise like my laws1061 mid term …
going through it critically reasoning … be explicit i feel like you skip over a lot … be clean’; 19:21 approving
REBUILD/CALIBRATION_SAMPLE.md and ‘run till completion’). Binding on every later agent and overriding SHEET_v12
wherever they conflict: (1) The answer is rebuilt from the facts in chronological order at the depth of the calibration
sample. Every contested point shows the facts that decide it, both readings of the deciding words, the rule in the source’s
words, why one reading wins, and a hedged conclusion; an asserted step is a fault (‘Further analysis is required as your
statement is conclusive’). (2) Prioritisation (Instructions: ‘how well issues are prioritised’; ‘answering the question will not
necessarily require you to address every topic’): depth goes to the points that change Rocky’s position (whether the 12
February email bound and the ‘Confirmed’ point; whether Rocky can sue Grace at all, AMPL having lent; whether the
$10,000 promise is enforceable and against whom; whether Rocky is bound to AMPL on the guarantee and whether the
call was premature; ‘once Grace repays the Loan’); uncontested elements take one clean sentence; minor matters
(Eastwood, Bowral, the Development Agreement, the routes around privity, the two readings of ‘the clause’) take a clause
or are omitted if they change nothing. (3) One authority per proposition; quote only the deciding words; no note-form
clauses; no parenthetical cross-references beyond one or two in the whole answer; an overall bottom line for Rocky at the
end. (4) Structure kept: Parts by claimant v defendant (I. Rocky v Grace; II. Rocky v AMPL, alternatively Pierre), headings
A. Offer to F. Privity, then further headings in course vocabulary only where needed. R-Z (no meta, no slides) and every
source-fidelity rule still bind. (5) SHEET_v12’s cores still state what must be decided, but a core is met only by visible
reasoning, and its floors and compression forms no longer bind; markers grade the reasoning first. (6) New marking
order: (a) two cold-lecturer markers who first read only the facts, the readings and the answer and write margin
comments in his real marker’s style, then score on SHEET_v12 read with R-AB; (b) a reasoning auditor who checks that
every conclusion has both readings and a reason; (c) tracer, voice auditor and opposing counsel as before. Stop when both
cold markers give 100 with no ‘conclusive’ comment, the reasoning auditor passes, the tracer finds no certain error, voice
and terminology pass, and opposing counsel finds no material unanswered argument under R-AA(2).

R-AC. Presentation and flow (Yusuf, 11 October 2026, 22:35, attaching a sample Distinction response as a STYLE
reference only: ‘Employing textual analysis for example … are we being too choppy … skipping to conclusions,
comparatively not being as critical reasoning explicitly … no we dont have a title for each stage although it should be each
element chronologically like this … times new roman and just like each person thing … so it looks like a proper
submission not just 1 sentence paragraphs’). Binding, overriding R-AB(4), R-X(6) and the brief’s A to F heading rule: (1)
Headings are by parties only (eg ‘I. ROCKY v GRACE’; ‘II. ROCKY v AMPL (ALTERNATIVELY PIERRE)’; a further party-
pair heading only if needed); no element headings (no ‘A. Offer’ etc.). (2) Under each party heading, the elements are
taken in chronological and course order, each in its own full paragraph that opens by naming the issue in course terms
(eg ‘The first issue is whether Rocky’s 12 February email was an offer.’), states the rule with authority, then works
through the facts explicitly (‘Employing textual analysis’, the words weighed one by one, both readings, why one wins),
and ends with a hedged conclusion; each Part ends with a short concluding paragraph for that party pair. (3) No one-
sentence paragraphs; no choppy runs of short sentences; transitions between paragraphs make the reasoning flow. (4)
Times New Roman 12 pt, double spaced (the builder already does this). (5) The sample is a style reference only: it is
Studocu material (R-I), never a source of law, never cited, and its law is not relied on; it is saved at
REBUILD/STYLE_REFERENCE_sample_distinction.pdf/.txt. (6) The heading words freed (about 50) go to explicit
reasoning, not to new issues.

R-AC(7). Authenticity review (Yusuf, 22:48: ‘even outside of the rubric … in case issues with rubric, genuinely just try and
find any other downsides which would stray from an authentic problem solving legal reasoning flow … showing the full
and proper thinking of the mind critically with all sources always in context to truly be right’). From now on every round
includes an AUTHENTICITY CRITIC who ignores the rubric and judges the answer as a senior contracts academic and
practitioner would: does it read as a real lawyer thinking a problem through (question framed from the client’s goal, facts
read closely, the governing test identified and applied step by step, authorities used for what they actually decided in
their full context and distinguished on their facts, both sides’ best arguments, a decisive reason, a conclusion and its
practical consequence), or does it read as box-ticking, name-dropping, quote-mosaic, label-assertion, or reasoning shaped
by a checklist? Its findings bind the reviser on the same footing as markers’.

R-AD. Exposure and completeness (Yusuf, 12 October 2026, 00:21: ‘wdym waiting till april 8th costs little? and what about
the personal guarantee so does he have to come up with all of it … making sure you really actually did everything in full,
and not just a botched effort getting half things and skipping half including key shi or strong arguments’). (1) The advice
states Rocky’s exposure precisely: if bound by the written clause (‘personally guaranteed repayment of the Loan’), he is
liable for the Loan, $1,150,000, once it is due and unpaid (now, on the 27 February reading; from 8 April 2025 if Grace
has not repaid, on the 8 April reading); if bound only by his oral undertaking and it promised a result, Grace’s default
breaches it (damages not assessed, per the Instructions); Grace’s ‘I might not pay at all!’ makes default a real risk; if
Grace never repays, the $10,000 never falls due; paying early likely forfeits it and nothing gives him a contractual claim
against Grace. Resisting the premature call buys time only; the advice must not say waiting ‘costs little’. (2) A
completeness audit precedes the final version: every sentence of the problem is either used or deliberately set aside with
a reason, and every strong argument for either side is put and met.

R-AE. Every point through legal principle (Yusuf, 12 October 2026, 00:33: ‘everything is through law principles not just …
commentary and truly getting to the gist of it, although arguments … should be lawful not feelings or glibs like costs
little’). Every sentence of reasoning and advice, including the closing advice, rests on a legal rule applied to the facts: the
source of each consequence is named (the condition precedent: Meehan 582; the terms of the guarantee clause; the oral
undertaking as a promise of result; privity; consideration), never on impressions, risk talk or practical colour (‘a real risk’,
‘buys time’, ‘costs little’, ‘weak’ without the legal reason). A practical recommendation is stated only as the legal
consequence of each course (eg paying before the Loan is due is not required by any term and, the $10,000 being
conditional on Grace’s repayment, likely forfeits it), with the facts that decide it.
