What changed in principle (R-L). The standard is the best possible 1,495-word answer, not the best possible answer.
The grid is cut from 34 graded units to 23 by the disclosed merges in Part 8. Each unit’s Excellent core is defined once, as
a short list of requirements that a real paragraph in his voice, at the unit’s floor length, has been shown to satisfy (the
paragraphs are in SHEET_v10_appendix_NOT_FOR_DRAFTERS.md; their counts total 1,494 with the headings, a v10 record that from v11
no longer proves the v11 cores fit: R-X(3), §1, Part 8; their voice statistics sit inside VOICE.md’s Part ranges; the appendix
also carries the identity table, core item by core item against the sentence that carries it, so that a critic can check R-L(2)
line by line, the five counted contrary-branch paragraphs (II.C2, I.D, I.E, II.G and II.F2) with the whole-body substitution
test, and the per-unit first-demotion list). Everything the core does not list is credit: it earns nothing extra and loses marks
only if stated wrongly. The Drops at each unit trigger only on core items. Official-only passages (Masters 362 on Crossley
v Maycock; Godecke 641 to 642, Walsh J adopting Bray CJ) are credit, never core, because the marker reads against the
course materials (R-L(5)). The mandatory quoted facts are reduced to those that decide a unit’s core (Appendix B); every
other fact is credit.

Locator form. [CB4 331 | 487 | Taylor and Owen JJ | majority, ratio] = casebook file, printed casebook page, report pin,
speaker, status. Unit codes in this version: Part I units are I.A to I.F3; Part II units are II.A to II.F2, II.G (the 12 months),
II.H (the condition ‘once Grace repays the Loan’) and II.I1 to II.I4 (AMPL’s call); v3’s G, II.G1, II.G2 and II.H1 to II.H5 are
those units under their old codes. CB1A Offer; CB1B Acceptance; CB2A Consideration 1; PO the Pao On extract; CB2B
Consideration 2; CB3A Intention; CB3B Certainty; CB4 Privity; S1A to S4A the slide decks (slide numbers are the PDF
page where the deck prints none, as in 4A; a slide locator in this sheet tells the marker where the course states a point
and is never a citation the answer may use: R-Z(2)); OFF = the official judgment text under 96 Official judgments/text/ (R-H).
‘RG skip’ marks a reading-guide skip range, recorded for the register and never required or deducted in the answer (R-F).

1. Core and credit (R-B as made precise by R-L)

Every graded unit lists its Core as numbered requirements and its Credit as everything else. Excellent = every core item
present and correct, and no credit item wrongly stated. A core item missing or wrong drops the unit as the Drops say; a
credit item is graded only if stated wrongly (then by the Drops’ ‘credit wrong’ line, never above Very Good for a wrong
rule and never above Fair for a wrong status or speaker that matters). Until v10 the core was exactly what the unit’s
appendix paragraph carried, so the floor in the grid was the measured cost of the core; from v11 that is no longer so (next
paragraph).

The cores are the requirement; the floors are a feasibility proof, not a quota (R-X(3)). The 1,495-word ceiling is
binding, and every unit’s Core, as this version states it, is what Excellent requires. The floors printed in the grid and in
each unit heading are the v10 counted paragraphs: a record that the v10 cores fitted in his voice, never a word quota a
drafter must hit or may not exceed at a unit. The v11 cores cost more than the v10 floors (Part 8 states each unit’s v11
core cost at the v10 counted forms; about 1,571 words in all), so no appendix now proves feasibility: the answer itself is
the proof, and the answer reviser re-allocates words by marks at risk per word, funding first every certain error, every
UPGRADES_r1 ADOPT AS CORE item and every core this sheet adds, by (a) zero-cost rewrites; (b) cutting duplication,
cross-references, restatements and credit text; (c) compressing units whose result is not in doubt (Rocky lent nothing on
12 February, I.F1; the preliminary paragraph, P; I.F3; II.E; II.F1), never dropping any unit’s core. A core item still
unfunded is recorded in the map as a residual risk and graded by the unit’s Drops (or Part 8’s first-demotion list); it is
never dropped silently, and this sheet never relaxes a descriptor to make the words fit. A marker grades each unit on its
Core whatever its length: a unit longer than its v10 floor is not over-length, and one shorter is not short, unless R5’s
prioritisation or R1’s conciseness is engaged. References in the descriptors and Part 9 to ‘the slack’, ‘not funded at slack
1’, ‘the counted form’ or ‘credit, written where words allow’ record the v10 word budget and the v10 counted model; from
v11 the counted forms are examples of wording that meets the core, never the only accepted wording, and the answer
reviser may fund any credit item under R-X(3) once the cores are funded.

Four accepted depths (R-B), three of them the tiers in the grid and the fourth a cross-reference (the floor figures in this
paragraph are the v10 records; Part 8 gives the v11 core costs). K (core full IRAC; three units; floors 95 to 126, being
II.F2 95, I.D 122 and II.C2 126: CRIT_round8 m1): the issue in course terms; the governing rule as the source states it
with authority, pin and speaker; the deciding facts quoted; the opposing side’s best point put and met; the decisive
reason; a conclusion in the tier for the strength with its consequence; further information where it changes the advice. C
(compact; eleven units; floors 56 to 89, II.I1 56 the lowest and II.H 89 the highest: CRIT_round8 m1): the issue; the rule’s
operative words with authority; the deciding fact; the opposing point in a clause; the decisive reason; the conclusion with
strength. L (one-line disposition; nine units; floors 20 to 48, I.F3 20 the lowest and I.C 48 the highest, as counted; I.C,
II.C1 and II.E are two-sentence L units, at 48, 26 and 35: CRIT_round6 m8): one or two sentences in which the quoted
deciding fact and the operative words of the rule precede the result, joined by ‘so’, ‘therefore’ or a colon, with the
authority footnoted; it is NOT a conclusive statement and is NOT caught by D5. A bare verdict, or a verdict followed by its
reasons in a later sentence, remains conclusion-first (D5). A colon-joined claim-then-proof sentence is his form (VOICE.md
1.3: ‘Colon = claim, then its proof’) ONLY where the claim before the colon is a classification or a fact claim that the
words after the colon prove (‘The words are arguably ambiguous: the instrument ’recorded’ the sum ‘advanced’ on 27
February …‘;’Meehan is distinguishable: …‘; ’Purporting is contested: …’); a unit’s verdict stated before its proof (‘Rocky
lent nothing: AMPL was his agent only if …’) is conclusion-first at any level (brief R6; VOICE.md section 4 item 10,
‘Verdict first, proof after a colon’, a feature not to copy) and is never licensed, whatever follows the colon (R-R(2);
CRIT_round4 M8, adopted; v4’s wider carve-out is withdrawn and I.F1’s counted sentence is rewritten in the L order).
The fourth depth: a cross-reference to a doctrine decided in another unit or Part, graded on the clause that applies the
earlier finding to the unit’s own question (§4), as at II.I4 (‘turns on the 12 months (G)’) and I.F2 (‘(II.G)’).

‘The operative words of the rule’ means the criterion the governing authority itself applies (its test, not its label): ‘an
external manifestation of assent’ (Empirnall 534), not ‘an acceptance’; ‘purporting to act on behalf of the principal and not
solely on the agent’s own behalf’ ([11.70]; in a case’s words, the agent ‘contracted for the [principal’s] protection as well
as for his own’, Lord Reid’s requisite (2) as Barwick CJ states it at Port Jackson 240, the case form R-Z(3) prefers), not ‘an
agent’; ‘so obscure and so incapable of any definite or precise meaning’ (Upper Hunter 437), not ‘certain enough’; ‘the
fulfilment of a condition upon which the contract depends’ (Meehan 581; the words sit before the [582] marker), not ‘a
condition’. A line that names a category (‘an unqualified acceptance’; ‘objectively intended to bind’; ‘capable of meaning’;
‘the promisee’) without the criterion is conclusive, however short, and is graded Good (CRIT_harsh_2 F2, adopted);
‘communicated assent’ or ‘manifested assent’ states the Empirnall 534 criterion in substance and passes (CRIT_round3
m8, adopted).

‘Conclusive’ (the marker’s own usage). A statement is conclusive if it (a) asserts a result without reasons, or (b)
quotes facts and asserts that they meet a test without applying the criterion the governing authority itself uses, or without
naming the fact that satisfies each element of that criterion. Calibration: his LAWS1061 marker graded ‘Jia is partially
paralysed, in ’ongoing pain’, and ‘can no longer work in the tech industry’: lasting impairment and lost livelihood apt to
produce MDD, an RPI, in a person of NF’ Good, with ‘Further analysis is required as your statement is conclusive’,
because Tame and Lederer were not applied. A sentence of that shape is Good here, however fluent. A sentence of three
or more semicolon-joined clauses each of which is a conclusion without its reason is conclusive (the unit capped at Good)
and an R1 lapse.

Verdict tiers (core item at every K and C unit; the L result follows from its line). Unhedged, ‘therefore’ after the
subject, where strong or uncontested; ‘likely’ before the verb where moderate to strong; ‘On balance, the better view is
that … likely …, [reason], though …’ where moderate, weak or open. ‘Likely’ on a strong point is understatement (VG);
‘clearly’ or an unhedged verdict on a moderate or open point is overstatement (VG). ‘Likely’ alone on a moderate, weak or
open point is a tier fault (VG) only where the sentence also omits the decisive reason; with the reason in the sentence or
the one before it, it is accepted (CRIT_harsh_2 F13, adopted; the C and L forms carry strength by the single word ‘likely’).
Two limits on ‘the one before it’ (v11): that sentence counts only if it states a reason FOR THE LEAN, so a preceding
sentence that states only the opposing point, or only that a category of evidence is admissible, supplies no reason, and a
lean whose only preceding sentence is the counter has no reason (VG; UPGRADES_r1 U24, the deciding passage being
Brambles 191, which makes ‘the genesis of the transaction’ admissible and says nothing of which date it favours); and a
reason given in another unit counts where the sentence cross-refers to that unit in terms (a parenthetical or ‘as at …’), the
fourth depth applied to a reason (R-V(1); CRIT_round10 m2: II.I4’s ‘likely exposure’ on the Pierre alternative takes its
reason from II.I3 by cross-reference).

Required authorities. The authorities in brackets on each grid line, without ‘eg’, are required for Excellent: one missing
is VG; none is Good. Authorities after ‘eg’ are alternatives, one of which suffices. Credit authorities are named in the
descriptors and are never required.

No meta language; no slide citations; a case for every proposition (R-Z; v12). The answer is advice to Rocky. Its
body and footnotes never mention weeks, classes, lectures, slides, the course, the readings, the syllabus, the rubric or the
assignment, and never frame anything as ‘outside the scope’ (or ‘outside Weeks 1 to 4’, or ‘not covered by this advice’ in
course terms); the restrictions that came from the task still govern what the answer covers, and the answer simply does
not say so (R-Z(1), (4)). No slide is ever cited (R-Z(2); R-A(3) and R-P’s slide limb are withdrawn): wherever this sheet’s
Rules at source, Credit or R7’s term list names a slide (S1A to S4A), that is the marker’s locator only, and the answer
states the point in its own words with a case or omits it. Every proposition is cited to a case, Trident included (R-Y(1)); the
casebook is a last resort, used only where no case in the readings states the proposition (R-Z(3)). Grading (R-Z(5)): a meta
reference or a slide citation is an R1 and an R8 fault, each row capped at Very Good for one instance and at Good for two
or more; it is graded at those rows and not again at the unit (one error, one deduction: Part 7). Every earlier line of this
sheet to the contrary is superseded, whether or not v12 edits it.

Decide-once rules. (i) The meaning of ‘subject to satisfactory conditions’ is decided once, at I.D (the reading of
‘conditions’ and whether the parties intended to be bound), with I.E compact on its one decisive authority applied to that
reading. (ii) The 12 months is decided once, at II.G, and carried by cross-reference at II.H and II.I4. (iii) The $10,000
bargain and AMPL’s call are one bargain whose elements are run once under II.A to II.F; I (AMPL’s call) is confined to the
burden aspect, the content of what Rocky promised and his assent to the written words, AMPL’s consideration, timing and
consequences; intention and certainty of the undertaking are carried by the clause at II.D (‘Rocky being bound likewise’;
the onus flip to AMPL is credit). (iv) The objective test of intention is stated once (I.D) and cross-referred. (v) Each aspect
of the general rule in relation to privity is stated once where it bites: the benefit aspect and the two limbs at I.F2; the
burden aspect at II.I1. (vi) The consistency traps a position engages are stated once where they bite (Appendix A). (vii)
The minor claims (the Development Agreement; the Eastwood and Bowral items; any reimbursement by Grace; the
$10,000 as a clause of the Loan Agreement) are satisfied in a clause each. (viii) The condition ‘once Grace repays the
Loan’ is one unit (II.H): whether it is a condition of AMPL’s obligation and nothing is yet payable, and whether Rocky’s
own payment meets it.

2. Tilt rule and strength caps (R-E; symmetric)

Each unit states the settled strength of the better conclusion. Strong (authority decides): a contrary conclusion is Poor
and, where listed in D6, carries that deduction. Moderate to strong: a contrary conclusion is capped at Very Good.
Moderate, weak or open: either conclusion can be Excellent if best-reasoned, and the unit says what best-reasoned
requires on each side; required items and downstream consequences are stated conditionally on whichever conclusion the
answer reaches. Overstating strength (an open or moderate point called clear or stated without a lean) and understating
it (‘finely balanced’ where authority decides) carry the same cap, Very Good; where the misstatement changes the advice
to the client (eg ‘the call was clearly premature, so do nothing’; ‘the call was clearly valid, so pay now’), Good. The rule
runs both ways: a tilt toward Rocky beyond the sources (eg ‘Rocky can enforce the Loan Agreement’; ‘the $10,000 is
payable now’; ‘the call was premature’ as clear; ‘Rocky can pay AMPL and still recover the $10,000’ as clear) and a tilt
against him beyond the sources (eg ‘Rocky is bound as guarantor of the principal’ as clear while II.I2 is open; ‘the call was
not premature, so pay now’) are graded alike. Correct advice that is bad news for Rocky is rewarded exactly as good news
is. At any moderate or open unit (I.A, I.D, I.E, II.B, II.F1, II.F2, II.G, II.I2, the fallback limb of II.I3 and the timing limb
of II.I4), a paragraph with no lean and no method is Good; with the method applied and no lean, Very Good; this rule is
general and not confined to II.G (CRIT_round3 m15, adopted). Three disclosed departures from ISSUES_SETTLED’s
ratings, each with its reason stated at the unit as R-R(9) requires (CRIT_round10 m6: v10 printed ‘Two’ and omitted the
I.D departure): at I.D an answer that deploys Crossley 362 and concludes ‘bound’ can reach Excellent, although S-4 rates
the point moderate to strong where Crossley is deployed, because the Court’s own hedges at 362 (‘no special form of
words is essential’; ‘Nor is any formula … so intractable’) and the absence of any contemplated formal contract are
reasons the passage itself admits (I.D Strength; Part 9 item 5); at II.F1 either reading of ‘the clause’ is Excellent if both
are run and the result is shown unchanged, although S-10 rates the guarantee reading ‘moderate to strong’, because the
ambiguity is textual and the legal result is the same on both readings (Part 9, item 17; CRIT_round3 m14, adopted); at
II.D the AMPL alternative is ‘moderate to strong’, not S-33’s ‘strong’, because Ermogenous [25] and [26] make the point
an objective weighing with the onus on Rocky and no High Court authority in the materials decides a family-and-company
weighing on these facts (II.D Strength; Part 9, item 6; CRIT_round4 m12).

3. Narrator statements (R-D as clarified by R-M; one rule, applied identically everywhere)

A narrator’s description in the problem is a fact to be taken as proven under the Instructions (‘You may assume the facts
provided in the scenario to be proven’), in its exact words and no further; what it implies beyond its words is argued as
‘arguably’, and the inference is drawn for each side. The three phrases, in R-M’s words, applied the same way whichever
party they help: - ‘the clause was included in the Loan Agreement before it was signed by Grace and AMPL’ proves
inclusion before signing and no more; its order relative to ‘Pierre agreed’ and which clause it was are ‘arguably’ each way
(II.C2, II.F1). - ‘the personal guarantee provided by Rocky’ proves that a personal guarantee was provided by Rocky and
that AMPL called under it, and no more; what ‘provided’ consisted of (an oral undertaking, assent to the written words, or
both) is ‘arguably’ each way (II.I1, II.I2). - ‘appropriate monthly repayments of the interest’ between 8 April 2024 and 8
February 2025 proves that interest was paid monthly, as the Loan Agreement required, in that period, and no more; what
that implies about the commencement of the 12 months is ‘arguably’ each way (II.G). Likewise: ‘AMPL and Grace entered
into a written loan agreement’ proves who entered into it and no more (the heading and parties clause are not narrated:
I.F2); ‘a letter of demand to Grace requiring repayment of the principal amount under the Loan Agreement with AMPL’ is
the narrator’s description of what the demand required, not Rocky’s quoted words nor the letter’s, so ‘the Loan
Agreement with AMPL’ is usable as the proven content of the demand and never as Rocky’s admission (CRIT_fidelity_3
M7, adopted; I.F2; T5); ‘AMPL called for payment of the Loan under the personal guarantee provided by Rocky’ proves
the call and that a guarantee was provided, so that AMPL called ‘on the undertaking’ (the oral promise) is the R-M
inference and is written ‘arguably’ at II.F2 exactly as at II.I1 and II.I2 (CRIT_round3 M8, adopted); ‘The Loan Agreement
also included a clause that Rocky personally guaranteed repayment of the Loan’ is the narrator’s description of the
clause’s effect, not the clause’s words, so the answer says ‘the clause is described as’ or quotes the narrator’s ‘that Rocky
personally guaranteed repayment of the Loan’, never ‘the clause states’ or ‘records’ (II.I1, II.I2; D4). The words ‘ensure
that Grace made all payments under the Loan Agreement promptly’ are the narrator’s report of what Pierre ‘expected’;
Rocky’s words are ‘I will do that if you also promise to pay $10,000 to me directly once Grace repays the Loan.’
Attributing ‘ensure … promptly’ to Rocky as his words is a D4 speaker fault. An answer that uses any narrator phrase as
evidence, says which use it makes, and marks the inference ‘arguably’, is eligible for Excellent whichever party the
inference helps. The words ‘narrator’, ‘narrated’, ‘narrative’ and ‘the facts narrate’ are this sheet’s analytic
labels and never words of the answer (R-R(1); CRIT_round4 M2): they join R7’s banned list, appear in no core
requirement’s prescribed wording and nowhere in the counted model; the drafter writes ‘nothing suggests’, ‘appears’, ‘is
shown’, ‘the facts arguably place’, ‘the clause described first’, ‘as described’, as his Part A does (‘nothing suggests SPC
knew or ought to have known’).

4. Evidence rule

A grade of Excellent is valid only if the marker (a) quotes the sentence or sentences of the answer that earn it, (b) names
which core items those sentences meet, and (c) confirms each rule the sentence states against the source page cited in
this sheet (not against this sheet’s paraphrase). A grade without that evidence is void. A sentence earns its full
requirement in one unit only; where a later unit cross-refers, that unit is graded on the clause that applies the earlier
finding to its own question. For every grade below Excellent the marker records the missing core item and a comment in
the marker’s style.

5. Placement

Substance is marked, and so is the one placement a ruling fixes. Rocky v Grace may run one A to F walk covering the 12
February email and the Loan Agreement (the counted structure) or two walks; AMPL’s call is a further heading inside
Rocky v AMPL (‘I. AMPL’s call’), never a Part III: the decide-once rule (§1 (iii)) runs the bargain’s elements once under
II.A to II.F, so a Part III would carry A to F headings with nothing under them or repeat Part II’s analysis, and v5’s Part III
option is withdrawn (CRIT_round5 m2, adopted; R-S(6)). The 12 months sits in Part II (Rocky v AMPL) as ‘G. The 12
months’, where it decides whether AMPL’s call was premature, with a one-clause cross-reference from Part I where
Rocky’s 5 March demand on Grace assumed the earlier date (R-Q; CRIT_round3 M9, adopted: two headings lettered G
and a bare ‘turns on G’ are removed); headings are lettered continuously within each Part, so Part II runs A to F, G (the
12 months), H (the condition) and I (AMPL’s call). A 12 months placed under Part I is graded on its substance but costs at
R3 (VG), because its relevance to Rocky’s claim against Grace is then unstated and the cross-reference crosses Parts.
What is marked: headings exactly ‘A. Offer’, ‘B. Acceptance’, ‘C. Consideration’, ‘D. Intention to create legal relations’, ‘E.
Certainty’, ‘F. Privity’ under each Part, in that order; further headings in course words or in the problem’s own words (‘G.
The 12 months’; ‘H. The condition’ (the v10 counted form; ‘condition’ is Gibbs CJ’s word at 581 to 582) or ‘H. The
condition ’once Grace repays the Loan’‘; ’I. AMPL’s call’ or ‘I. AMPL’s call of 9 March 2025’); from v11 headings G, H and
I may take a course-vocabulary form where it costs no more words than the counted form (R-X(6)): ‘H. Condition
precedent’ (three words for three; Gibbs CJ at Meehan 582, CB3B 184: ‘not a condition precedent to contract’, and ‘a
condition precedent to completion’), which also names that doctrine once for R7; a longer course form (‘G. Construction of
the 12 months’, +2; ‘I. Privity: AMPL’s call’, +1) is accepted and not deducted, but its words must be found inside the
answer’s own allocation under §1; the brief’s required A to F headings are unchanged; the sheet’s own labels (‘the
payment condition’, ‘prevention’, ‘the Pierre branch’) are never headings or text (R7; CRIT_fidelity_3 m13, adopted); a
heading that adopts the narrator’s ‘personal guarantee’ presupposes the content decided at II.I2 and is a VG at R3.

Part 3. Descriptor: PRELIMINARY

P. Preliminary: claim C3 and the Development Agreement (2.0; L; floor 34)

     Issue. What the advice covers: ‘advise Rocky on the success of any contractual claims he could make’, considering
     ‘only … the course materials from weeks 1 to 4’. The restriction governs what the answer covers; the answer never
     says so, never names weeks or the course, and never frames anything as outside the scope (R-Z(1), (4)).
     Core (v12; R-Z(1) and (5)). P’s core no longer requires excluded matters to be named: v11’s core (1), a clause
     naming breach, damages and guarantee law as ‘outside Weeks 1 to 4’, is withdrawn, and any such clause is from v12
     a meta reference graded at R1 and R8 under R-Z(5), not here. Breach (including Rocky’s own ‘promptly’ exposure),
     damages, guarantee law and frustration are simply not argued, and an answer that says nothing of them loses
     nothing. (1) Claim C3 identified and disposed of in substantive terms: any reimbursement or indemnity by Grace if
     Rocky pays AMPL under ‘the personal guarantee provided by Rocky’, the claim NAMED so that a cold reader can
     follow it (reimbursement for paying AMPL: UPGRADES_r1 U31, three of six cold readers in Stage-5 round 1 having
     been unable to identify C3 from ‘no reimbursement’ alone), and disposed of because no promise by Grace to Rocky
     to that effect appears. An accepted form is ‘Grace promised Rocky no reimbursement for paying AMPL’ (eight
     words); the v11 absolute clause ‘Grace having promised Rocky no reimbursement for paying AMPL’ is equally
     accepted where it trails a substantive main clause (CRIT_round6 m11’s order), never one that names matters as
     excluded; the critic’s ‘Grace promised Rocky nothing’ is equally acceptable, though ‘no reimbursement’ does not
     read against I.C’s inference that ‘Confirmed’ arguably implied a promise to repay (CRIT_round5 M5; R-S(2); Part 9
     item 29). (2) The Development Agreement as stipulated (‘legally binding’), Rocky’s entitlement arising only ‘if the
     Project made a profit’, which is not yet shown (the counted form; never ‘not yet narrated’: R-R(1)), so no present
     claim. Placement: both items may sit in a preliminary paragraph that states only what the advice covers in
     substantive terms, or, where the answer omits a preliminary paragraph (R-Z(1) permits it), wherever the answer first
     disposes of them (eg C3 in a clause at II.I4, the Development Agreement in a line at the head of Part I); they are
     graded here wherever they appear. A sentence restating the question’s own scope (‘assessed in New South Wales on
     the Weeks 1 to 4 materials’) is from v12 a meta reference graded under R-Z(5) at R1 and R8 (v3’s core (1),
     withdrawn by CRIT_round3 M15, stays withdrawn).
     Credit. ‘a valid and binding clause’ quoted; ‘minimal’ is still a profit, so the condition may be met on completion
     (further information, written with its direction, eg ‘unless the ’minimal’ profit, once realised, triggers it’: R6;
     UPGRADES_r1 U22 records this as credit, first among credit if words remain; a drafter who writes ‘not narrated’
     without ‘yet’ risks a marker reading the planted ‘minimal’ as missed: CRIT_round3 m17); NSW matters because the
     Electronic Transactions Act 2000 (NSW) governs the emails (stated in substantive terms, never as the task’s choice
     of law). Naming a doctrine no fact raises (eg estoppel with no reliance narrated; duress; the postal rule; adequacy) is
     a conciseness fault (R1), not a non-issue to be rewarded (CRIT_harsh_2 F10, adopted). Withdrawn from credit in v12
     (R-Z(1)): ‘each outside matter tied to its planted fact in a bracket’ (it named matters as excluded) and ‘agency is live
     because no ’assume … authorised’ note is given’ (it speaks about the question, not the law; the sheet keeps it as its
     own reason that agency is live).
     Drops. VG: claim C3 not identified anywhere in the answer (R4 then caps at Good: a claim missed), or C3 gestured
     at without saying what the reimbursement would be for (‘no reimbursement’ alone: UPGRADES_r1 U31); breach,
     damages or guarantee law argued rather than left alone. (Naming any matter, frustration included, as outside Weeks
     1 to 4, the course or the scope, or restating the question’s scope, is graded at R1 and R8 under R-Z(5), not here:
     v11’s ‘outside matter argued rather than flagged’, ‘one of the three not named’ and frustration credit-wrong lines are
     withdrawn, CRIT_round4 m17 and R-R(10) carried.) Good: the answer opens with a conclusion (‘Rocky has no claim
     against Grace …’), or the Development Agreement walked through formation. Fair: guarantee law or damages
     argued as governing law. Poor: textbook or non-course law applied; a breach claim on the Development Agreement
     asserted on facts not narrated; assignment or subrogation stated as a rule. Omitted: neither claim C3 nor the
     Development Agreement addressed anywhere in the answer.
     Marker comments. ‘Identify the non-issue and move on.’ ‘Name the claim you dispose of: reimbursement for what?’
     ‘Do not open with a conclusion.’ ‘This is advice to the client: do not refer to weeks, slides or the course.’ (D5 on the
     first line is this sheet’s rule, stricter than his LAWS1061 marker, who graded his conclusive one-line preliminary
     Excellent: Part 9, item 14.)



Part 4. Descriptors: PART I. ROCKY v GRACE (27%)

Settled results this Part reaches (ISSUES_SETTLED header table): the 12 February email gives Rocky no claim (strong on
the result; the formation route moderate, intention deciding); the Loan Agreement claim fails on privity (strong). One A to
F walk for both is the counted structure.

I.A Offer: the 5 February call and bullets 2 and 3; bullet 1 offer or invitation to treat (3.0; C; floor 73) [Gibson
298, distinguished and the similarity named with its direction]

     Issue. Whether ‘I will lend $1 million to you by 15 February for 1 year at a rate of 5% interest subject to satisfactory
     conditions’ is an offer capable of acceptance by ‘Confirmed’; and whether anything else in the call or the email could
be.
Rules at source. ‘you must manifest an intention to be bound by acceptance’ and ‘Cannot be an offer if there is not
an intention to create legal relations’ [S1A slide 11 | slide only: the elements ‘overlap’]; ‘Invitation to treat | An
invitation for others to make an offer or enter into a negotiation.’ [S1A slide 27 | slide only]; correspondence is read
by ‘the conventional approach of looking at the handful of documents relied upon as constituting the contract sued
upon and seeing whether upon their true construction there is to be found in them a contractual offer … and an
acceptance of that offer’ [CB1A 41 | 297 | Lord Diplock, Gibson v Manchester City Council | ratio; Lord Keith
agreeing, Lord Fraser agreeing with Lord Diplock and Lord Russell]; ‘The words “may be prepared to sell” are fatal
to this; so is the invitation, not, be it noted, to accept the offer, but “to make formal application to buy”’ [CB1A 42 |
298 | Lord Diplock | ratio]; the second paragraph: ‘If the final sentence had been omitted the wording of the second
paragraph, unlike that of the first, with its use of the indicative mood in such expressions as “the interest rate will
change”, might have been understood by council tenants to whom it was addressed as indicating a firm offer of a
mortgage’ [CB1A 42 | 298 | Lord Diplock | ratio: the paragraph that is Grace’s analogy]. The direction matters and is
a D2 fault if reversed (CRIT_fidelity_3 M1; CRIT_round3 M1, adopted): the indicative paragraph might have read as
a firm offer only WITHOUT its final sentence (‘This letter should not be regarded as firm offer of a mortgage’); with
the disclaimer it could not. An answer that says a disclaimed indicative promise might be read as a firm offer states
the opposite of the page; ‘The terms of the first sentence (“we have more work to do”, “where we’re at [499] the
moment”, “maybe”) are not those of a present offer.’ [CB1A 57 | 498 to 499 | the Court, Mobil Oil Australia Ltd v
Wellcome International Pty Ltd] (credit).
Facts (deciding). ‘proposed that Rocky offer’; ‘I will lend’; ‘Please confirm agreement’; ‘subject to satisfactory
conditions’ (deciding here as well as at D, because the similarity cannot be named without it; Appendix B now
agrees). Supporting (credit): ‘Potential project over in Eastwood subject to success of Hail Mary Project’; ‘wait and
see at present’; ‘long-term’; ‘Confirming discussions from our meeting this morning’.
Core. (1) Grace’s 5 February call classified in a clause on its deciding words, by the course’s name for it (‘proposed
that Rocky offer’: an invitation to treat, the course’s ‘invitation for others to make an offer or enter into a
negotiation’, S1A printed slide 27; RG1A (2): ‘How is an offer distinguished from an invitation to treat?’; the v11
form, ‘Grace’s call ’proposed that Rocky offer’, an invitation to treat’, replaces v10’s ‘a request’, −3: UPGRADES_r1
U7; R-X(4)), and bullets 2 and 3 disposed of in a clause ON THEIR DECIDING WORDS as not promissory (the v11
form: ‘bullets 2 and 3, ’Potential’ and ‘wait and see’, promise nothing’, +5, ‘wait and see’ quoted on one side of the
problem’s dash as D9 requires: UPGRADES_r1 U15, the deciding passages being the problem’s ‘Potential project
over in Eastwood subject to success of Hail Mary Project.’ and ‘wait and see at present.’ against S1A slide 11, ‘you
must manifest an intention to be bound by acceptance’; v10’s core asked only that the bullets be ‘disposed of in a
clause as not promissory’ while the Drops graded ‘the bullets not classified on a deciding word’ VG, a contradiction
now removed; Part 9 item 44). (2) The issue framed as construction of the exchange. (3) Gibson distinguished on its
operative words (‘may be prepared to sell’ and an invitation ‘to make formal application’, against ‘I will lend’ with
amount, date, term and rate and ‘Please confirm agreement’, which invites the assent that concludes a bargain). (4)
The similarity named with its direction: the Council’s letter’s second paragraph, written in the indicative mood,
which Lord Diplock construed (the v11 form is ‘the letter’s indicative paragraph’, or ‘the mortgage paragraph’, for
v10’s ‘Lord Diplock’s indicative paragraph’, which misattributed the letter to the judge: UPGRADES_r1 U27, the
deciding passage being CB1A 42, Gibson 298, ‘the wording of the second paragraph, unlike that of the first, with its
use of the indicative mood … might have been understood by council tenants to whom it was addressed as indicating
a firm offer of a mortgage’; zero words), an indicative promise, might have been understood (his words, or an
accurate paraphrase such as ‘could read’) as ‘a firm offer’ only absent its disclaimer; ‘subject to satisfactory
conditions’ may be the disclaimer’s analogue, which is why the qualifier is carried to D and E rather than decided
here (S1A slide 11: the elements overlap), and the cross-reference ‘(D, E)’ is written. The bare parenthetical is the
accepted minimum for the carry (CRIT_round4 m2: a marker may read it as a cross-reference rather than a reason);
the fuller ‘a question for intention and certainty (D, E)’ (+8 words) is credit, written where words allow. (5) A
classification (an offer in form, or an invitation to treat) in the moderate tier, with the reason in the same or the
preceding sentence; the counted form is ‘it is likely an offer’, and ‘it is likely an offer in form’ (+2), which says what
A decides and leaves bindingness to D and E, is credit, written where words allow (CRIT_round5 m9, not funded).
Credit. The Eastwood condition failing on the narrator’s projection (S1A slide 21, ‘A failure of a condition’, slide
only); Rocky would be the Eastwood promisor in any case; Rocky’s reply states no terms, so it invites negotiation, not
a counter-offer; ‘long-term’ explains ‘1 year’; ‘Confirming discussions’ as framing a record; the Restatement
definition via [3.10] (editors’ text, attributed to the Restatement if used); Willes J’s alternative construction in
Felthouse at 876 (a memorandum of a prior bargain; ‘the former is the more likely’ [CB1B 70 | 876]); the bullets as
‘discussions’ items that would not be terms of any bargain (the former I.E2 severance point is credit: severance
presupposes terms, and Whitlock 461 (Knox CJ’s principle as adopted by Taylor, Menzies and Owen JJ) must never
be stated as the joint judgment’s own words: D2). Further information: what was said at the meeting ‘this morning’.
Strength and tilt. Moderate. Either classification earns Excellent if the qualifier is carried to D and E and the result
for Rocky is the same; an answer calling it an invitation to treat must meet ‘I will lend’ and ‘Please confirm
agreement’; an answer calling it an offer must name the second-paragraph similarity.
Consistency. The qualifier is dealt with once (I.D) and carried; the answer must not hold the email an offer accepted
by ‘Confirmed’ and then advise Rocky to sue on it without D, E and F (I.F1).
Citation. Gibson v Manchester City Council [1979] 1 WLR 294, 298 (Lord Diplock) (297 for the conventional
approach if stated). No slide is cited (R-Z(2); v11’s ‘Slide statements cited by deck and slide number’ is withdrawn):
the slide-only points in this unit’s Rules (S1A slides 11 and 27) are stated, if at all, in the answer’s own words and
footnoted to Gibson where Gibson carries them, the invitation-to-treat classification resting on Gibson 298 (‘to make
    formal application to buy’).
    Drops. VG: Gibson not distinguished on its words, or the second-paragraph similarity not named, or the qualifier not
    carried to D and E, or the 5 February call or the bullets not classified on a deciding word (for the bullets, ‘Potential’
    and ‘wait and see’ or equivalent words quoted: U15), or the letter’s paragraph described as Lord Diplock’s own (a
    misattribution of the document, graded once here and not also under D2, the words being the letter’s and quoted
    correctly: U27). The call classified as ‘a request’ without the course’s name is graded at R7 (an applied doctrine
    unnamed), not here. Good: ‘the email is an offer’ with no reasons. Fair: offer rules stated generally (definition only);
    the bullets ignored while the email is treated as wholly promissory. Poor: the direction of Lord Diplock’s second
    paragraph reversed (a disclaimed promise said to read as a firm offer: D2); the qualifier ignored and a binding loan
    contract declared at A; ‘Confirmed’ treated as the offer; a contract found on 5 February or a claim on either bullet.
    Omitted: no offer analysis of the email. Credit wrong (VG): Rocky’s 5 February reply called a counter-offer (a credit
    item stated wrongly, graded by this line and never at Good: CRIT_round4 m17; R-R(10)). Credit wrong, Poor (D2):
    Knox CJ’s severance words as the joint judgment’s own, a wrong speaker, which D2 grades Poor with its −3 and §1
    caps at Fair in any event, so v9’s listing of it on the VG line is withdrawn and the fault is graded once, by D2
    (CRIT_round9 m4; R-U(6)).
    Marker comments. ‘Further analysis is required as your statement is conclusive.’ ‘Distinguish Gibson on its words,
    ’may be prepared to sell’ against ‘I will lend’, and meet the paragraph that resembles the email.’ ‘The second
    paragraph is the Council’s letter, not Lord Diplock’s.’ ‘Name the category: an invitation to treat.’ ‘The qualifier
    belongs to intention and certainty; carry it forward rather than deciding it twice.’

I.B Acceptance: ‘Confirmed’ takes the email as sent (2.0; L; floor 21) [Empirnall 534; eg Butler 404]

    Rules at source. ‘The objective theory of contract requires an external manifestation of assent to an offer.’ [CB1B
    71 | 534 | McHugh JA (Samuels JA agreeing; Kirby P to substantially the same effect), Empirnall Holdings Pty Ltd v
    Machon Paull Partners Pty Ltd | ratio]; ‘To form a contract, the acceptance must be made in response to the offer.’
    [CB1B 66 | [3.100] | editors]; a reply with ‘such additions … that it was in law a rejection of the offer and constituted
    a counter-offer’ [CB1B 94 | 404 | Lord Denning MR, Butler Machine Tool Co Ltd v Ex-Cell-O Corp (England) Ltd | the
    traditional analysis shared by Lawton and Bridge LJJ; ‘the counter-offer kills the original offer’ is Megaw J’s, quoted];
    ‘the offeror may always prescribe the method of acceptance’ [CB1B 68 | 233 | Isaacs ACJ, The Crown v Clarke |
    Higgins and Starke JJ concurring in the result] (credit); receipt ‘when the electronic communication becomes
    capable of being retrieved by the addressee at an electronic address designated by the addressee’ [CB1B 90 |
    Electronic Transactions Act 2000 (NSW) s 13A(1)(a)] (credit).
    Facts (deciding). ‘Confirmed’; ‘15 minutes later’; ‘Please confirm agreement.’
    Core. One sentence: the operative criterion (an external manifestation of assent) and the deciding facts
    (‘Confirmed’, ‘15 minutes later’, in the mode the email asked for, adding nothing, so no counter-offer, the doctrine
    named in the course’s term: RG1B (2), ‘What is the legal effect of a counter-offer?’; S1B PDF page 42; the v11 form
    adds ‘so no counter-offer’, +3: UPGRADES_r1 U7; R-X(4)) preceding the result that the reply took the email as sent,
    qualifier included, so that whatever ‘subject to satisfactory conditions’ does it does to the whole bargain (D, E). A
    form that says what ‘Confirmed’ did rather than what a word did (‘Grace’s ’Confirmed’ added no term, so it was
    assent to the whole email, not a counter-offer’) is equally Excellent; ‘qualifier included’ must have an antecedent the
    reader can see in the unit or the cross-reference (R1).
    Credit. Lapse and revocation (nothing suggests either on a fifteen-minute reply; not required: CRIT_harsh_2 F10);
    receipt under s 13A(1)(a) when retrievable at Rocky’s address, ‘arguably’ designated by his using it (one clause is
    neither required nor over-citation; a citation of s 13A for a point it decides nothing on invites ‘the reference should
    not include’); if anything formed, it formed on 12 February 2024; whether ‘Confirmed’ assented to a bargain or
    confirmed a record of ‘discussions’ is the Masters 360 question decided at D; Grace’s private need and anger
    irrelevant (objective test). Lord Westbury’s ‘a term of the assent’ (Masters 362) concerns an assent given ‘subject to
    a provision as to a contract’ and is not the rule for an unqualified ‘Confirmed’.
    Strength. Strong on correspondence; bindingness turns on D and E.
    Drops. VG: the criterion stated without the deciding facts, or counter-offer not excluded. Good: ‘Grace accepted’
    bare. Fair: ‘Confirmed’ treated as accepting an unqualified loan. Poor: the postal acceptance rule applied to the

I.C Consideration: a promise for a promise; the Loan Agreement’s fresh terms (2.0; L; floor 48; AMPL’s price
named) [eg Beaton 168, Australian Woollen Mills 456 to 457; Roscorla 237 credit here, required at II.I3]

    Rules at source. ‘consideration must be satisfied in the form of a price in return for the promisor’s promise or a
    quid pro quo. The price can be in the form of an act, forbearance or promise.’ [CB2A 104 | 168 | Kirby P, Beaton v
    McDivitt | majority on the result; alone on the consideration question]; ‘there must subsist, so to speak, [457] the
    relation of a quid pro quo’ [CB2A 101 | 456 to 457 | the Court, Australian Woollen Mills Pty Ltd v Commonwealth |
    ratio; said ‘In cases of this class’]; ‘a consideration past and executed will support no other promise than such as
    would be implied by law’ [CB2A 113 | 237 | Lord Denman CJ delivering the judgment of the Court, Roscorla v
    Thomas | ratio]; a new contractual entitlement as consideration: ‘The effect of accepting the 19 September 1991
    offer was to give a contractual entitlement. That is a benefit capable of amounting to consideration’ [CB1B 81 | 182 |
    Heydon JA, Brambles Holdings Ltd v Bathurst City Council | his reasoning alone: Ipp AJA (Mason P agreeing) found
    consideration in practical benefits (OFF Brambles [116], where Ipp AJA holds the 20 February 1990 letter ‘a
    contractual offer … accepted by conduct’, against the premise Heydon JA calls ‘crucial’ at 182, that the 1990 letters

    (credit).

I.D Intention to create legal relations: objective test and onus; ‘subject to satisfactory conditions’ decided
once; Rocky’s best case named with its authority (6.0; K; floor 122) [Ermogenous [25] to [26]; Masters 360,
361 (Niesmann) and 363]

    Issue. Whether the parties objectively intended to be bound immediately by the email exchange, or exchanged only
    ‘a record of terms … as a basis for the negotiation of a contract’.
    Rules at source.
      The inquiry ‘requires an objective assessment of the state of affairs between the parties … (as distinct from the
      identification of any uncommunicated subjective reservation or intention that either may harbour)’ and ‘may
      take account of the subject-matter of the agreement, the status of the parties to it, their relationship to one
      another, and other surrounding circumstances’ [CB3A 137 | [25] | Gaudron, McHugh, Hayne and Callinan JJ,
      Ermogenous v Greek Orthodox Community of SA Inc | majority, ratio]; ‘we doubt the utility of using the
      language of presumptions in this context. At best, the use of that language does no more than invite attention
      to identifying the party who bears the onus of proof.’ [CB3A 137 | [26] | same].
      The question Masters asked: ‘whether … this document on its true construction constitutes a binding contract
      … or only a record of terms upon which the signatories were agreed as a basis for the negotiation of a
      contract’; ‘All the essentials of a contract are there; but whether there is a contract depends entirely upon the
      meaning and effect of the final sentence’ [CB3A 154 to 155 | 360 | the Court (Dixon CJ, McTiernan and Kitto JJ),
      Masters v Cameron | ratio].
      The three classes, framed for parties who ‘also agree that the matter of their negotiation shall be dealt with by
      a formal contract’ [CB3A 155 | 360]; class 2 requires parties who ‘intend no departure from or addition to that
      which their agreed terms express or imply’ [360]; Niesmann (Rich and Starke JJ, as the Court reports them):
      signing ‘did not make the signing of a contract a condition of agreement, but made it a condition of the
      obligation to pay’ [CB3A 155 | 361]; class 3 where parties ‘have dealt only with major matters and contemplate
      that others will or may be regulated by provisions to be introduced into the formal document’ or ‘wish to
      reserve to themselves a right to withdraw’ [CB3A 155 | 361]; Lord O’Hagan, adopted: ‘if any prospective
      contract, involving the possibility of new terms … remains to be adopted, matters must be taken to be still in a
      train of negotiation’ [CB3A 155 | 361].
      ‘no special form of words is essential’ and ‘Nor is any formula, such as “subject to contract”, so intractable as
      always and necessarily to produce that result.’ [CB3A 156 | 362 | the Court]; Lord Westbury in Chinnock,
      adopted for the natural sense of ‘subject to contract’ [CB3A 156 | 362] (said of an assent given subject to a
      formal contract; not a rule for an unqualified ‘Confirmed’: I.B).
      Jessel MR in Winn v Bull, adopted, for a qualifier not referring to a formal contract: ‘When it is not expressly
      stated to be subject to a formal contract it becomes a question of construction, whether the parties intended
      that the terms agreed on should be put into form, or whether they should be subject to a new agreement the
      terms of which are not expressed in detail’ [CB3A 156 | 363 | the Court adopting Jessel MR; the official text
      reads ‘should merely be put into form’, the casebook dropping ‘merely’ (R-K): quote the casebook’s form or
      paraphrase]. This is the course passage nearest the planted qualifier and is the mandatory decisive authority.
      Official-only, CREDIT, never core (R-L(5)): the sentence the casebook elides at 362: ‘Again, Sir George Jessel
      M.R. said in Crossley v. Maycock (1874) LR 18 Eq 180: “if the agreement is made subject to certain conditions
      then specified or to be specified by the party making it, or by his solicitor, then, until those conditions are
      accepted, there is no final agreement such as the Court will enforce”’ [OFF Masters | paragraph 12, ‘(at p362)’
      | the Court | the Court’s own second illustration of ‘the natural sense of such words’ | official-only (O-10)]. The
      Court adopted Jessel MR’s words as showing ‘the natural sense of such words’; Crossley’s own facts are not in
      the materials (gap: Z9), so the answer must not characterise them (CRIT_fidelity_2 M1, adopted); the limit to be
      disclosed if used is that the paragraph is framed on ‘subject to contract’ and ‘expressions of similar import’ and
      keeps the hedges ‘no special form of words is essential’ and ‘Nor is any formula … so intractable’.
      Fourth class: parties ‘content to be bound immediately and exclusively by the terms which they had agreed
      upon whilst expecting to make a further contract in substitution’ [CB3A 157 | [5.190] | McLelland J in Baulkham
      Hills quoting Sinclair, Scott & Co v Naughton | editors’ note; S3A slide 24]; the categories ‘are intellectual aids,
      but whether parties have come to a binding agreement is a matter of their objectively ascertained intention’
      [CB3A 157 | [26] | Giles JA (McColl JA and Young CJ agreeing), Tasman Capital Pty Ltd v Sinclair | editors’ note
      quoting] (credit).
      Later conduct (credit): ‘post-contractual conduct is admissible on the question of whether a contract was
      formed … not admissible on the question of what a contract means’ [CB1B 73 | 164 | Heydon JA, Brambles |
      unanimous by adoption: Ipp AJA at 184, Mason P at 155 | RG skip (register only)]; Rocky’s ‘cure by later
      conduct’ argument (‘too uncertain or too illusory to enforce at a particular time … may by reason of the parties’
      subsequent conduct become sufficiently specific’) and its limit (‘must be capable of proving all the essential
      elements of an express contract’) [CB1B 78 to 79 | 177 to 178 | McHugh JA in Integrated Computer Services,
      quoted by Heydon JA].

I.E Certainty: illusory promise on the reading carried from D; Gibbs J’s dictum with its hedges and the

    Issue. Whether a promise to lend ‘subject to satisfactory conditions’ is too uncertain to bind because it leaves the
    lender a discretion whether to perform, or leaves an essential matter to future agreement.
    Rules at source.
         ‘A particular promise may be illusory because the contract effectively gives the promisor an unfettered
         discretion as to whether to perform the promise.’ [CB3B 159 | [6.10] | editors]; S3B slide 3 (the categories
         ‘often overlap’; ‘courts will strive to give effect to a contract if possible’).
         ‘A consideration is illusory if its payment or fulfilment depends upon an unfettered discretion vested in the
         promisor.’ and ‘even when no objective criterion of measurement is available, it may still be possible to infer a
         promise to act honestly and/or reasonably … the consideration will not be illusory if the discretion must be
         exercised within specified parameters.’ [CB3B 166 | 150 to 151 | McHugh JA, Biotechnology Australia Pty Ltd v
         Pace | majority in result with Kirby P; Hope JA dissenting].
         Kitto J in Placer, quoted by Gibbs CJ [CB3B 183 | 581 | Gibbs CJ quoting Placer Development Ltd v
         Commonwealth at 356 | cite as quoted, never as if read].
         Gibbs CJ’s limit: ‘that principle does not apply where the discretion or option of the contracting party relates,
         not to the performance of the contractual obligations themselves, but only to the fulfilment of a condition upon
      which the contract depends’ and ‘There is a concluded agreement as to the terms of the contract which, if the
      condition is satisfied, leaves no discretion in either party as to whether he shall carry them out.’ [CB3B 183 to
      184 | 581 to 582 | Gibbs CJ, Meehan v Jones | his reasoning; unanimous result; Aickin J died before judgment]; a
      clause ‘which speaks of “satisfactory terms and conditions”’ requires the purchaser’s satisfaction; ‘No doubt it
      may be implied that the purchaser will act honestly in deciding whether or not he is satisfied.’ [CB3B 183 | 581
      (the sentence sits wholly after the [581] marker) | Gibbs CJ]; Mason J: ‘subject to an implied obligation that he
      will act honestly, or honestly and reasonably, in endeavouring to obtain finance and in deciding whether to
      accept or reject proposals for finance’ [CB3B 185 | 588 | Mason J], and ‘The limitation that the purchaser must
      act honestly, or honestly and reasonably, takes the case out of the principle that’ a promise accompanied by a
      discretion whether to perform is no contract [CB3B 185 | 589 to 590 (the sentence sits before the [590] marker,
      which falls inside the quoted principle; OFF Meehan paragraph 23 closes ‘(at p590)’) | Mason J]; ‘it must be
      reached honestly, or honestly and reasonably’ [CB3B 186 | 590 | Mason J]; Wilson J: ‘the court will imply no
      greater obligation on the purchaser than that he is obliged to act honestly in determining whether the available
      finance is satisfactory’, ‘I am inclined to think there is force in this view’, and ‘it is sufficient for me, like Mason
      J, to refrain from expressing a concluded view’ [CB3B 188 | 597 to 598 | Wilson J, who agreed with Mason J
      ‘that each of the issues in the appeal should be resolved’ alike (597) but inclined to honesty alone]; no majority
      on honest against reasonable (v10 printed ‘Mason J (Wilson J agreeing)’ against the ‘honestly, or honestly and
      reasonably’ words, which Wilson J did not adopt, and printed 580 to 581 for Gibbs CJ’s sentence; both corrected
      in v11 from the source page). The meeting of the honesty fetter, same judge and same page: ‘This point would
      have force if the contract left the court at large to assess what is “finance on satisfactory terms and conditions”,
      yielding no indication as to what the parties meant by that expression.’ [CB3B 185 | 587 (before the [588]
      marker; UPGRADES_r1 U1 printed 588) | Mason J | inner quotation never reproduced (D9): quote only ‘left the
      court at large’ or paraphrase]. The official text of 578 reads ‘subject to finance, or to suitable finance, or to
      satisfactory finance’; the casebook drops the second and third limbs (R-K).
      The course passage squarely on terms reserved to one contracting party, now CORE (CRIT_round3 M11,
      adopted as a swap funded by P and I.F2): Gibbs J, obiter: ‘it does not necessarily follow from what I have said
      that an agreement which left further terms to be settled by one of the parties, rather than by his solicitors,
      would be treated as a concluded contract’; ‘it seems to me that there would be no binding contract in such a
      case, which would fall within the principle’ in Thorby v Goldberg (quoted); ‘Perhaps it may be different where
      agreement has been reached on all essential terms but the determination of subsidiary matters has been left to
      one of the parties’; ‘it is unnecessary to express any concluded opinion on these matters’ [CB3B 190 | 646 to
      647 | Gibbs J, Godecke v Kirwan | obiter, hedged both ways; never spliced (D2)]. Both hedges travel with it:
      ‘obiter’ (or the hedge ‘doubted’) and ‘Perhaps’ for subsidiary matters; the loan’s unspecified ‘conditions’ may be
      subsidiary (amount, date, term and rate being fixed) or essential, which is why the dictum tips but does not
      decide.
      Rocky’s counter, course-extracted (credit): Walsh J (Mason J agreeing) in Godecke, construing cl 6: what the
      solicitors could require ‘must be reasonable in an objective sense, and in case of [643] dispute this is a matter
      which the court can decide’; the purchaser ‘is agreeing presently to accept as part of the bargain such
      additional provisions, if any, as are required’ [CB3B 188 to 189 | 642 to 643 | Walsh J, Mason J agreeing at 648 |
      majority reasoning]. Official-only, credit (R-L(5)): Walsh J’s adoption of Bray CJ, ‘there is no reason in principle
      for holding that there cannot be any binding contract if some matter is left to be determined by one of the
      contracting parties’, ‘subject to the qualifications to which he refers’ (not reproduced in any file) [OFF Godecke
      | paragraph 16, ‘(at p642)’ | official-only (O-11)]. Gibbs J also disagrees with Bray CJ’s observation and finds
      Sweet & Maxwell rested on ‘the familiar and objective standard of reasonableness’ [CB3B 190 | 647 | Gibbs J]
      (credit).
      Kirby P (3) ‘the court will not accept for itself a discretion which the parties have, by their agreement, reserved
      to one or other of them’ and (4) ‘Usually, there is no objectively right decision in these cases’ [CB3B 163 to 164
      | 135 | Kirby P, Pace]; McHugh JA’s Thorby line at 154 to 155 (points toward Rocky; never cited as holding
      Meehan inapplicable); Murphy J at 596 to 597 (alone); Whitlock 461 and Hall v Busst 222 (analogies only);
      MacRobertson Miller 132 to 133 (Barwick CJ; the slide’s ‘we promise to fly you unless we choose not to’ is the
      slide’s own words, S1A slide 18).
Facts (deciding). ‘subject to satisfactory conditions’ (Rocky both promisor and judge of ‘satisfactory’). Supporting
(credit): no ‘reasonably’; no object named; bullet 2’s ‘subject to success of Hail Mary Project’.
Core. (1) The issue framed on the illusory-promise criterion (the promisor’s ‘unfettered discretion’; Pace 150 to 151
or Placer via Meehan 581 in the footnote). (2) Meehan, the opponent’s best authority, distinguished on Gibbs CJ’s
own limit, not on its result: ‘satisfactory terms and conditions’ bound there because the discretion related only to
‘the fulfilment of a condition upon which the contract depends’ (finance from a third party) and there was ‘a
concluded agreement as to the terms’, whereas here, on the reading decided at D and CARRIED in terms (‘(D)’ or ‘on
the reading at D’; the counted form: ‘whereas here (D) the conditions are the loan’s own terms’), the conditions are
terms of Rocky’s own loan (or, on the external reading, the email is Meehan-shaped and not illusory); Meehan’s
similarity is NAMED before it is distinguished (its clause, like the email, turned on what was ‘satisfactory’ to the
party who would perform: UPGRADES_r1 U1, special care 1). (2A) ROCKY’S BEST ANSWER PUT AND MET in a
clause (UPGRADES_r1 U1, adopted as core; R-X(5)): an implied duty to act honestly in judging what is ‘satisfactory’
would fetter the discretion and so save the promise, as Gibbs CJ, Mason J and Wilson J in Meehan each implied at
least honesty (Murphy J, alone, apart: implying ‘honest’ ‘adds nothing’, CB3B 187, 597; UPGRADES_r1’s ‘all three …
judges in the extract’ omits him) (Gibbs CJ 581, ‘No doubt it may be implied that the purchaser will act honestly’;
Mason J 588 to 590, for whom honesty ‘or honestly and reasonably’ is what ‘takes the case out of the principle’;
Wilson J 597) and as McHugh JA allowed at Pace 151 (‘even when no objective criterion of measurement is available,
it may still be possible to infer a promise to act honestly and/or reasonably’); met on the reading decided at D: an
honest judgment fetters a choice WITHIN agreed terms and cannot supply terms never agreed, which is what
‘satisfactory conditions’ leaves to Rocky (Mason J’s own limit at 587, the point ‘would have force if the contract left
the court at large … yielding no indication as to what the parties meant’; Gibbs J’s doubt at Godecke 646 about ‘an
agreement which left further terms to be settled by one of the parties, rather than by his solicitors’). The v11 form,
after ‘whereas here (D) the conditions are the loan’s own terms’: ‘, which Rocky’s implied duty to judge ’satisfactory’
honestly cannot supply’ (+10), footnoted Meehan 581 (Gibbs CJ) or 589 to 590 (Mason J), either accepted. Honesty
only is core: ‘or honestly and reasonably’ is credit and is never stated as settled by a majority (the credit-wrong line
below stands). On the external reading at D the fetter is accepted and the promise is not illusory, and that branch is
equally Excellent. Asserting the reading at E as fact, with no carry from D, where D reached it only as ‘the better
view … likely’, is overstatement (VG: CRIT_round4 M7, adopted). (3) Gibbs J’s dictum applied with both hedges in
the same sentence AND the ‘Perhaps’ exception answered: he doubted, obiter, that terms left to one party bind,
‘Perhaps’ excepting ‘subsidiary matters’, which the unspecified ‘conditions’ arguably exceed WITH THE REASON
STATED (Gibbs J’s own contrast at 647 is a ‘vital matter (such as price or rental)’ left to one party against ‘subsidiary
matters’; the reason is that the matters left to Rocky are conditions of the loan itself, its own terms, not a subsidiary
means of carrying a settled bargain into effect, so they are arguably vital rather than subsidiary; the counted form is
‘which the loan’s own conditions arguably exceed’, the words ‘the loan’s own conditions’ carrying the reason, and
‘which conditions of the loan itself arguably exceed’ (+1) is the same reason in the critic’s words: CRIT_round5 M4,
adopted under R-S(2)); so the promise is arguably illusory (or, on the external reading at D, not). A sentence that
states the ‘Perhaps’ and then concludes ‘so … illusory’ without saying why the conditions are not subsidiary is a non
sequitur, the decisive reason missing (VG: CRIT_round4 M7, adopted); v5’s ‘which these arguably exceed’ was that
shape, marking the inference without its reason (CRIT_round5 M4). (4) A conclusion consistent with D, HEDGED TO
THE STRENGTH THE SOURCE SUPPORTS, ‘arguably’, with I.D deciding the outcome (R-X(5): certainty alone is
weak to moderate, O-11, and the honesty fetter is a real answer on the materials; v10’s ‘likely’ is from v11 one tier
too strong for this unit, an overstatement graded VG under §2), the reason in the same sentence, and the outcome
for Rocky left to D (on either reading the 12 February result stands on I.F1). The case is named in fn 12 (Godecke
646–7 (Gibbs J)); naming it in the text as well (‘In Godecke, Gibbs J, obiter, doubted …’, +2) is credit, written where
words allow, because the counted S3 follows a Meehan sentence footnoted to Gibbs CJ and a marker reading the text
alone could take ‘Gibbs J’ for the Meehan judge and then read ‘obiter’ as wrong for a judgment just applied as ratio
(CRIT_round7 m2; not fundable at slack 1; Part 9 item 39).
Credit. Walsh J (Mason J agreeing) at 642 to 643 as the counter for Rocky, met (the clause there imported an
objective reasonableness standard and left the matter to solicitors, whereas ‘satisfactory’ names no standard and
leaves the terms to the promisor himself) or accepted (with Mason J’s ‘honestly, or honestly and reasonably’ as the
fetter); Walsh J at 641 to 642 (official-only); whether the loan’s ‘conditions’ are subsidiary or essential argued in
terms; Kirby P (3) and (4); McHugh JA’s Thorby line; Gibbs CJ at 579 (the objective reading he did not prefer);
Murphy J and waiver (met: waiver protects a party who is not the promisor of the performance in issue, and gives
Rocky nothing, he having lent nothing); Hall v Busst and Milne [6.20]; Pace’s two qualifications; MacRobertson
Miller by analogy; ‘5% interest’ without ‘per annum’ and ‘1 year’ without a start answered by Upper Hunter 436 to
437; ‘the better view’ form with a ‘though’ concession.
Strength and tilt. Weak to moderate on certainty alone (O-11), decided at D; presented as contested, the verdict
‘arguably’ (R-X(5)). Either conclusion can be Excellent if it identifies that the point turns on the meaning of
‘conditions’, takes the reading decided at D, applies Gibbs CJ’s limit (not merely Meehan’s result) and deals with the
honesty fetter: met on the internal reading (it cannot supply terms never agreed), accepted on the external reading
(the promise then not illusory). The Pace result and Hope JA’s dissent (CB3B 168 to 169) are credit.
Consistency. E and D decided once each and consistently; either way the 12 February result for Rocky (no claim)
stands on I.F1.
Citation. Meehan v Jones (1982) 149 CLR 571, 580–2 (Gibbs CJ); for the honesty fetter 581 (Gibbs CJ) or 589–90
(Mason J), on its own marker, one source per marker (R-R(3)); 587 (Mason J) if the meeting is footnoted; 590 (Mason
J); Biotechnology Australia Pty Ltd v Pace (1988) 15 NSWLR 130, 150–1 (McHugh JA) or 135 (Kirby P); Godecke v
Kirwan (1973) 129 CLR 629, 646–7 (Gibbs J) with ‘obiter’ (or ‘doubted’) and ‘Perhaps’ in the text; 642–3 (Walsh J,
Mason J agreeing) if used, 641–2 as an official-only footnote.
Drops. VG: the implied duty to act honestly not put, or put and not met (a promise held illusory with Rocky’s best
answer unanswered: U1; R-X(5)); the verdict stated ‘likely’ or unhedged rather than ‘arguably’ (R-X(5); §2);
Meehan’s similarity not named before it is distinguished; Meehan distinguished on its result only, not on Gibbs CJ’s
limit; Gibbs J’s dictum absent, or stated without ‘Perhaps’ (D3 and Poor if stated as a holding), or the ‘Perhaps’
exception stated and not answered; the reading asserted at E without the carry from D; the issue framed without the
criterion; a conclusion inconsistent with D. Good: ‘the clause is illusory’ or ‘Meehan applies’ stated. Fair: certainty
heads listed with no application; Meehan not mentioned. Poor: McHugh JA at 154 to 155 cited as holding Meehan
inapplicable (a misreading: S-5); the Gibbs J splice (D2); Murphy J’s ‘unlimited’ discretion stated as the Court’s
holding; Placer quoted as if from its own extract; the slide’s ‘unless we choose not to’ attributed to Barwick CJ.
Omitted. Credit wrong: honest/reasonable treated as settled by a majority (VG); Walsh J 641 to 642 quoted as the
casebook’s text.
