II.A Offer: Pierre’s ‘expected’ likely a request; Rocky’s words an offer of a bilateral contract (2.5; L; floor 33)
[Gibson 298 for the criterion applied to ‘expected’; Brambles 177 (Heydon JA quoting McHugh JA), or Mobil
500 by contrast, for the bilateral contract; Butler 404 credit (the counter-offer point); S1A slides 11 to 12 are
not the citation. The three are authorities for three different propositions and were never alternatives for one
another, so v6’s ‘eg’ list, which §1 reads as alternatives one of which suffices, is withdrawn: CRIT_round6 m9]

     Rules at source. The criterion of an offer in a course case: a letter is an offer only if it is ‘a contractual offer capable
     of being converted into a legally enforceable open contract … by Mr Gibson’s written acceptance of it’ [CB1A 42 |
     298 | Lord Diplock, Gibson]; the bilateral contract in a course case: ‘In classical theory, the typical contract is a
     bilateral one and consists of an exchange of promises by means of an offer and its acceptance’ [CB1B 78 | 177 |
     McHugh JA in Integrated Computer Services Pty Ltd v Digital Equipment Corp (Aust) Pty Ltd, quoted by Heydon JA
     in Brambles]; ‘A unilateral contract is one in which the act of acceptance of the offer is also an executed
     consideration for the promise offered.’ [CB1A 59 | 500 | the Court, Mobil]; the slides’ wording, ‘Must contain an
     invitation/ a proposal/ an option that the other party can accept or reject’ [S1A slide 11 | slide only] and ‘Bilateral
     contract – contract is made by exchange of promises to do something – then the parties do what they agreed’ [S1A
     slide 12 | slide only; the slide’s dashes never reproduced], may be paraphrased but is NOT cited, because course
     cases state the point and a slide citation where a course case states the point is over-citation (R-P; CRIT_round3 M6,
     adopted in substance); a reply adding a term ‘was in law a rejection of the offer and constituted a counter-offer’
     [CB1B 94 | 404 | Lord Denning MR, Butler]; the request test: ‘whether there has been a request by the alleged
     promisor that the promisee shall do the act on which the latter relies. Such a request may, of course, be expressed or
     implied.’ [CB2A 101 to 102 | 458 | the Court, Australian Woollen Mills]; ‘the language of command’ [CB1B 76 | 171 |
     Heydon JA | obiter] (credit).
     Facts (deciding). ‘expected’; ‘I will do that if you also promise’.
     Core. One sentence: the operative criterion (a proposal the other party can accept so as to bind the proposer) and
     the deciding word: Pierre’s ‘expected’ proposed nothing Rocky could accept so as to bind Pierre, so it was LIKELY a
     request (relevant to consideration), not an offer, the hedge matching the moderate-to-strong tier (§1; CRIT_round4
     M4, adopted: v4’s unhedged ‘so it was a request’ was its own VG); Rocky’s ‘I will do that if you also promise’,
     seeking a return promise rather than an act, offered a bilateral contract (strong; unhedged). ‘Likely an invitation to
     treat, not an offer’ (+2) is equally Excellent at II.A, Pierre’s expectation inviting the offer Rocky then made; R7
     requires the course’s name for an invitation to treat once in the answer, and the counted structure gives it at I.A, so
     ‘a request’ here (whose function is the consideration point at II.C2) is not an R7 lapse (R-X(4); UPGRADES_r1 U7).
     Citation. Two propositions, two markers (R-R(3); the counted form): Gibson 298 (Lord Diplock) on the criterion
     applied to ‘expected’ (‘so it was likely a request’), and Brambles 177 (Heydon JA), quoting McHugh JA in Integrated
     Computer Services, on the bilateral contract; v5’s single footnote listing both is withdrawn (CRIT_fidelity_5 Finding
     5).
     Credit. The definite terms (‘$10,000’; ‘to me directly’; ‘once Grace repays the Loan’); the request test in Australian
     Woollen Mills 458 named for ‘so it was a request’; if Pierre’s words were an offer, Rocky’s ‘$10,000’ term made his
     reply a counter-offer (Butler 404), the operative offer still being Rocky’s; the unilateral reading named and rejected
     on ‘if you also promise’, with Mobil 506 stated in its own terms (no ‘universal proposition’ against revocation after
     commencement).
     Strength. Moderate to strong that Pierre’s words were not the accepted offer (S-31); strong that Rocky’s offer was
     of a bilateral contract. A conclusion that Pierre’s words were the accepted offer is capped at Very Good; a unilateral
     contract found against ‘if you also promise’ is Poor.
     Drops. VG: either classification without its deciding word, or the criterion absent; ‘a request’ unhedged (the tier). (A
     slide cited for the criterion is graded at R1 and R8 under R-Z(5), not here; v11’s ‘a citation fault at R8’ line is
     superseded.) Good: ‘not an offer’ or ‘Rocky made an offer’ bare. Fair: ‘expected’ treated as an enforceable promise
     by Pierre; offer elements recited generally. Poor: Pierre’s words held an offer accepted by ‘I will do that’ with the
     $10,000 ignored; a unilateral contract with Rocky’s performance as acceptance. Omitted.
     Marker comments. ‘Classify Pierre’s words with the deciding word: ’expected’.’ ‘Read the requested act or promise
     from the offer’s own words.’

II.B Acceptance: ‘Pierre agreed’; the oral bargain bound without the document; Lord Greene MR’s analogy
disclosed (3.0; C; floor 62) [Empirnall 534 or Brinkibon 41; Brambles 176 (Heydon JA, quoting Lord Greene
MR); Masters 361]
    Rules at source. ‘The objective theory of contract requires an external manifestation of assent to an offer.’ [CB1B
    71 | 534 | McHugh JA, Empirnall]; ‘In the common case of contracts, whether oral or in writing inter praesentes,
    there is no difficulty’ [CB1B 88 | 41 | Lord Wilberforce, Brinkibon | reasoning on where a contract is formed];
    Masters class 1: parties who ‘have reached finality in arranging all the terms of their bargain and intended to be
    immediately bound … but at the same time propose to have the terms restated in a form which will be fuller or more
    precise but not different in effect’ [CB3A 155 | 360 | the Court]; Lord Blackburn, as the Court reports him: a
    stipulated formal agreement ‘does not, by itself, show that they continue merely in negotiation’ [CB3A 155 | 361];
    Heydon JA’s examples where ‘Offer and acceptance analysis does not work well’: exchanges of contracts to sell land,
    of which Lord Greene MR observed ‘Parties become bound by contract when, and in the manner in which, they
    intend and contemplate becoming bound. It is a question of the facts of each case’, and, in the next paragraph,
    ‘contracts between numerous parties, or even two parties, negotiated at meetings but not assented to until each
    party executes counterparts’ [CB1B 77 | 176 | Heydon JA, Brambles, quoting Lord Greene MR in Eccles v Bryant
    (cited there as ‘[1948] 1 Ch 93 at 104’, Heydon JA’s slip for ‘[1948] Ch 93’: R-X(2)) of the land-exchange practice |
    his reasoning on acceptance; neither example states that meetings bind only on execution; Lord Greene MR’s words
    never attributed to the meetings example (D2); ‘in the next paragraph’, not ‘two sentences later’: CRIT_fidelity_3
    m15, adopted]. Masters 360 to 361 states that in the first two classes ‘there is a binding contract’ and class 3’s ‘wish
    to reserve to themselves a right to withdraw’; it does not state the ‘when and how they contemplate’ formula, which
    is Lord Greene MR’s alone (CRIT_fidelity_3 M5, adopted).
    Facts (deciding). ‘Pierre agreed’. Supporting (credit): ‘before it was signed by Grace and AMPL’ (the one fact
    AMPL’s execution argument could cite; moved from deciding to supporting in v9 to match Appendix B and the
    counted paragraph, which quotes only ‘agreed’ and puts AMPL’s argument without a fact, the argument being weak
    because the document was ‘signed by Grace and AMPL’ and nothing suggests that Rocky was to sign it:
    CRIT_round8 m2); Rocky not a signatory; ‘Rocky would check in consistently with Grace via email’ (post-formation
    conduct, admissible on formation).
    Core. (1) One sentence: the criterion (an external manifestation of assent in response) and the deciding fact: Pierre
    ‘agreed’ (the counted form: ‘Pierre ’agreed’, communicating unqualified assent in response’; never ‘the narrator’s
    ’Pierre agreed’‘: R-R(1), CRIT_round4 M2, adopted), so the bargain formed on 8 April 2024, subject to who accepted
    (F). (2) AMPL’s argument put in a clause (nothing bound until the document was executed, by analogy with contracts
    negotiated at meetings and assented to on execution) and met with the analogy disclosed in the text: ’as Lord Greene
    MR said of exchanges of contracts’, parties are bound when and how they contemplate becoming bound, footnoted
    Brambles 176 in the AGLC r 1.3 form, ‘Brambles (n X) 176 (Heydon JA), quoting Eccles v Bryant [1948] Ch 93, 104
    (Lord Greene MR)’ (R-X(2); UPGRADES_r1 U8: the report is ‘[1948] Ch 93’, as the Court prints it in Masters at CB3A
    156, ‘Eccles v Bryant and Pollock [1948] Ch 93 at 94’, and as the official Masters text prints it; the ‘[1948] 1 Ch 93’
    at CB1B 77 is Heydon JA’s own slip, faithfully reproduced by the casebook, the 1948 Chancery volume carrying no
    volume number; only the first-named party on each side is cited, so ‘and Pollock’ is dropped; v10’s ‘1 Ch’ form is
    withdrawn and is a citation fault (VG at R8) from v11; R8; Part 9 item 30); and nothing in the exchange made the
    bargain subject to a document or reserved a right to withdraw (Masters 361 for the reservation of a right to
    withdraw, on its own marker: the counted form carries two markers inside the sentence, one per proposition, and no
    sentence-end marker). Stating the formula as a general rule, footnoted to Masters 360 to 361 or to Heydon JA as his
    own, is the D2 fault (CRIT_fidelity_3 M5; CRIT_round3 M2 at II.B, adopted). (3) A conclusion in the moderate tier
    (‘likely’), with the consistency consequence that the $10,000 and the undertaking stand or fall together (T8).
    Credit. Only AMPL and Grace were to sign; the record in the Loan Agreement as class 1 at most; ten months of
    check-ins as conduct confirming a bargain; Heydon JA’s test for cases where offer and acceptance cannot be found
    (‘can an agreement be inferred?’ [CB1B 80 | 179], his route); Pierre’s private view of whom he spoke for not decisive.
    Strength and tilt. Acceptance in response strong on ‘agreed’; formation of a binding contract with someone
    moderate to strong (ISSUES_SETTLED S-15; v6’s ‘Formation strong’ departed from S-15 without a reason, which R-
    R(9) forbids, and is corrected: CRIT_round6 m7; no Drop depended on it, so no grade changes); the oral bargain
    binding without the document moderate (S-17). Either conclusion can be Excellent if the parties’ contemplated mode
    of becoming bound is applied to the stated facts and T8 is drawn; a ‘no oral contract’ conclusion must advise that the
    undertaking falls with it.
    Drops. VG: AMPL’s analogy not met; Lord Greene MR’s words used without the disclosure; T8 not drawn; the
    criterion absent at (1). Good: ‘an oral contract formed’ bare. Fair: a writing requirement asserted (none in the
    materials). Poor: Lord Greene MR’s words attributed to the meetings example or stated as Heydon JA’s rule that
    meetings bind only on execution (D2); Masters class 3 found to defeat the $10,000 while the undertaking is held
    binding. Omitted.
    Voice note. The one-clause counter-frame ‘AMPL would say nothing bound until execution.’ is kept as a frame under
    R1 (his ‘On the other hand, only SPC knew its finances and that GR would rely’); CRIT_round5 m10’s proposal to join
    it to the next sentence at no cost was REJECTED in v6, and remains so, because the body’s under-12 share would fall
    to 8.5%, below his Parts’ floor of 9.1% (the round-5 additions removed two short verdicts), so the frame now carries
    the voice statistics as well as the counter.
    Marker comment. ‘Consider how these parties contemplated becoming bound; the same answer governs both sides
    of the bargain.’

II.C1 Consideration: the price of Rocky’s promise, which moved from him; if Pierre promised, Rocky’s
detriment (1.5; L; floor 26) [Australian Woollen Mills 456 to 457; Beaton 181 (McHugh JA, quoting Patteson
J); Musumeci 745]
    Rules at source. ‘it should be made to appear that the statement or announcement which is relied on as a promise
    was really offered as consideration for the doing of the act, and that the act was really done in consideration of a
    potential promise inherent in the statement or announcement’ and ‘the relation of a quid pro quo’ [CB2A 101 | 456 to
    457 | the Court, Australian Woollen Mills | ratio; said of unilateral contracts, adopted as the bargain requirement by
    Kirby P in Beaton at 168]; consideration ‘must be moving from the plaintiff’ [CB2A 107 | 181 | Patteson J in Thomas v
    Thomas, quoted by McHugh JA]; ‘It is of course long settled that detriment to the promisee suffices as consideration’
    [CB2B 122 | 745 | Santow J, Musumeci v Winadell Pty Ltd | first instance, NSW]; ‘Must flow between the parties in
    both directions’ [S2A slide 2 | slide only]; RG2A: ‘Must consideration be of benefit to the other party?’.
    Facts (deciding). ‘if you also promise’. Supporting: ‘$10,000’ against ‘$1,150,000’.
    Core. One or two sentences: the criterion (the price of the promise, moving from the promisee) and the deciding
    words: the $10,000, given ‘if you also promise’, priced Rocky’s promise, WHICH (Rocky’s promise, his undertaking)
    moved from him as promisee; the $10,000 moves from AMPL or Pierre, and a sentence whose grammar makes the
    $10,000 move from Rocky is a confused statement of ‘consideration must move from the promisee’ (Good;
    CRIT_round3 M2, adopted); on the alternative that Pierre promised personally (‘If Pierre promised’, never ‘the Pierre
    branch’: R7), ROCKY’S detriment (undertaking to procure another’s performance) LIKELY suffices though the
    benefit runs to AMPL: the promisee is named by name, never by a pronoun whose nearest antecedent is Pierre (v4’s
    ‘his detriment’ read as Pierre’s and stated the wrong promisee’s detriment: CRIT_round4 M3, adopted), and the
    verdict carries ‘likely’ for the moderate-to-strong tier (CRIT_round4 M4). Each proposition carries its own footnote
    marker (CRIT_round3 m2; R-R(3)): Australian Woollen Mills 456 to 457 on ‘priced Rocky’s promise’ (the bargain
    relation); Beaton 181 (McHugh JA, quoting Patteson J in Thomas v Thomas: consideration ‘must be moving from the
    plaintiff’) on ‘which moved from him as promisee’ (CRIT_fidelity_4 Finding 5, adopted: 456 to 457 states the bargain
    requirement, not the ‘moving from’ rule in terms); Musumeci 745 on the detriment sentence.
    Credit. Adequacy not weighed ([4.150]); not McHugh JA’s first situation in Beaton at 182 to 183 (minority on the
    result); the practical benefit exception, the bona fide compromise and the deed (S2B slide 4) belong at II.C2 and only
    on AMPL’s sequence.
    Strength. Strong if AMPL promised; moderate to strong if Pierre promised.
    Drops. VG: the Pierre alternative run without saying why a benefit to a third person is no objection, or the criterion
    absent; ‘suffices’ unhedged on the Pierre alternative; ‘moving from the promisee’ footnoted to 456 to 457 alone (a
    citation imprecision). Good: ‘there is consideration’ bare; the $10,000 said to move from Rocky; the wrong party’s
    detriment named or implied by a pronoun. Fair: consideration found in the 27 February request; the practical benefit
    exception used as the main route. Poor: the exception said to apply because AMPL benefits (element (ii) ignored).
    Omitted.

II.C2 Consideration: the existing legal duty rule, conditional on who promised (5.5; K; floor 126) [Wigan 594;
Sundell 327; Pao On 632 if Pierre promised (the casebook [4.300] never needed, Pao On stating the point: R-
Z(3))]

    Issue. Whether Rocky’s ‘I will do that’ was consideration, or only a promise to do what he was already bound to do;
    and to whom any prior duty was owed.
    Rules at source. ‘The general rule is that a promise to perform an existing duty is no consideration, at least when
    the promise is made by a party to a pre-existing contract, when it is made to the promisee under that contract, and it
    is to do no more than the promisor is bound to do under the contract. The rule expresses the concept that the new
    promise, indistinguishable from the old, is an illusory consideration.’ [CB2B 131 to 132 | 594 | Mason J, Wigan v
    Edwards | majority reasoning (Walsh and Gibbs JJ agreeing; Menzies J and McTiernan ACJ reaching the same
    conclusion)]; AMPL’s best authority: ‘the only consideration which was suggested in argument as being given in
    exchange for the alleged promise was that the [defendant] would do what was necessary to ensure that the goods
    were delivered. But we are satisfied that the [defendant] was bound to do so under the original contract. In our view
    every promise allegedly given to the plaintiff by the defendant under the so called second agreement was identical
    with a promise given under the original agreement.’ [CB2B 113 to 114 | 327 | the court in TA Sundell & Sons Pty Ltd
    v Emm Yannoulatos (Overseas) Pty Ltd, quoted in the editors’ note [4.190]; no judge named; S2B slide 8]; ‘The
    presence of a request does not, however, in itself establish a contract.’ [CB2A 103 | 550 | the Privy Council in
    Australian Woollen Mills]; the course’s exception for a duty owed to a third party: ‘A promise to perform an act
    which the promisor is under an existing duty to a third party to perform may constitute good consideration.’ [CB2B
    125 | [4.300] | editors’ note]; ‘Their Lordships do not doubt that a promise to perform, or the performance of, a pre-
    existing contractual obligation to a third party can be valid consideration.’ [[1980] AC 614, 632 | the Board | verified
    in the official text; course-stated at [4.300] and S2B slides 5 and 9 (‘promises to third parties’)]; the practical benefit
    exception: Glidewell LJ’s elements, (i) ‘if A has entered into a contract with B to do work for, or to supply goods or
    services to, B in return for payment by B’, (ii) ‘at some stage before A has completely performed his obligations
    under the contract B has reason to doubt whether A will, or will be able to, complete his side of the bargain’ (B, the
    party promising the extra payment, doubts A, the performing party; v3’s ‘whether B will perform’ reversed the
    parties: CRIT_fidelity_3 M3, adopted), (v) ‘B’s promise is not given as a result of economic duress or fraud on the
    part of A’ [CB2B 119 | 15 to 16 | Glidewell LJ, Williams v Roffey Bros & Nicholls (Contractors) Ltd], Santow J’s recast
    [CB2B 123 | 747 | first instance]; the bona fide compromise qualification [CB2B 132 | 594 to 595 | Mason J]; Wigan
    595 (‘the additional promise should be regarded as an element of the bargain’) as an analogy only, never a rule of
    interdependence; Stilk via Glidewell LJ at 11 (Lord Ellenborough CJ’s passage is printed after the [11] marker and
    the extract elides before [13]; no [12] is printed: CRIT_fidelity_3 m16, adopted) (an analogy for promising more).
    Facts (deciding). ‘also’; ‘Pierre agreed and the clause was included’; ‘ensure that Grace made all payments …
promptly’ (the narrator’s report of Pierre’s expectation); ‘repayment of the Loan’. Supporting: the clause narrated
before the exchange; on 27 February Rocky only ‘asked’; ‘the personal guarantee provided by Rocky’.
Core. (1) The rule NAMED in the course’s term, the existing legal duty rule (RG2B: ‘What is the existing legal duty
rule?’; S2B PDF page 8 heading ‘Existing Legal Duty Rule’; the v11 form opens ‘The existing legal duty rule’ for
v10’s ‘The general rule’, +2: UPGRADES_r1 U7; R-X(4)), and stated in Mason J’s words with its hedge (‘at least
when’), applied to its own premises: was Rocky a party to a pre-existing contract with the promisee when he
promised, and was the promise ‘made to the promisee under that contract’? The rule’s content (‘a promise to
perform an existing duty is no consideration’) must appear in the paragraph, in S1 or carried by the Sundell clause
of S2 (‘a promise to ensure performance already owed is … no consideration’), as the counted paragraph carries it; a
paragraph that quotes only the hedge and never states what the rule does is conclusive under §1(b) (CRIT_round7
m3, a note at no cost). (2) AMPL’s strongest argument at full strength: ‘also’ and the clause described before the
exchange presuppose a guarantee already given, and Sundell denied consideration to a promise to ensure
performance already owed (paraphrased without quotation marks and cited by report, or quoted in the ‘quoted in’
form: see Citation; the counted form is ‘AMPL would say ’also’ and the clause described first presuppose an existing
guarantee: a promise to ensure performance already owed is, as in Sundell, no consideration’). (3) Rocky’s answers,
numbered as limbs (i) to (iv) so that the conditional core and the Strength line refer to them without ambiguity
(CRIT_round9 m10; R-U(6)): (i) no earlier binding promise by Rocky appears; (ii) Rocky’s 27 February request ‘does
not … in itself establish a contract’, the limb TIED TO ITS FACT (‘Rocky asked’ AMPL on 27 February ‘to advance
$1,150,000 to Grace’), never ‘a request’ or ‘his request’ without saying whose and when, three cold readers in Stage-
5 round 1 having been unable to tell whose request (the v11 form ‘Rocky’s 27 February request’ for ‘a request’, +2:
UPGRADES_r1 U20, the deciding passage being CB2A 103, Australian Woollen Mills (PC) 550, ‘The presence of a
request does not, however, in itself establish a contract’; a limb applied to no fact is conclusive under §1(b)); (iii) the
sequence ‘Pierre agreed and the clause was included’ arguably places the clause after the exchange (‘arguably’: R-
M; the counted form is ‘The facts arguably place the clause after the exchange’, never ‘the narrative’ or ‘is narrated’:
R-R(1)); and (iv) ensuring ‘all payments … promptly’ exceeds ‘repayment of the Loan’ (the defined principal), a
promise to do more (whether it does is a construction of the two quoted phrases, not a fact either way: the counted
‘exceeds’, unhedged because it compares the words on their face, ‘all payments’ taking in the monthly interest and
‘repayment of the Loan’ naming the defined principal, ‘arguably exceeds’ (+1, not funded) and, on the contrary
branch, ‘arguably adding nothing to a guarantee’ are all accepted, the verdict’s ‘likely’ carrying the strength: Part 9
item 38(h); CRIT_round8 m5) (credit, in terms: Sundell’s promisor owed an identical duty to the same promisee
under a prior contract, whereas no prior contract bound Rocky). (4) The alternative that Pierre promised, conditional
on II.F2 (CRIT_harsh_2 F4, adopted): if Pierre promised personally, the rule’s second premise fails on its own words,
the promise being made to Pierre while any prior guarantee was owed to AMPL, and the course’s exception for a
promise to perform a duty owed to a third party (Pao On 632, the Board: ‘a promise to perform, or the performance
of, a pre-existing contractual obligation to a third party can be valid consideration’; the editors’ [4.300] states it too
but is never the citation, a case stating it: R-Z(3)) supplies consideration. (5) A conclusion in the tier for the strength,
carrying its reason in the verdict sentence without restating (3) and (4) (the counted form: ‘On balance, Rocky likely
gave consideration, his promise exceeding anything that bound him, though the order of clause and $10,000 would
settle it’; v5’s verdict restated S3’s and S4’s reasons at a cost of six words and was shortened under R-S(4), the
reason surviving in the clause ‘his promise exceeding anything that bound him’), with the further information that
settles it (the order in which the clause and the $10,000 were agreed) in the trailing clause, STATED WITH ITS
DIRECTION (the v11 form: ‘though a clause agreed first would favour AMPL’, −2 against v10’s ‘though the order of
clause and $10,000 would settle it’, which names the information and not its effect: UPGRADES_r1 U3; R6;
Instructions p 1, ‘how it would impact your conclusion’).
Conditional core (the contrary branch, R-E; CRIT_complete_bias_2 A-3, adopted). An answer that concludes
Rocky was arguably already bound to the same promisee must then run ‘the exceptions to the general rule’ with ‘the
exact elements of those exceptions’ (RG2B-Q1): the practical benefit exception named and set aside in a clause on
Glidewell LJ’s elements (i) and (ii) (a guarantee is not ‘work … goods or services’; B’s ‘reason to doubt’ is doubt
whether A will complete, and Pierre’s doubt was about Grace, who ‘was experiencing significant financial
difficulties’, not about Rocky), the bona fide compromise excluded (no disputed claim narrated), and the
consequence drawn for the $10,000 and the undertaking (T8). Two drafting rules on this branch (CRIT_round10 m4
and m5, adopted as rule text): elements (i) and (ii) are footnoted Williams v Roffey 15 (Glidewell LJ), both sitting
before the [16] marker (CB2B 119), never ‘15–16’, which is the pin only where element (v) is also stated (‘the
reference should not include …’); and element (ii) is written as doubt ABOUT Grace (‘doubt arguably about Grace’),
never ‘Grace’s doubt’ or a possessive that makes Grace the doubter, B’s ‘reason to doubt’ being doubt held by the
promising party about the performing party. On that branch those items replace core item (3)(iii) (the order of clause
and $10,000, which the branch accepts, marked ‘arguably’); the ‘more than’ limb, (3)(iv), is NOT replaced, because
Mason J’s rule applies only where the promise ‘is to do no more than the promisor is bound to do under the contract’
(594), so a ‘no consideration’ conclusion must address whether ensuring ‘all payments … promptly’ exceeded a
guarantee of ‘repayment of the Loan’, and on this branch it does so in a clause marked ‘arguably’ (the counted form:
‘’all payments … promptly’ arguably adding nothing to a guarantee’); v7’s ‘replace … the fourth limb’ contradicted
the Strength line below and is withdrawn (CRIT_round7 m8, adopted; R-T(2)). The floor is the same, and the
appendix (section 7) carries a counted contrary-branch paragraph of 126 words, at the 126 floor, with its own
verifier run (3 quotations, 0 FAIL), so that R-L(2) holds on both branches.
Credit. The practical benefit exception named and set aside on the preferred conclusion too; Wigan 595 as an
analogy with its compromise context named; Stilk’s qualification; whether the guarantee was executed as a deed
(S2B slide 4); Mobil 508 (the words ‘extant obligation’ are the trial judge’s, quoted by the Full Court, which then
    decides in its own words that the franchisees’ performance ‘did not attract the rule that performance of an existing
    contractual obligation owed to the promisor cannot qualify as valuable consideration’; a drafter who quotes ‘extant
    obligation’ attributes it to the trial judge, never to the Court: CRIT_fidelity_4 Finding 7, adopted); the consistency
    point that AMPL cannot both say Rocky was bound from 27 February and rely on that advance as consideration for
    the undertaking (T17, stated once here or at II.I3).
    Strength and tilt. Consideration given for the $10,000 (moderate to strong, S-11). A contrary conclusion is capped
    at Very Good (its ceiling on this branch), and reaches it only if it rests on the order of clause and $10,000 and ‘also’
    marked ‘arguably’, addresses the ‘more than’ limb, answer (3)(iv) (marked ‘arguably’ on this branch), and meets
    answers (3)(i) and (ii) (on that branch by the reading that ‘also’ and the clause described first presuppose an earlier
    assent, so that Rocky was arguably bound, the counted contrary paragraph’s S2: appendix section 7), and runs the
    conditional core; it is Poor only where an earlier binding promise by Rocky is asserted as a narrated fact (D4). The
    preferred conclusion must itself mark ‘the clause followed the agreement’ as ‘arguably’. The Fair drop for the
    practical benefit exception applies only where it is run without first asking whether there was a pre-existing
    contract.
    Consistency. T8 (the $10,000 and the undertaking are one bargain) stated here or at II.B; T17 as above.
    Citation. Wigan v Edwards (1973) 47 ALJR 586, 594 (Mason J) (the casebook heading’s ALJR report, not the slide’s
    ALR). Sundell: ONE rule (CRIT_fidelity_3 M8 and CRIT_round3 m3, adopted, option (b)): the court’s words exist in
    the course only in the editors’ note at [4.190] (‘said (at 327)’), so the court’s words, if QUOTED, take the AGLC
    ‘quoted in’ form (which cites the casebook and spends one of its two permitted footnotes); if PARAPHRASED without
    quotation marks (‘a promise to ensure performance already owed’), the report alone, (1955) 56 SR (NSW) 323, 327,
    no judge named, is the citation and is not a second-hand quotation fault. From v12 the paraphrase by report is the
    preferred form, a case then carrying the proposition and the casebook being a last resort (R-Z(3)); the ‘quoted in’
    form remains accepted, the court’s words existing in the course only in the note. The counted paragraph
    paraphrases. Pao On v Lau Yiu Long [1980] AC 614, 632, as the Board or ‘(Lord Scarman for the Board)’ (R-P), is the
    only citation for the third-party exception: v11’s alternative ‘Class 2B Slides, slide 9’ is withdrawn (R-Z(2)), and
    [4.300] is not cited, Pao On stating the point (R-Z(3)).
    Drops. VG: Sundell not met, or its words quoted and cited by report alone; Sundell named without its holding (a
    promise to ensure performance already owed is no consideration), the case cited bare as if its name were the reason
    (UPGRADES_r1 U6, the deciding passage being CB2B 114, [4.190], the court ‘said (at 327)’: ‘the only consideration
    which was suggested in argument as being given in exchange for the alleged promise was that the [defendant] would
    do what was necessary to ensure that the goods were delivered. But we are satisfied that the [defendant] was bound
    to do so under the original contract.’); limb (ii) applied to no fact (‘a request’ with no ‘Rocky’s’ or ‘27 February’:
    U20); the further information given without its direction (U3); the ‘more than’ limb missing; the order of clause and
    $10,000 asserted without ‘arguably’; the Pierre alternative not run on the rule’s second premise; the exceptions not
    named on AMPL’s sequence where the answer accepts it. Good: ‘no existing duty because Rocky had no prior
    contract’ bare. Fair: the rule stated generally with no reference to ‘a party to a pre-existing contract’; the practical
    benefit exception run without first asking whether there was a pre-existing contract. Poor: Wigan’s rule stated
    without ‘at least when’ as an absolute rule and applied to defeat the $10,000; Sundell attributed to a named judge or
    to Wigan; Wigan 595 cited as a general rule that two promises stand or fall together; an earlier binding promise
    asserted as narrated fact. Omitted.
    Marker comments. ‘Ask first whether there was a pre-existing contract at the moment Rocky promised, and to
    whom any duty was owed.’ ‘Keep Mason J’s hedge: ’at least when’.’ ‘Meet AMPL’s best authority, Sundell.’ ‘Answer
    with specificity to individual Ps: to whom was the promise made?’ ‘Glidewell LJ’s element (ii) is B’s reason to doubt
    whether A will complete.’

II.D Intention to create legal relations: cousins and a company; the letter of comfort point with its direction
(‘weaker here’); the Pierre alternative re-weighed in a clause; binds Rocky likewise (5.0; C; floor 80)
[Ermogenous [25] to [26]; Banque Brussels 521 to 523]

    Rules at source. Ermogenous [25] and [26] (I.D), including ‘it was for the appellant to demonstrate that there was
    such a contract’ [CB3A 137 | [26]]; ‘no presumption should be made about the enforceability of agreements made in
    a social or domestic context … although the nature of the relationship between the parties and the context in which
    the agreement was made remain important considerations’ [CB3A 146 | [5.90] | editors’ note; stated via
    Ermogenous]; ‘it was made quite clear to both Spedley and ANI that the facility was subject to BBL obtaining a
    suitable letter of comfort or some other indication of commitment from ANI’ [CB3A 144 | between 521 and 523 |
    Rogers CJ (so the casebook prints him; ‘Comm D’ is outside knowledge and is not used: CRIT_fidelity_3 m3,
    adopted), Banque Brussels Lambert SA v Australian National Industries Ltd | first instance, NSW, 1989]; ‘If the
    statements are appropriately promissory in character, courts should enforce them when they are uttered in the
    course of business and there is no clear indication that they are not intended to be legally enforceable.’ [CB3A 144 |
    523 | Rogers CJ; S3A slide 14 misprints ‘no clear intention’]; ‘if the paragraph is read as follows: “it is our practice to
    ensure that Spedley is at all times in a position to repay all loans made to it by your Bank”, its promissory nature is
    much clearer. I see no relevant difference between that and saying that “we promise to ensure that” …’ [CB3A 146 |
    525 to 526 | Rogers CJ | the hedge ‘far more thorny’ kept; inner quotations never nested]; ‘The letter of comfort
    developed as an alternative to a guarantee or surety’ [CB3A 143 | 520 | Rogers CJ recounting]; S3A slide 13 (‘No
    invariable rule’); S3A slide 4 (the traditional presumptions, stated as traditional only); Todd v Nicol 78 (Mayo J, first
    instance; presumption language read with Ermogenous).
    Facts (deciding). ‘Rocky’s cousin’; ‘promise’; ‘to me directly’; ‘at AMPL’s office’; ‘I will do that’. Supporting: AMPL
‘owned and controlled’ by Pierre; on 27 February Rocky ‘asked’ and Pierre ‘agreed’ with no stated terms; a
company’s ‘$1,150,000’ loan; ten months of check-ins.
Core. (1) No presumption either way ([26]); the onus on Rocky as the party alleging the contract; the relationship
(‘cousins’) a factor weighed against the objective features (‘promise’, a fixed sum ‘to me directly’, ‘at AMPL’s office’
in the course of a company’s loan). (2) AMPL’s best point met in a clause whose shape is claim, then the point that
helps AMPL, then the answer (CRIT_round3 M14, adopted; v3’s ‘the analogy fails: … yet …’ announced failure before
the point that helps AMPL and read as muddled): the point against intention is that the undertaking resembles a
LETTER OF COMFORT (the course term, CB3A 143 [5.70], S3A slide 13; the counted form is ‘Against intention, the
undertaking resembles a letter of comfort’; v6’s ‘AMPL would call the undertaking a letter of comfort’ attributed to
AMPL an argument that defeats its own call, AMPL having ‘called for payment of the Loan under the personal
guarantee provided by Rocky’, and a marker could write ‘Answer with specificity to individual Ps’, so the point is
now put without a party: CRIT_round6 m5, adopted at no cost (nine words for nine); a drafter who attributes it to
AMPL loses nothing where the one-bargain clause (‘Rocky being bound likewise’) follows, which answers the
attribution, and ‘liken the undertaking to a letter of comfort’ is equally acceptable; v4’s ‘a cousin’s comfort’ remains
withdrawn as a coined label: CRIT_fidelity_4 Finding 3 and CRIT_round4 m7); in Banque Brussels the letter
conditioned the facility (‘the facility was subject to BBL obtaining a suitable letter of comfort’; the counted form says
‘conditioned’, ‘preceded’ being an inference from ‘subject to … obtaining’ that the extract does not state in terms,
CB3A 144 between [521] and [523]: CRIT_fidelity_6 Finding 3, adopted, −2), a mark of commercial seriousness
WEAKER here, not absent, AND THE REASON FOR ‘WEAKER’ STATED (the difference named, not only its direction:
in Banque Brussels ‘the facility was subject to BBL obtaining a suitable letter of comfort’ (CB3A 144, between 521
and 523), so the comfort was a condition of the lending, whereas here ‘the $1,150,000 was advanced by AMPL on
the same day’, 27 February, before the 8 April undertaking; the v11 form adds ‘, AMPL having already advanced’
after ‘weaker here’, +4: UPGRADES_r1 U28, core (2) having required ‘the difference stated with its direction’ while
the counted form gave the direction only) (Rocky’s undertaking arguably also preceded the signing, ‘before it was
signed’, the order and which clause being ‘arguably’ each way under R-M (CRIT_fidelity_9 Finding 7; R-U(6)), and
was ‘expected’ by Pierre; ‘absent’ overstated the difference: CRIT_round4 m7, adopted; the difference stated with its
direction: CRIT_harsh_2 F8); yet the undertaking was priced (‘$10,000 … to me directly’) and given ‘in the course of
business’ at a company’s office, which supplies what the timing lacks. (3) A conclusion on the AMPL alternative and,
in a clause, whether it holds if Pierre promised, RE-WEIGHED and not merely named: if Pierre promised personally
the relationship (‘cousins’) bears directly on the promisor and ‘at AMPL’s office’ bears less, so the clause must say
what still carries the finding, which is the price (‘$10,000 … to me directly’); the counted form is ‘the price
outweighing cousinhood even if Pierre promised’ (CRIT_round5 M2, adopted under R-S(2): v5’s ‘against either’
named the alternative in two words and re-weighed nothing, the shape his marker graded Good with ‘Answer with
specificity to individual Ps’); and the consistency clause that the same finding binds Rocky to his undertaking (T3;
the former II.H5 (v2) carried here; the counted form from v8 is the absolute clause ‘Rocky being bound likewise’, his
construction (VOICE.md 2.7, example 42), replacing v7’s ‘and binds Rocky likewise’, whose grammatical subject was
‘Intention’, which binds no one (CRIT_round7 m4, adopted at no cost; ‘and the finding binds Rocky likewise’ or ‘the
same finding binding Rocky’, +2, are equally acceptable: Part 9 item 38); credit: the onus then on AMPL as the party
asserting it, Ermogenous [26]). Rocky’s ‘I will do that’, given for a price, being more plainly promissory than the
‘practice to ensure’ paragraph Rogers CJ held promissory (v3’s core (3)) is CREDIT (moved to fit the count; the
counted paragraph does not carry it: CRIT_round3 M10).
Credit. The ‘practice to ensure’ comparison (525 to 526); Banque Brussels 523 for what is ‘appropriately
promissory’; the ten months of check-ins; Rogers CJ’s 520 (recounting, not finding); Proscour (‘It is not a full-sized
guaranty … Refusal of such performance opens a case for damages’, a commentator’s account of French law called
‘refreshingly honest and sensible’ at 521, the [521] marker preceding that sentence: CRIT_fidelity_3 m12, adopted),
used only to frame the difference between a guarantee and an undertaking to ensure, never as the Australian
remedies rule; S3A slide 13’s ‘a guarantee enforceable as a matter of contract law’; the content of the undertaking
being certain on either reading (II.E, II.I2).
Strength and tilt. Moderate to strong if AMPL promised, a disclosed departure from ISSUES_SETTLED’s ‘strong’
(S-33; header C6) with its reason stated here from the source page as R-R(9) requires (CRIT_round4 m12):
Ermogenous [25] makes the inquiry ‘an objective assessment of the state of affairs between the parties’ that ‘may
take account of the subject-matter of the agreement, the status of the parties to it, their relationship to one another,
and other surrounding circumstances’, and [26] puts the onus on the party alleging the contract; the casebook’s own
note at [5.90] says ‘the nature of the relationship between the parties and the context in which the agreement was
made remain important considerations’; Banque Brussels 523 is a first-instance NSW judgment about a commercial
letter between companies, not a cousin’s oral undertaking. No High Court authority in the materials decides a
family-and-company weighing on these facts, so a well-reasoned contrary conclusion that applies [25] and [26] to the
onus, the relationship, the unpriced request of 27 February and ‘expected’ is a reasoned application of the governing
authority, not law wrong, and R-E’s ‘strong’ consequence (Poor) would misgrade it; it is capped at Very Good
(CRIT_complete_bias_2 B-4; Part 9 item 6). A contrary conclusion resting on a family presumption is Poor and D6.
Moderate to strong if Pierre promised, likewise.
Consistency (T3). Rocky cannot argue family honour to escape the undertaking while claiming the $10,000.
Citation. Ermogenous [25]–[26] (Gaudron, McHugh, Hayne and Callinan JJ); Banque Brussels Lambert SA v
Australian National Industries Ltd (1989) 21 NSWLR 502, 521–3 or 523 (Rogers CJ), 525–6 for the ‘practice to
ensure’ passage.
Drops. VG: the Banque Brussels difference named without its direction, or ‘weaker here’ stated without its reason
(AMPL having already advanced, or an equivalent fact: U28), or the price not used as the answer, or the counter put
    in a self-contradicting shape (‘the analogy fails: [the point that helps AMPL]’); onus missing; the Pierre alternative
    not addressed; the consistency clause (‘Rocky being bound likewise’) missing. Good: ‘commercial context, so
    intention’ with presumption language; a cross-reference without re-weighing for Pierre, including ‘against either’ or
    ‘against AMPL or Pierre’ with no clause saying what carries the finding if Pierre promised (CRIT_round5 M2). Fair:
    traditional presumptions applied as the test. Poor: ‘cousins are presumed not to intend legal relations’; Rocky’s
    undertaking held a mere honourable assurance while the $10,000 is enforced. Omitted. Credit wrong: Proscour
    stated as Australian law on damages (Fair); Banque Brussels cited as appellate (D3).
    Marker comments. ‘Ermogenous: no presumption; assess the objective features and the onus.’ ‘Name the
    difference from Banque Brussels and say which way it cuts.’ ‘Answer with specificity to individual Ps as required:
    AMPL and Pierre.’

II.E Certainty: ‘all payments … promptly’ measurable; ‘once Grace repays’ not illusory (2.5; L; floor 35) [Upper
Hunter 437 (Barwick CJ, quoting Lord Wright); Meehan 581 to 582]

    Rules at source. ‘So long as the language employed by the parties … is not “so obscure and so incapable of any
    definite or precise meaning that the court is unable to attribute to the parties any particular contractual intention”,
    the contract cannot be held to be void or uncertain or meaningless.’ [CB3B 161 | 437 | Barwick CJ, ‘to use Lord
    Wright’s words in G Scammell & Nephew Ltd v Ouston’, Upper Hunter | the inner quotation never nested; the
    quoted words are Lord Wright’s, so the footnote reads ‘(Barwick CJ, quoting Lord Wright in G Scammell & Nephew
    Ltd v Ouston)’ and R8’s second-hand list includes him; Mason J RECITES the same words at 587 as the respondents’
    first proposition (‘The respondents’ case rests on three propositions: (1) a contract which is expressed in language
    “so obscure …” is void for uncertainty’), which he does not dispute, so 587 is a recited submission and not a second
    adoption; Upper Hunter 437 is the adoption and the citation (CRIT_fidelity_4 Finding 4, adopted; CRIT_fidelity_3
    m8)]; contrast Mobil: ‘find a way’ ‘is simply too vague and uncertain to be capable of giving rise to contractual
    obligation’ [CB1A 58 | 499 | the Court] (credit); ‘The fact that the condition is one whose performance [582] lies
    wholly or partly within the power of one of the parties to the contract does not mean that there is no binding
    contract once the condition is fulfilled.’ [CB3B 184 | 581 to 582 | Gibbs CJ, Meehan].
    Facts (deciding). ‘all payments … promptly’; ‘once Grace repays the Loan’. Supporting: ‘$10,000’; ‘to me directly’.
    Core. One or two sentences: the criterion (a term fails only if ‘so obscure’ that no contractual intention can be
    attributed to it) applied to ‘all payments … promptly’, which takes its content from the Loan Agreement’s own
    timetable (monthly interest; principal at the expiry of 12 months); and AMPL’s promise not illusory, its condition
    depending on a third party’s act, Grace’s repayment (credit: ‘once Grace repays the Loan’ quoted here; AMPL has no
    discretion once it is met). The counted form is two sentences, each carrying its own footnote (Upper Hunter 437 on
    the first; Meehan 581 to 582 on the second), the second a ten-word element sub-verdict with its reason in an
    absolute clause (‘AMPL’s promise is not illusory, its condition depending on Grace’), which is his form under R1; v5’s
    single sentence carried one marker for two sources (CRIT_fidelity_5 Finding 5).
    Credit. Mobil compared (similar: ‘find a way’ and ‘ensure’ both leave the means to the promisor; different: Mobil’s
    result was itself undefined, whereas Rocky’s result is defined by the timetable); the contrast with I.E (the same
    doctrine, opposite result, the discretion there lying with the promisor); any dispute about the 12 months is
    construction, not certainty (II.G); the sum, payee and trigger stated.
    Strength. Strong; a contrary conclusion is Poor.
    Drops. VG: the criterion absent (a bare ‘capable of meaning’); AMPL’s promise not addressed for illusoriness (the
    second sentence, the first-demotion item in Part 8, mirrored here: CRIT_round6 m8). Good: bare. Fair: the condition
    called uncertain. Poor: ‘ensure’ held void for vagueness; AMPL’s promise held illusory because Grace might never
    repay. Omitted.

II.F1 Privity: Rocky the promisee; ‘the clause’ both ways with the reason (1.0; L; floor 38) [Coulls 494 to 495
(Windeyer J dissenting) alone; never a slide (R-Z(2)) or [11.10] (R-Z(3); R-U(1)); Coulls 487 is not the citation]

    Rules at source. The two limbs (I.F2): Windeyer J at 494 (‘only those who are parties to a contract can sue upon it’)
    and 495 (‘a plaintiff who sues on a promise must show a consideration for it provided by him’), dissenting on
    construction, alone: S4A slide 2 states only the party limb, and [11.10] is an editors’ note for a rule a course case
    states, so neither is the citation (R-Z(2), (3); R-U(1); CRIT_fidelity_9 Finding 1, v9’s ‘or S4A slide 2, or [11.10]’
    withdrawn); Coulls 487 decides that signature does not make one a party and that there was no express or implied
    promise to the wife, and does not state the two-limb rule, so it is not the citation for ‘promisee for consideration, not
    barred’ (CRIT_fidelity_3 m6 and CRIT_round3 m6, adopted); example 1: ‘If A and B enter into a contract under which
    A promises B (for consideration) to pay $1,000 to C, and A fails to honour this promise, C has no right to sue A to
    recover the money.’ [CB4 327 | [11.10] | editors]; Coulls 487; Trident [14] (Mason CJ and Wilson J: ‘a stranger to the
    consideration cannot maintain an action at law upon it’; by report, no pin; [15] asks whether the two rules are one)
    (credit; CRIT_complete_bias_2 A-7 adopted).
    Facts (deciding). ‘to me directly’; ‘the clause’. Supporting: ‘Pierre agreed and the clause was included in the Loan
    Agreement before it was signed by Grace and AMPL.’
    Core. One sentence: Rocky is the promisee and named payee (‘to me directly’) of an oral promise made to him for
    his own consideration, so he is a party who gave consideration and privity does not bar the oral claim; and, in a
    clause, both readings of ‘the clause’ (CRIT_harsh_2 F7, adopted): it most naturally means the guarantee clause
    named before it (the reason stated in a few words: the definite article refers back to the only clause previously
    named; v10’s ‘just named’ pointed at a place in the answer rather than in the problem, an answer-external deictic a
    cold reader could not resolve, and is from v11 an R1 lapse; the v11 form is ‘the guarantee clause named before it’,
    +1: UPGRADES_r1 U29; ‘the guarantee clause the problem names first’ is equally Excellent; v3’s floor gave no
    reason, which the harsh critic graded VG: CRIT_round3 table 1.1), and even a written $10,000 term leaves him the
    oral promise made to him (accepted bare in the counted form; its reason, that Rocky is party to neither document
    and the materials state no rule displacing an oral promise by a later written record (gap G8), is credit, ‘he being
    party to neither document’ (+6) where words allow: CRIT_round4 m8, disclosed; credit also: example 1 named,
    Rocky being then a non-party to the Loan Agreement; the order of clause and $10,000 weighed in terms).
    Credit. The gap disclosed if relied on (no rule in the materials on an oral promise and a later written record); Grace
    is not a party to the $10,000 bargain; the order of the clause and the $10,000 weighed in terms, marked ‘arguably’
    (R-M). From v11 (UPGRADES_r1 U17, credit only; its proposed weight change REJECTED, the weights being R-C
    calibrated): the written-term reading’s reason, ‘he being party to neither document’ (+6), stays credit, example 1 at
    [11.10] (‘C has no right to sue A to recover the money’, CB4 327) supporting it.
    Strength and tilt. Either reading of ‘the clause’ is Excellent if both are run, the preferred reading carries its reason
    (the definite article for the guarantee reading; the order of clause and $10,000, marked ‘arguably’, for the written-
    term reading), and the result is shown unchanged; weighing the other reading’s reason in terms is credit
    (CRIT_round10 M1) (CRIT_complete_bias_2 B-5, adopted: the ambiguity is textual, not legal); one reading only is
    Good.
    Drops. VG: the criterion (party plus consideration) absent; ‘most naturally’ with no reason; Coulls 487 cited for the
    two-limb rule (a citation fault); [11.10] cited for the rule where Coulls 494 to 495 states it (a citation fault: R-Z(3); R-
    U(1)); a slide cited for either limb or for the rule is graded at R1 and R8 under R-Z(5), not here (v11’s slide-2
    citation-fault line is withdrawn). Good: bare; one reading only. Fair: the written-term reading adopted and the claim
    said to fail on privity with the oral contract ignored. Poor: Rocky treated as a third-party beneficiary of the Loan
    Agreement for the $10,000. Omitted.
    Marker comment. ‘Identify the planted ambiguity in ’the clause’ and run both readings.’

II.F2 Privity: who promised, AMPL through Pierre or Pierre personally (4.0; K; floor 95) [the doctrine of
agency, one source per marker (R-R(3)): a case first under R-Z(3), Port Jackson 240 (Barwick CJ) for the
criterion stated in Lord Reid’s form; the casebook [11.70] only as a last resort, per R-A(4) as amended by R-
X(1); either suffices for the criterion; Ermogenous [24] credit]

    Issue. Whether the $10,000 promise (and so Rocky’s counterparty, and the promisee of his undertaking) is AMPL or
    Pierre.
    Rules at source. The doctrine of agency: S4A slide 16 (as at I.F1; a locator only, never cited: R-Z(2)); the course’s
    statement of what must be shown: the principal ‘expressly or impliedly consented to the agent acting on the
    principal’s behalf so as to effect the principal’s relations with third parties’ and, ‘with respect to the particular
    transaction under consideration, the agent was purporting to act on behalf of the principal and not solely on the
    agent’s own behalf. Ratification refers to the principal’s adoption or confirmation of a contract made without prior
    authorisation.’ [CB4 336 | [11.70] | editors’ note; cited from v12 as at I.F1: a case first, Port Jackson 240 (Barwick
    CJ) for the elements stated in Lord Reid’s form (‘contracted for the stevedore’s protection as well as for his own’;
    authority ‘antecedently or by ratification’), Trident (Brennan J or Deane J) by report only for the bare route, The New
    York Star only for authority; the casebook [11.70] a last resort (R-Z(3)), at most twice in the answer, never stacked
    with a case for the same proposition (R-R(3)); no slide (R-Z(2))]; ‘To be a legally enforceable duty there must, of
    course, be identifiable parties to the arrangement’ [CB3A 137 | [24] | Ermogenous] (credit); Windeyer J’s ‘the
    company (by its agent O’Neil) promised’ [CB4 332 | 492 | dissenting on construction] (illustration only). No court in
    the set states a rule of ratification in terms (O-7): ratification is not used to supply the purporting element.
    Facts (deciding). ‘owned and controlled’; ‘you’; ‘at AMPL’s office’; ‘payments under the Loan Agreement’; ‘the
    personal guarantee provided by Rocky’ (AMPL’s later call, quoted in the narrator’s words, the inference that it was a
    call on the undertaking marked ‘arguably’: R-M; CRIT_round3 M8, adopted). Supporting (credit): ‘Pierre agreed to
    provide the money to Grace and the $1,150,000 was advanced by AMPL’ (the narrator’s own 27 February usage,
    Pierre agreeing and AMPL advancing: moved from core to credit to fit the count); the $10,000 a payment outside the
    loan; ‘a cousin’ at ‘you’; on the guarantee reading of ‘the clause’ the $10,000 is nowhere in AMPL’s document.
    Core. (1) The issue framed on the doctrine of agency, and the two factual questions the course’s statement requires
    put in the answer’s own words as ‘whether’ clauses, never as a direct question with a question mark (his Part A has
    none; the counted form: ‘turns on agency: whether AMPL consented to Pierre acting on its behalf, and whether
    Pierre purported to act for AMPL or solely for himself’: CRIT_round4 m9, adopted), with one footnote for the
    criterion (Port Jackson 240 where the clause states it in Lord Reid’s form, as ‘whether Pierre contracted for AMPL as
    well as, or instead of, himself, with its authority’; [11.70] as the last resort where it states the editors’ form: R-Z(3)).
    (2) Consent answered on ‘owned and controlled’ (the counted form: ‘AMPL being ’owned and controlled’ by Pierre
    supplies consent’; credit: the problem’s own 27 February usage, ‘Pierre agreed’ preceding an advance ‘by AMPL’).
    ‘Purporting is contested’ is accepted as the counted opening of (3) (CRIT_round4 m9, second limb: ‘Whether Pierre
    so purported is contested’ (+2) is equally acceptable); the counted form now joins (2) and the opening of (3) in one
    sentence (‘AMPL being ’owned and controlled’ by Pierre supplies consent; purporting is contested: ‘if you also
    promise’ was put to ‘you’‘), because the removal of the credit fact left a ten-word sentence beside a nine-word one,
    which R1 forbids (no two under-12 sentences adjacent). (3) Purporting contested with the other side’s best fact met:
    for an AMPL conclusion, ’if you also promise’ put to ‘you’ (the deciding fact for Pierre; that the $10,000 was a
    payment outside the loan is a supporting fact, credit, as this unit’s Facts list has always rated it, and v5’s counted
    sentence carried it as ‘for a payment outside the loan’ against R-L(2); it was removed in v6, a disclosed move under
R-S(4) named in Part 8, and a drafter who adds it is graded the same), met with ‘at AMPL’s office’, ‘payments under
the Loan Agreement’ and AMPL’s later call under ‘the personal guarantee provided by Rocky’, ‘arguably’ his
undertaking (never ‘AMPL called on the undertaking’ as fact: R-M); for a Pierre conclusion, those facts met with
‘you’, the cousinhood and, on the guarantee reading, the absence of the $10,000 from AMPL’s document. The three
facts may be weighed inside one or two sentences, his stacked-facts form (VOICE.md 2.3, examples 14 and 15), or as
‘Firstly … Secondly, however … Thirdly …’ sentences; neither is required (R1 as restated; CRIT_round3 m9, adopted
in part). (4) A conclusion in the open-point tier (‘On balance … likely …, because …, though …’) whose ‘because’ is
Pierre’s dealing throughout about AMPL’s loan (v3’s ‘AMPL asserted its other side’ deleted as cryptic and as
restating the R-M inference: CRIT_round3 M8); the other party pleaded in the alternative, which the trailing clause
‘though ’you’ keeps Pierre arguable’ does (the counted form from v8; v7’s ‘keeps Pierre arguable in the alternative’
carried three words the clause did not need, removed under R-R(7) to fund CRIT_round7 M1: Part 8; the words ‘in
the alternative’ are not required and a drafter who writes them loses nothing) (credit: the consequence stated here
that whoever promised the $10,000 is the person to whom Rocky’s undertaking ran, which II.I1 draws in any event).
(5) The further information that decides it, with its direction, in the trailing clause (from v11 CORE, a K unit carrying
none being the contradiction five of six Stage-5 round-1 markers graded at R6: UPGRADES_r1 U3): what Pierre said
about whom he spoke for, and which way it cuts; the v11 form adds ‘, decisively so if he spoke for himself’ after
‘though ’you’ keeps Pierre arguable’ (+7); a form naming AMPL’s later adoption of the $10,000 promise, with its
direction, is equally Excellent.
Credit. Ratification named and set aside (it concerns a contract ‘made without prior authorisation’, and prior
authorisation is not the difficulty); in Port Jackson the carrier’s antecedent authority to contract for the stevedore
was found as a fact on the evidence of their course of dealing (OFF Port Jackson, Barwick CJ [2] ‘(at p236)’: the
stevedore ‘had for years acted as stevedore in the discharge of the carrier’s ships’ and ‘was aware of the use by the
carrier of a form of bill of lading’ including the Himalaya clause; [21] ‘(at p241)’: the appellate Court ‘having
reviewed the evidence’ found the carrier ‘was acting with the appellant’s authority’, ‘i.e. with its antecedent
authority’, which Barwick CJ held ‘a correct conclusion of fact’; the trial judge had found ratification, [19]), and in
The New York Star the Board records that the trial judge found agency ‘only by ratification’ while the Court of
Appeal ‘found that the necessary agency was directly established by the evidence, so that reliance on ratification
was not necessary’ (OFF New York Star [7] ‘(at p303)’ for Sheppard J’s finding that ‘the necessary agency was
established only by ratification’, and [8] ‘(at p303)’ for the Court of Appeal’s; v8 labelled both [8]: CRIT_fidelity_8
Finding 2); the stevedore’s knowledge of the offer and its reliance on it go to acceptance and consideration (OFF
Port Jackson, Mason and Jacobs JJ [12] ‘(at p274)’: ‘the evident probability of the appellant acting in reliance on the
shipper’s promise or offer when he discharges the goods so long as he has knowledge of the existence of that
promise or offer’), and, in the authority finding Mason and Jacobs JJ adopted from Glass JA (OFF Port Jackson [5] ‘(at
p271)’: the stevedore ‘proved that it was familiar with the terms of the bill of lading in general and with the clauses
purporting to exempt independent contractors in particular’, and claims had been ‘rejected in reliance upon the
exemption clauses’, from which there was ‘a clear implication’ that the carrier acted ‘as agent with the authority of
its principal the stevedore’; ‘On this point we need do no more than state our agreement with what Glass J.A. has
said’), the stevedore’s familiarity with the clauses and its reliance on them in rejecting earlier claims were part of
the course of dealing from which authority was implied (CRIT_fidelity_9 Finding 3; R-U(6): v9’s ‘not to authority’ was
too wide and is withdrawn); so the cases support inferring Pierre’s authority from AMPL’s ownership and control, as
a course of dealing supported it there, but neither addresses ‘purporting’ (v7’s gloss, ‘infer authority from the
principal’s knowledge of and reliance on the clauses’, was imprecise rather than wrong, naming the familiarity and
reliance without the course of dealing they formed part of, and is replaced by the statement above: CRIT_fidelity_7
Finding 2 and CRIT_fidelity_9 Finding 3, adopted as corrections under R-T(3) and R-U(6); every span above checked
verbatim against the saved official texts, which are credit, never cited for a course-stated proposition, and never
quoted in the answer); Ermogenous [24]; intention re-weighed if Pierre promised (II.D); a second further-information
item beyond core (5) (AMPL’s later adoption of the $10,000 promise, or what Pierre said, whichever core (5) does
not use), with its direction.
Strength and tilt. AMPL likely the promisor (moderate, S-7); Pierre in the alternative. Either conclusion can be
Excellent if both questions are answered from the stated facts, the other side’s best fact is met, the alternative is
pleaded, and the consequences for II.C2, II.D, II.I1, II.I3 and II.I4 are drawn conditionally on the conclusion (T11).
Citation. For the two elements, from v12 a case first (R-Z(3)): Port Jackson Stevedoring Pty Ltd v Salmond &
Spraggon (Aust) Pty Ltd (1978) 139 CLR 231, 240 (Barwick CJ), where the sentence states the criterion in Lord
Reid’s form (requisites (2) and (3), OFF Port Jackson [18] ‘(at p240)’); it does not count toward R-H’s cap of three,
the proposition being course-stated at [11.70]; Trident General Insurance Co Ltd v McNiece Bros Pty Ltd (1988) 165
CLR 107 (Brennan J), or (Deane J), by report, no pin (R-Y(1)), only for the bare agency route. The casebook as the
last resort, in the R-A(4) form as amended by R-X(1), Contract: Cases and Materials [11.70] ALONE (at most twice in
the answer, counting I.F1; the v11 counted fn 35 read ‘Robertson and Paterson (n 13) [11.70]’; v10’s ‘Paterson and
Robertson’ order is withdrawn), accepted without deduction where the sentence states the editors’ own ‘purporting
… not solely’ form, which no case states in those words. No slide is ever cited (R-Z(2)): v11’s acceptance of ‘Class 4A
Slides, slide 16’ for the bare route is withdrawn, and a slide citation is graded at R1 and R8 under R-Z(5). A footnote
stacking a case and [11.70] for the same proposition is over-citation under R8 (CRIT_fidelity_4 Finding 2;
CRIT_round4 M10; R-R(3)). An answer that states the two elements as law with no source is Good on the citation
item; one that footnotes the editors by name outside R-A(4) is a D1 fault.
Drops. VG: one question not answered from the facts; the alternative not pleaded; the other side’s best fact not met;
the further information absent or given without its direction (core (5): U3); ‘AMPL called on the undertaking’ stated
as fact (D4 also). Good: ‘Pierre acted for AMPL’ bare; the elements stated as law with no source; no method and no
     lean (§2). Good also: ratification said to settle that AMPL promised (ratification is a course concept at [11.70], so
     using it as the decisive reason on a unit where either conclusion can be Excellent is a wrong decisive reason, not law
     wrong: CRIT_round4 m13; R-R(10); v4’s Poor exceeded R-E). Fair: agency assumed without the questions; corporate
     law beyond the course applied. Poor: ‘AMPL’ and ‘Pierre’ treated as interchangeable throughout. Omitted.
     Marker comments. ‘Answer with specificity to individual Ps as required: AMPL and Pierre.’ ‘Ask both questions the
     doctrine of agency requires, from the facts.’

II.G The 12 months (5.0; C; floor 76) [Brambles 163 to 164 and 191; for the objective reading eg Brambles 168
or Ermogenous [25]]

Why it is here (R-Q). ‘repayable at the expiry of 12 months’ states no start; it decides (i) whether AMPL’s 9 March 2025
call was premature, (ii) whether Rocky’s undertaking was broken on 27 February 2025 (breach exposure, which the
answer does not argue and never frames as outside any scope: R-Z(1), (4)), (iii) when the $10,000 condition can be met. It
does not decide Rocky’s claim against Grace (privity defeats it on either reading), which is why it sits in Part II after F,
with Part I carrying only the one-clause cross-reference at I.F2 (the 5 March demand assumed the earlier date). The
reader of II.I4’s ‘turns on the 12 months (G)’ is then in the same Part (CRIT_round3 M9, adopted).

     Issue. Whether ‘the expiry of 12 months’ ran from the advance of 27 February 2024 or the agreement of 8 April
     2024: a question of construction on which no reading or slide states when such a period begins (gap disclosed: Z9),
     and of construction, not uncertainty (Meehan 578: ‘It is only if the court is unable to put any definite meaning on the
     contract that it can be said to be uncertain’; the former I.E2 point, now carried here).
     Rules at source.
           Objective meaning: Ermogenous [25] (I.D; ‘in the same sense as it is used in other contractual contexts’) (eg).
           Ambiguity and genesis: ‘if the language of a contract is ambiguous, evidence of surrounding circumstances is
           admissible for the purposes of construing the contract … Where ambiguities exist, mutually known facts
           establishing the commercial purpose of the contract, the genesis of the transaction, the background and the
           context in which the parties are operating will be admissible … the shared beliefs of the parties as to their
           respective rights as they existed immediately before the contract was entered into are admissible’ [CB1B 82 |
           191 | Ipp AJA (Mason P agreeing on the contractual claim), Brambles | majority; conditional on ambiguity
           throughout; RG skip (register only); ‘Ipp AJA’ per R-K, the casebook heading ‘IPP JA’ accepted and not
           deducted]; ‘pre-contractual conduct is only admissible on questions of construction if the contract is ambiguous
           and if the pre-contractual conduct casts light on the genesis of the contract, its objective aim, or the meaning of
           any descriptive term’ [CB1B 73 | 163 | Heydon JA | unanimous by adoption (O-9) | RG skip]; ‘whatever might
           flow from the factual matrix, it cannot be resorted to unless the 12 July 1990 contract is ambiguous’ [CB1B 76 |
           167 | Heydon JA].
           Exclusion: post-contractual conduct ‘not admissible on the question of what a contract means’ [CB1B 73 | 164 |
           Heydon JA, unanimous by adoption]; ‘the actual opinions of the parties are irrelevant’ absent rectification or
           estoppel by convention [CB1B 76 | 168 | Heydon JA]. Contra, older (credit): Mayo J admits subsequent conduct
           ‘where meaning is in doubt … to explain, but not to contradict, the language used’ [CB3A 147 | 74 | Mayo J,
           Todd v Nicol | first instance, SA, 1957; the passage sits after the [74] marker and before any [75] marker:
           CRIT_fidelity_3 m11, adopted].
           Construction technique (credit): Lindley LJ declined to push ‘the doctrine of taking language most strongly
           against the person using it’ too far [CB1A 45 | 263 | Carlill | one of three concurring judgments]; ‘It is for the
           defendants to shew what it does mean’ is a step against a maker pleading vagueness, not a rule that the user of
           words bears a burden; Bowen LJ’s ‘two constructions … each of which is good sense’ [CB1A 46 | 267].
     Facts (deciding). ‘recorded’; ‘advanced by AMPL on the same day’; ‘the expiry of 12 months’; ‘appropriate’ (R-M:
     compliance in that period, no more). Supporting (credit): ‘4% per annum’; ‘Interest was to be paid by Grace
     monthly’; ‘I have until 8 April 2025’; AMPL’s argument that ‘the 12 months had begun then’; Rocky’s 13 February
     2025 email and 5 March demand; the drafter not stated.
     Core. (1) The issue framed as construction. (2) The method: objective meaning; surrounding circumstances
     admissible only if the words are ‘ambiguous’; later conduct excluded on meaning (Heydon JA 163 to 164 for the
     ambiguity gate and the exclusion of later conduct, and 168 (‘the actual opinions of the parties are irrelevant’) or
     Ermogenous [25] for the objective reading, the counted fn 36 reading ‘Brambles (n 19) 163–4, 168 (Heydon JA)’,
     because 163 to 164 do not state the objective limb (CRIT_fidelity_9 Finding 4; R-U(6)); Ipp AJA 191 in the footnote;
     the counted form from v8 is ‘later conduct excluded on meaning’, the grid line’s own words, replacing v7’s ‘later
     conduct never on meaning’, a clause whose predicate (‘admissible’) had to be supplied from the clause before it, the
     note-form ellipsis R1 names and said the model did not carry: CRIT_round7 m5, adopted at no cost; ‘later conduct
     never admissible on meaning’ (+1) is equally acceptable). (3) Ambiguity decided, not assumed, with the reason and
     the inference marked: the instrument ‘recorded’ as the ‘Loan’ the sum ‘advanced’ on 27 February, so it is ‘arguably’
     ambiguous (the counted form is the classification-then-proof colon §1 and R-R(2) permit: ‘The words are arguably
     ambiguous: the instrument ’recorded’ the sum ‘advanced’ on 27 February, that advance its ‘genesis’‘; ’recorded an
     earlier advance without a commencement’ is a derived fact: CRIT_fidelity_3 m1, adopted; the stated terms give no
     commencement, and a drafter who says so says ‘as stated’ or ‘as described’, never ‘as narrated’: R-R(1)); if
     ambiguous, the admissible ‘genesis’ is the advance ‘on the same day’; if held unambiguous, the circumstances are
     excluded and the result follows from the words (either path applies the method: CRIT_complete_bias_2 B-6, adopted;
     ‘ambiguity assumed’ and ‘ambiguity denied without reasons’ are graded alike, VG). (4) Both readings, the opposing
     one in a clause: for 27 February the term attaches to ‘the Loan’, the defined sum advanced that day; for 8 April the
terms (the rate, monthly interest, the 12 months) first APPEAR then (the counted form is ‘though the terms appear
first on 8 April’; v5’s ‘were fixed on 8 April’ stated a derived fact as fact, when the terms were first fixed being
nowhere stated and ‘recorded’ being the problem’s word: CRIT_fidelity_5 Finding 2 and CRIT_round5 m3, adopted;
‘were arguably first fixed’ (+1) and ‘were recorded on 8 April’ (0) are equally acceptable) (credit: Grace’s
‘appropriate’ payments from 8 April fit that timetable, though equivocally; the fuller 8 April clause with ‘appropriate’
(+6) was not fundable and stays credit: CRIT_round4 m10, disclosed). (5) A verdict in the open-point tier with a lean,
its reason, ‘though only slightly’ placed directly after the lean it qualifies (‘it likely ran from 27 February, though
only slightly, the known starting point being …’: CRIT_round4 m10’s dangle fixed at no cost; the marker is the
honest weak-strength marker and is not redundant with ‘likely’, which alone is the moderate tier: CRIT_round3 Part
3.4’s proposed saving rejected on the sheet’s own tier rule), and the further information that decides it (the Loan
Agreement’s words; credit: its drafter; whether interest ran from 27 February) in the same sentence (credit: the
readings cross-referred to II.H and II.I4, where they are applied). The v10 counted verdict was ‘On balance, it likely
ran from 27 February, though only slightly, on that genesis; the agreement’s words would decide it’, its reason
carried by reference to the sentence before (‘that advance its ’genesis’‘); v5’s verdict restated the genesis in a seven-
word clause and was shortened under R-S(4) (Part 8). Two v11 requirements on that verdict. (i) THE LEAN’S
REASON says why the date is preferred, not only that the genesis is admissible: Ipp AJA’s passage makes ’the
genesis of the transaction’ admissible where ‘ambiguities exist’ (CB1B 82, Brambles 191) and says nothing about
which date it favours, so admissibility is no reason for either date, and a lean whose only preceding sentence is the
counter has none (§1; UPGRADES_r1 U24); the reason is that the term attaches to ‘the Loan’, the sum ‘advanced’ on
27 February (eg ‘, the Loan having been ’advanced’ then’, about +6 where a draft lacks it; ‘that advance its ’genesis’’
followed by ‘on that genesis’ carries it only where the preceding sentence ties the term to the advance). (ii) THE
FURTHER INFORMATION WITH ITS DIRECTION: ‘unless the agreement’s words date the term from 8 April’ (+4
against ‘the agreement’s words would decide it’, which names the information and not its effect: UPGRADES_r1 U3;
R6); on an 8 April lean, the mirror (‘unless the agreement’s words date it from the advance’).
Credit. The three excluded statements named (Grace’s ‘I have until 8 April 2025’; AMPL’s ‘the 12 months had begun
then’; Rocky’s 2025 emails); the NSW preference for Heydon JA (Court of Appeal, unanimous by adoption) over Mayo

timing ‘does not itself require a conclusion that the agreement is void’); the parties’ shared beliefs immediately

8 April 2024 payment was interest in arrears. The Masters class 1 ‘restatement’ analogy must not be used for 27

Strength and tilt. Open (S-1); the best-reasoned answer a slight lean to 27 February 2024 (weak), with 8 April a
real alternative. Either conclusion can be Excellent if it applies the method, meets the other side’s best fact (‘the
Loan’ advanced on 27 February; or terms first fixed on 8 April with ‘appropriate’ payments from 8 April), states weak
strength honestly and carries both readings. ‘Finely balanced’ with no lean and no method is Good (the general rule
in §2); a date stated ‘clearly’ or with no strength is Very Good, its one grade at this unit (§2; R6; R-E: overstating and
understating carry the same cap); where the overstatement is carried into advice (‘so the call was valid; pay now’),
that is graded once, at II.I4, whose Strength line grades it Good (CRIT_round9 M3; R-U(4): v9 graded the same
answer Good in this unit’s Drops).
Consistency (T1). Rocky’s 5 March demand arguably presupposes 27 February (the I.F2 cross-reference, written
‘arguably’: CRIT_fidelity_4 Finding 1); his best defence to AMPL’s call presupposes 8 April. The answer decides the
point once and says so (credit: his own conduct is inadmissible on meaning but damaging in practice; he loses
nothing by preferring 8 April, his claim against Grace failing on privity either way).
Citation. Brambles Holdings Ltd v Bathurst City Council (2001) 53 NSWLR 153, 163–4, 168 (Heydon JA) and 191

II.H The condition ‘once Grace repays the Loan’: a condition of AMPL’s obligation with its reason, nothing
payable on 9 March 2025; whether Rocky’s own payment meets it, the commercial reading put and met; the
