185 met; Mobil 500 credit]

One unit (v3’s II.G1 and II.G2 merged to fit the count, R-L(1); the heading in the answer is ‘H. The condition’, ‘H. The
condition ’once Grace repays the Loan’’ or, from v11, ‘H. Condition precedent’ (R-X(6), three words for three, naming the
doctrine once for R7), never ‘the payment condition’, a sheet label: R7).

     Issue. Whether ‘once Grace repays the Loan’ is a condition of AMPL’s obligation rather than of formation, so that
     nothing was payable on 9 March 2025; and whether the condition can be met if Rocky, not Grace, pays AMPL (so
     that Rocky could pay $1,150,000 and lose the $10,000: T13).
     Rules at source. ‘Once the condition is fulfilled, within the time allowed by the contract for its fulfilment, the
     contract becomes completely binding.’ and ‘It is clear that the condition in Special Condition 1(b) is not a condition
     precedent to contract. Certain obligations under the contract attached immediately the contract was signed although
     the condition had not been fulfilled.’ [CB3B 184 | 582 | Gibbs CJ, Meehan]; time for fulfilment (credit): ‘In the
     absence of specification of time, the implication would be, I think, that it should take place within a reasonable time.’
     [CB2A 110 | 176 | Mahoney JA, Beaton | obiter]; Walsh J, ‘within a reasonable time’ [CB3B 189 | 643 | on the
     hypothesis ‘If, contrary to my view’]. Objective meaning of Rocky’s words: ‘The question becomes one of
     construction, of ascertaining the intention of the parties, and of applying it’ and ‘no narrow or pedantic approach is
     warranted, particularly in the case of commercial arrangements’ [CB3B 161 | 437 | Barwick CJ, Upper Hunter | both
     sentences sit after the [437] marker, so the pin is 437, not 436 to 437 (CRIT_fidelity_5 Finding 4, adopted); the
     course’s construction passage (CRIT_fidelity_3 m10, adopted in part), and the same page, read on, says ‘In the
     search for that intention, no narrow or pedantic approach is warranted, particularly in the case of commercial
     arrangements’, which leans toward a commercial reading of Rocky’s words (CRIT_round5 m5, noted; CRIT_round6
     m4; CRIT_round7 M1 and m15, adopted): from v8 the counted S2 puts and meets that commercial reading in terms,
     so Upper Hunter 437 is again the COUNTED citation on S2 (fn 39), the marker who checks it finding the very
     passage the sentence answers, and Ermogenous [25] (‘it is used in the same sense as it is used in other contractual
     contexts. It describes what it is that would objectively be conveyed by what was said or done, having regard to the
     circumstances in which those statements and actions happened’) [CB3A 137 | [25] | Gaudron, McHugh, Hayne and
     Callinan JJ | majority, ratio] is the accepted ALTERNATIVE, neither deducted (R-T(1))]. The duty to co-operate: ‘The
     McDivitts have a contractual duty to do everything necessary on their part to enable the plaintiff to have the benefit
     of the contract: Secured Income Real Estate (Aust) Ltd v St Martins Investments Pty Ltd (1979) 144 CLR 596 at 607
     per Mason J.’ [CB2A 109 | 185 | McHugh JA, Beaton, citing Mason J | the principle is Mason J’s in the High Court,
     applied by McHugh JA; McHugh JA was in the minority on the RESULT in Beaton, which does not touch the
     principle’s weight, so ‘a minority view’ misdescribes it and is never written (CRIT_fidelity_3 M6; CRIT_round3 M4,
     adopted); R8’s second-hand list includes ‘Mason J via Beaton 185’]; prevention (CREDIT from v6, R-S(4)): ‘There may
     also be a case (it is, perhaps, difficult to imagine one) in which the offeror may prevent the offeree from completing
     the act of acceptance … yet the offer will be held not to have been revoked.’ [CB1A 59 | 500 | the Court, Mobil |
     reasoning on an assumed (‘supposed’) unilateral offer about revocation, hedged by the Court itself (‘perhaps,
     difficult to imagine one’); it states no rule that a party who prevents a condition cannot rely on its non-fulfilment, so
     it is Rocky’s weaker argument and, if used, is used only as a disclosed analogy with its status in the text, never bare
     (CRIT_fidelity_3 m9, adopted); demoted from core to credit to fund the round-5 corrections (CRIT_round5 lens 3
     move 1; R-S(4)), the duty to co-operate being the course’s real counter]; implication: ‘only when it is clearly
     necessary [86] to introduce the term in order to make the contract operative according to the intention of the parties
     as indicated by the express terms’ [CB3A 151 | 85 to 86 | Mayo J, Todd v Nicol, citing Latham CJ in Scanlan’s New
     Neon | first instance, SA]; Mason J quoting Butts v O’Dwyer and ‘The consequence would be that he had an
     obligation to do all that was reasonable on his part to obtain that finance’ [CB3B 186 | 591 | Mason J (Wilson J
     agreeing at 597 to 598) | the hedge in the next sentences kept: ‘However, I have no need to decide the question’
     (CRIT_fidelity_2 m6, adopted)]; against: ‘it does not seem to me necessary, in order to give business efficacy to a
     contract, that a condition should be implied that the purchaser will make reasonable efforts to obtain finance’ [CB3B
     183 | 581 | Gibbs CJ | stated against Mason J (591) and Wilson J (598) in the extract; cited only with that status].
     Lindley LJ’s ‘It is for the defendants to shew what it does mean’ (Carlill 263) is not a rule placing a burden on Rocky
     as the user of the words; Mahoney JA’s ‘under no obligation to procure it’ (Beaton 177) does not touch a promisor’s
     own election.
     Facts (deciding). ‘once Grace repays the Loan’ (Rocky’s own words); ‘had not yet repaid the Loan’. Supporting:
     AMPL ‘called for payment of the Loan under the personal guarantee provided by Rocky’.
     Core. (1) One sentence in the L form: the criterion WITH ITS REASON (a condition of the obligation, not of
     formation, BECAUSE the bargain bound at once, B, so that obligations attached before the condition was fulfilled:
     ‘Certain obligations under the contract attached immediately the contract was signed although the condition had not
     been fulfilled’, 582; the doctrine NAMED once in the course’s term, condition precedent, in the heading or the text
     (Gibbs CJ at 582: ‘It is clear that the condition in Special Condition 1(b) is not a condition precedent to contract’ and
     ‘Whether the condition is described as a condition precedent to completion, or as a condition subsequent, seems
     largely a matter of words’, CB3B 184; R-X(4); the text form ‘a condition precedent to AMPL’s obligation, not to
     contract’ costs about +5 against v10’s ‘qualifies AMPL’s obligation, not formation’, and the heading ‘H. Condition
     precedent’ costs nothing, either sufficing); the v10 counted form was ‘The condition, the bargain binding at once,

     own moderate-to-strong classification with no reason, the shape §1 grades Good; CRIT_round4 m11’s second limb is
thereby funded), THE CONTRACT becoming ‘completely binding’ once it is fulfilled (Gibbs CJ’s subject at 582 is the

on 9 March 2025 Grace ‘had not yet repaid the Loan’, so nothing was payable and the claim is premature. (2) The
second question framed on the objective meaning of Rocky’s own words, ‘once Grace repays the Loan’, which name

adopted; R-T(1)): the opponent’s best construction argument is that a commercial term is not read narrowly or

by whomever; AMPL’s answer, now CORE and no longer credit, is the pricing point, that the $10,000 priced Grace’s
repayment, the outcome Rocky undertook to procure, not Rocky’s own payment after her default; the counted form

his’ is equally acceptable where words allow); a paragraph that writes ‘literally’ and never says why the literal
reading governs leaves the construction counter unput, the shape his marker graded ‘Further analysis is required as
your statement is conclusive’ (VG); the verdict proper sits at (5), after the counters; the counted footnote on this
sentence is Upper Hunter 437 (Barwick CJ), placed at the end of the sentence because 437 carries both the
construction test and the ‘no narrow or pedantic approach’ words the clause meets (CRIT_round7 m15), Ermogenous
[25] being equally acceptable (R-T(1)). (3) Rocky’s best argument put in a clause: the duty ‘to do everything

completion is CREDIT since v6 (R-S(4); Part 8), usable only with its status in the text (‘reasoning on an assumed
offer’). (4) AMPL’s answer in a clause, the DECISIVE REASON that meets the duty on its own terms (CRIT_round4
M9, adopted; R-O): nothing in the facts suggests that AMPL’s call on Rocky stops Grace repaying AMPL, so AMPL
has omitted nothing the duty ‘to do everything necessary’ requires (the counted form: ‘Yet nothing suggests AMPL’s

rather than a stated fact, which R-O licenses as a fact and not as guarantee law); a drafter who also runs Mobil

possibility that ’Grace repays the Loan’; S4 meets the call form, and S2’s pricing point is in substance the answer to

II.I AMPL’s call of 9 March 2025 (16.5; a further heading inside Part II; the Part III option was
withdrawn in v6, §5)

Why it is in the advice: it is the setter’s final fact and the reason advice is sought now; it is the other side of the bargain
Rocky sues on, so every argument on it is an argument on the $10,000; the 12 months (II.G) decides its timing. The
elements of the bargain are not re-run here; I is confined to the burden aspect, the content of what Rocky promised and
his assent to the written words, AMPL’s consideration, timing and consequences; intention and certainty of the
undertaking are carried by the II.D clause. The four paragraphs sit under the one heading ‘I. AMPL’s call’ (the Part III
option is withdrawn: §5; CRIT_round5 m2); they may open ‘Whether …’ or with a classification; II.I2’s counted opening
(‘Rocky promised what Pierre ’expected’, whereas …’) is a classification of the two contents and is not a conclusion first
(CRIT_round3 Part 3.5 noted the visible inconsistency; it is not a fault).

II.I1 The burden aspect; the clause alone; Rocky likely bound by his own undertaking, to AMPL only if Pierre
acted for it (4.0; C; floor 56) [Trident (Deane J) by report, judge named, no pin (R-Y(1), (2)), never a slide (R-
Z(2)); Empirnall 534 and 536; Coulls 487]

     Issue. Whether a clause in a contract between AMPL and Grace, described by the narrator as one ‘that Rocky
     personally guaranteed repayment of the Loan’, can by itself bind Rocky; and, if not, whether he is bound by his own
     undertaking, and to whom.
     Rules at source. ‘Nor can it impose an enforceable burden on a non-party.’ [S4A slide 2 | slide; a locator only, never
     cited: R-Z(2)]; ‘nor can it impose an enforceable burden on a non-party’ [CB4 327 | [11.10] | editors; ‘This chapter
     will focus on the benefit aspect of the rule’; a last resort only, never needed here: R-Z(3)]; the case statement of both
     aspects, the preferred source from v12 (R-Y(2)): ‘the general rule that only the parties to a contract are bound by,
     and entitled to enforce, its terms’ [Trident, course reading (R-Y(1)) | Deane J, his opening sentence | verified in the
     corpus’s Deane J segment | by report, judge named, no pin, the reading carrying no CLR page markers], and Deane
     J’s further statement, ‘a contract between two or more parties does not, of itself, directly confer rights or impose
     liabilities upon persons who are not parties to it’ [same | Deane J | verified likewise]; either may be quoted or
     paraphrased; Trident being a course reading, the footnote never counts toward the R-H cap of three and there is no
     cap of one Trident footnote (R-J and R-N superseded: R-Y(1)); ‘The objective theory of contract requires an external
     manifestation of assent to an offer.’ [CB1B 71 | 534 | McHugh JA, Empirnall]; signature NOT NECESSARY: ‘Eric does
     not sign contracts’ and ‘Since Empirnall has taken the benefit of the work with knowledge of the terms on which it
     was offered, an objective bystander would conclude that Empirnall had accepted the offer’ [CB1B 72 | 536 | McHugh
     JA, Empirnall | a party bound without signing]; signature NOT DECISIVE: ‘the mere fact that her signature appears
     does not make her a party; this is a question to be resolved upon a consideration of the written instrument itself’
     [CB4 331 | 487 | Taylor and Owen JJ, Coulls | signature not sufficient; whether it is necessary was not in issue, so
     487 is never cited for ‘not necessary’: CRIT_fidelity_3 m7, adopted].
     Facts (deciding). ‘that Rocky personally guaranteed repayment of the Loan’ (the narrator’s description); ‘I will do
    that’. Supporting: ‘signed by Grace and AMPL’; ‘the personal guarantee provided by Rocky’ (R-M).
    Core. (1) The burden aspect stated: a contract cannot impose an enforceable burden on a non-party, so ‘the clause
    as described’ (the narrator’s description is quoted or paraphrased at II.I2, whose unit it is: Appendix B; never ‘the
    clause states’ or ‘records’: D4) binds Rocky only if he manifested assent to it (Empirnall 534), the question for II.I2;
    the reason is not that Rocky did not sign, signature being ‘neither necessary nor decisive’ (not necessary: Empirnall
    536; not decisive: Coulls 487; T10), each footnoted to its own authority. (2) Rocky is LIKELY bound by his own ‘I will
    do that’, a promise in a bargain to which he was party (the hedge matching ‘bound to someone (moderate to strong)’:
    CRIT_round4 M4, adopted; the counted form is ‘His own ’I will do that’ likely binds him’); AMPL may enforce it only
    if Pierre accepted for AMPL (II.F2); if Pierre accepted for himself, AMPL is a non-party to the undertaking and can
    enforce only the clause, on II.I2’s assent and II.I3’s consideration, its entry into the Loan Agreement remaining
    arguable consideration on that alternative (S-13, moderate), so Rocky’s exposure to AMPL is then likely absent but
    arguable and is never stated as settled (CRIT_round9 M1; R-U(2): v9’s ‘has nothing to enforce’ stated as settled a
    consequence II.I3 leaves arguable).
    Credit. The T2 reconciliation in four words, ‘as to that clause only’, after ‘binds him only if he assented’ (Rocky’s
    assent, if found, makes him party to the guarantee promise alone, not promisee of Grace’s promise to repay, so I.F2’s
    ‘not a party’ and II.I2’s ‘likely assented’ do not contradict: CRIT_round5 m12, credit, +4, written where words
    allow); Rocky differs in kind from Mrs Coulls (named as promisor, not payee); (v12: v11’s credit ‘the gap disclosed
    (no extracted judgment develops the burden aspect …)’ is withdrawn, Trident being a course reading that states it
    (R-Y(1)) and a statement about what the readings extract being meta language (R-Z(1))); joint promisees on the
    undertaking (if given to Pierre and AMPL jointly, ‘an action to recover must have been brought by both promisees’:
    Coulls 486; weak for Rocky); the Loan Agreement’s text not narrated (the answer never asserts it ‘purports
    expressly’ to be between AMPL and Grace: D4). From v11 (UPGRADES_r1 U11, credit only): ‘as to that clause only’
    remains the first credit to write here; R10 accepts the T2 reconciliation without it only where I.F2’s joint-promisee
    clause states that no promise by Grace runs to Rocky, Taylor and Owen JJ framing two questions, ‘whether the
    testator’s wife was a party to the contract and whether, upon its true construction, the contract contains a promise
    by the company to pay to her’ (CB4 330, Coulls 486).
    Strength and tilt. The clause alone cannot bind Rocky (strong, S-8): ‘Rocky is bound because the clause is in the
    Loan Agreement’ is Poor with the D6 deduction; so is ‘Rocky is not bound to anyone because he never signed’. Bound
    to someone (moderate to strong); bound to AMPL (moderate, capped by the II.F2 conclusion and stated conditionally
    on it).
    Citation. Trident General Insurance Co Ltd v McNiece Bros Pty Ltd (1988) 165 CLR 107 (Deane J), by report with
    the judge named and no pin (R-Y(1), (2)): Trident is a Class 4A reading, so it is cited as any course case is, one
    source per marker, with no cap on the number of Trident footnotes; the reading carries no CLR page markers, so no
    pin can be verified, and the verifier’s lint ‘report citation with no pinpoint’ is an accepted, disclosed consequence,
    not a fault (R-Y(1); Part 9 item 7). v11’s alternative ‘Class 4A Slides, slide 2’ is withdrawn (R-Z(2)); a slide citation
    here is graded at R1 and R8 under R-Z(5). Residual risk, disclosed (CRIT_round7 m6, carried): the real marker reads
    the footnote, not this sheet, and may record a report citation with no pinpoint as a minor non-compliance at R8; the
    slide form that once removed that risk is barred, so the risk stands, and this sheet grades the no-pin form as no fault
    (Part 9 item 7); Empirnall 534 for assent; Empirnall 536 for ‘not necessary’; Coulls 487 only for ‘not decisive’ and
    ‘the written instrument itself’. One footnote marker per proposition inside the sentence (CRIT_round3 m2, adopted):
    the counted form reads ‘neither necessary[fn Empirnall 536] nor decisive[fn Coulls 487]’, two markers; v5’s single
    marker listing both sources is withdrawn (CRIT_fidelity_5 Finding 5, adopted).
    Drops. VG: the AMPL/Pierre dependency not drawn; signature-not-the-test absent (T10); Coulls 487 cited for ‘not
    necessary’ (a citation fault); ‘binds him’ unhedged (the tier). Good: ‘the clause cannot bind a non-party’ bare; ‘Rocky
    is bound by his oral promise’ bare; ‘a fortiori because he did not sign’ as the reason for the right result (right result,
    wrong reason, conclusive: the same grade as I.F2’s ‘Grace is right because Rocky did not sign’, because the same
    signature reasoning with the same right result carries the same grade whichever party it helps: CRIT_round4 M11;
    R-R(4); v4’s Poor was asymmetric). Fair: the benefit aspect only; the oral route missed and liability rested on the
    clause; the clause said to ‘state’ or ‘record’ a guarantee as a stated fact (D4). Poor: either D6 conclusion. Omitted.
    Marker comments. ‘Privity has two aspects: the burden aspect decides whether the clause alone binds Rocky;
    signature is not the test either way.’ ‘Answer with specificity to individual Ps: to whom was Rocky’s undertaking
    given?’

II.I2 Content: what Pierre ‘expected’ against the clause as described; assent to the written words on Empirnall
535 to 536; the benefit named; ‘attended’ as the knowledge fact, the inference marked; ‘provided by Rocky’
(3.5; C; floor 80) [Empirnall 535 to 536]

    Rules at source. ‘A more accurate statement is that where an offeree with a reasonable opportunity to reject the
    offer of goods or services takes the benefit of them under circumstances which indicate that they were to be paid for
    in accordance with the offer, it is open to the tribunal of fact to hold that the offer was accepted according to its
    terms. A useful analogy is to be found in the “ticket cases” where an offeree, who has or ought to have knowledge of
    the terms of a contract of carriage or bailment, is generally bound unless he raises objection’ [CB1B 72 | 535 |
    McHugh JA (Samuels JA agreeing; Kirby P to substantially the same effect), Empirnall | ratio. McHugh JA’s OWN
    criterion has three elements: an offeree with a reasonable opportunity to reject the offer of goods or services; who
    takes the benefit of them; under circumstances which indicate that they were to be paid for in accordance with the
    offer; and his holding at 536 rests on actual knowledge (‘taking the benefit of an offer with knowledge of its terms
    and knowledge of the offeror’s reliance’). The ‘has or ought to have knowledge of the terms’ words are the
ANALOGY (‘A useful analogy’), confined by its own words to ‘a contract of carriage or bailment’ (MacRobertson
Miller is the course’s ticket case, CB1A 48); v3 spliced the analogy’s words into the rule as its second limb and
deducted for omitting an ‘ought to have’ limb the rule does not contain: CRIT_fidelity_3 M2 and CRIT_round3 M3,
adopted]; ‘Since Empirnall has taken the benefit of the work with knowledge of the terms on which it was offered, an
objective bystander would conclude that Empirnall had accepted the offer on those terms and conditions’ [CB1B 72 |
536]; ‘The ultimate issue is whether a reasonable bystander would regard the conduct of the offeree, including his
silence, as signalling to the offeror that his offer has been accepted’ [535]; contra: ‘an offeror cannot erect a contract
between himself and the offeree by the device of stating that unless he hears from the offeree he will consider the
offeree bound’ [CB1B 71 | 534 | McHugh JA]; Felthouse 876 (uncommunicated assent); ‘In all the circumstances it
was for the defendant to make plain that its conduct amounted only to taking advantage of the proposal but not to
submitting to the disadvantages’ [CB1B 81 | 181 | Heydon JA, Brambles | his route] (credit). Lord Denning MR’s ‘last
shot’ (Butler 404 to 405) is his alone and concerns standard forms: a remote analogy at most.
Facts (deciding). ‘expected’ (and the narrator’s ‘ensure that Grace made all payments … promptly’); ‘that Rocky
personally guaranteed repayment of the Loan’ (the narrator’s description); ‘the personal guarantee provided by
Rocky’ (R-M); ‘attended’ (Rocky, Grace and Pierre ‘attended a meeting at AMPL’s office’, at which the Loan
Agreement was entered into and the clause ‘was included … before it was signed’: the attendance is the one fact in
the problem that goes to McHugh JA’s knowledge element, so it is DECIDING and is quoted in the counted
paragraph, ‘included at the meeting he ’attended’‘: CRIT_round4 M5, adopted; Appendix B). That the inclusion
happened at the meeting rather than in a draft before it is itself an inference from ’At the meeting … entered into’
and ‘included … before it was signed’; the counted form is now ‘included, arguably at the meeting he ’attended’’
(CRIT_fidelity_6 Finding 2 and CRIT_round6 m6, adopted, +1 from the slack), so that the inference that helps AMPL
is marked exactly as the inference that helps Rocky at II.C2 (‘The facts arguably place the clause after the
exchange’) is, §3’s one rule applied identically whichever party it helps. v6 carried the unmarked form ‘included at
the meeting he ’attended’’ and its changes file attributed that acceptance to words in R-S(6) that the ruling does not
contain; the acceptance was a reviser’s judgment call (CRIT_round5 m4, recorded rather than funded), disclosed as
such in SHEET_v7_changes.md and now moot. A drafter who writes the unmarked form is graded as the identity
table graded v6: Excellent, the inference being carried by the quoted word and by ‘likely’ in the verdict, though the
marked form is the counted one. Supporting: ‘included … before it was signed’; his presence at the inclusion is an
inference from attendance, so ‘in his presence’ is written ‘arguably’ or as ‘at the meeting he attended’
(CRIT_fidelity_3 m2, adopted); no objection shown; nothing narrated says Rocky read or was shown the clause’s
words; ten months of check-ins (admissible on formation of the oral bargain and on Rocky’s assent, inadmissible on
the meaning of ‘ensure’ or of the clause).
Core. (1) The two contents distinguished with the right speakers: what Rocky promised is what Pierre ‘expected’,
reported by the narrator as ensuring ‘all payments … promptly’ (interest included; procuring performance), whereas
the clause is described as one ‘that Rocky personally guaranteed repayment of the Loan’ (the defined principal; ‘on
default’ is guarantee law and is not said). (2) Assent to the written content decided on McHugh JA’s own criterion as
the source states it: whether, with ‘a reasonable opportunity to reject’, Rocky took the benefit of AMPL’s written
offer ‘with knowledge of its terms’, THE OFFER NAMED so that ‘its’ keeps its antecedent (McHugh JA at 536:
‘taking the benefit of an offer with knowledge of its terms’, CB1B 72; v10’s prescribed ‘took the benefit ’with
knowledge of its terms’’ detached ‘its’ from ‘an offer’; the v11 form ‘took the benefit of AMPL’s written offer ’with
knowledge of its terms’‘, +4: UPGRADES_r1 U13; knowledge of the offeror’s reliance, which describes Empirnall’s
facts and appears in no statement of the rule, and the disclosure of the analogy (Part 9 item 33) remain credit) (535
to 536; the ’under circumstances which indicate’ element may be stated in terms, credit); the BENEFIT NAMED,
because the criterion has a benefit element and a paragraph that names none is conclusive under §1(b): the $10,000
promise, taken as the other side of the clause recording the bargain he had just proposed (CRIT_round3 M3,
adopted: moved from credit to core); the KNOWLEDGE ELEMENT APPLIED TO A FACT, because the holding at 536
rests on ‘taking the benefit of an offer with knowledge of its terms’ and a paragraph that states the knowledge
element and names no fact going to it before concluding ‘assented’ is the conclusive shape his marker graded Good
(§1(b); CRIT_round4 M5, adopted: moved from credit to core): the fact is Rocky’s attendance at the meeting where
the clause was included, carried by the quoted word (‘included, arguably at the meeting he ’attended’‘, the counted
form from v7), the inference from attendance to knowledge being marked by ’arguably’, by the quotation and by
‘likely’ in the verdict, and a drafter who wants the knowledge inference itself explicit writes ‘arguably knew’; the
ticket-case analogy (‘has or ought to have knowledge’) is CREDIT, usable only with its confinement to ‘a contract of
carriage or bailment’ disclosed; for AMPL, the clause was included without objection shown (never ‘without narrated
objection’: R-R(1)), to record the bargain he had just proposed (credit), and the narrator’s ‘the personal guarantee
provided by Rocky’ proves that he provided one; for Rocky, that stipulation establishes neither the clause’s words
nor that he saw them (R-M) (credit: ‘provided’ arguably describes the oral undertaking; he was the offeror of ‘that’).
(3) A conclusion in the tier for the strength, with the further information named in the trailing clause (the clause’s
words; whether Rocky saw them; whether he objected) AND ITS DIRECTION (eg on a ‘likely assented’ verdict,
‘unless he never saw its words’; on a ‘likely did not assent’ verdict, ‘unless he saw and accepted its words’; a same-
count rewording of the counted trailing clause, which named the information without its effect: UPGRADES_r1 U3;
R6), which is also the answer to Rocky’s strongest counter, that the $10,000 was the price of his own completed oral
offer and not of the written clause (Kirby P at 170 by analogy: an additional term arising after acceptance ‘cannot
represent consideration’; credit in terms, met by the trailing clause because whether he assented to the written
content turns on the words he saw: CRIT_round4 M5).
Credit. Heydon JA’s ‘make plain’ (his route); the oral bargain complete before the clause was written (Kirby P at
170, by analogy: an additional term arising after acceptance ‘cannot represent consideration’); Proscour only to
    frame the difference; on either content Rocky is exposed if Grace defaults on a due date, the measure (a debt on call
    or damages) being remedies law, which the answer does not argue and never frames as outside any scope (R-Z(1),
    (4)); whether the clause was in the draft before the conversation; on the Pierre alternative the benefit taken was
    Pierre’s promise, not AMPL’s, which weakens assent to AMPL’s written content (S-18, weak to moderate;
    CRIT_round9 M1, credit: the counted ‘the $10,000 promise being his benefit’ names the promise whoever made it,
    and a drafter who states the weakening in a clause loses nothing).
    Strength and tilt. Assent to the written ‘personally guaranteed’ content moderate on the guarantee reading of ‘the
    clause’, weak to moderate on the other (S-9). Either conclusion can be Excellent if it applies McHugh JA’s criterion
    with the benefit named, meets the other side’s best fact (including ‘provided by Rocky’ on the R-M line), and names
    the further information; no method and no lean is Good (§2). An answer that treats ‘provided by Rocky’ as stipulated
    evidence of assent is eligible for Excellent provided it says what the stipulation does and does not establish (not the
    clause’s exact words).
    Drops. VG: further information missing, or given without its direction (U3); the offer whose benefit was taken not
    named, so that ‘its terms’ has no antecedent (U13); ‘provided by Rocky’ neither used nor tested; the ‘reasonable
    opportunity to reject’ or the knowledge element omitted so that AMPL’s best words are understated. Good: ‘Rocky is
    bound as guarantor’ bare; the benefit taken not named (§1(b)); the knowledge element stated and applied to no fact
    before ‘assented’ (§1(b); ‘attended’ or an equivalent fact absent). Credit wrong: the ticket-case ‘ought to have’ words
    stated as McHugh JA’s rule without the confinement (VG; a wrong rule). Fair: ‘ensure’ and ‘guarantee’ treated as the
    same with no reasons; ‘ensure … promptly’ attributed to Rocky as his words (D4); the clause said to ‘state’ or
    ‘record’ a guarantee (D4). Poor: Proscour’s damages statement applied as the Australian rule; Rocky’s silence held
    acceptance on Felthouse’s facts; Butler’s ‘last shot’ used as more than a remote analogy. Omitted.
    Marker comment. ‘Empirnall asks whether an offeree with a reasonable opportunity to reject took the benefit with
    knowledge of the terms: what benefit did Rocky take, and what does the problem tell us he knew of the clause?’

II.I3 Consideration moving from AMPL, conditional on II.F2; Pao On’s second condition on timing; AMPL’s
fallback put (4.0; C; floor 73) [Pao On 629 to 630; Roscorla 237]

    Issue. Whether AMPL, as promisee calling on Rocky’s undertaking, gave consideration for it.
    Rules at source. ‘Must flow between the parties in both directions’ [S2A slide 2 | slide only]; past consideration: ‘a
    consideration past and executed will support no other promise than such as would be implied by law’ [CB2A 113 |
    237 | Lord Denman CJ delivering the judgment of the Court, Roscorla]; Pao On: ‘An act done before the giving of a
    promise to make a payment or to confer some other benefit can sometimes be consideration for the promise. The act
    must have been done at the promisors’ request: the parties must have understood that the act was to be
    remunerated either by a payment or the conferment of some other benefit: and payment, or the conferment of a
    benefit, must have been legally enforceable had it been promised in advance.’ and its application, ‘The parties
    understood at the time of the main agreement that the restriction on selling must be compensated for by the benefit
    of a guarantee against a drop in price’ [PO | [1980] AC 614, 629–30 | the Board (Lord Scarman delivering) | ratio on
    the First Question]; the Board’s gloss, read in the official text; course-stated in substance only (Roscorla 237;
    Australian Woollen Mills (PC) 550, ‘The presence of a request does not, however, in itself establish a contract’), the
    words themselves sitting in the casebook’s omitted pp 126 to 130; never quoted, unpinnable (CRIT_fidelity_8 Finding
    4; ISSUES_SETTLED’s register line, ‘course-stated in substance’): the recital of a prior request ‘is not sufficient in
    itself’ and the second precondition ‘is simply one of the construction of the words of the contract in the
    circumstances of its making’ (O-3); a duty owed to a third party: Pao On 632 (II.C2); Glidewell LJ: ‘Pao On is a case of
    a tripartite relationship’ [CB2B 119 | 15]; AWM ‘really offered as consideration … really done in consideration’ (456
    to 457); Asquith LJ’s whole sentence [CB2A 102 | 460 | quoted by the Court | never cut to ‘an agreement to forbear
    … is a good consideration’ (D2)]; detriment to the promisee suffices (Musumeci 745).
    Facts (deciding). ‘27 February’ as the date of the advance; ‘Rocky asked’ (from v11 the first condition, the request,
    met on that word: UPGRADES_r1 U32, reversing CRIT_round3 M5 option (a)‘s move to credit); ’I will do that if you
    also promise to pay $10,000’ (the pricing that meets AMPL’s fallback: U2). Supporting (credit): Pierre’s expectation
    narrated only on 8 April and AMPL’s return being Grace’s interest (the two reasons why nothing shows a 27
    February understanding; credit for the same reason); nothing narrated on 27 February about a guarantee; AMPL
    entered the Loan Agreement with Grace at the same meeting.
    Core. (1) The point stated conditionally on II.F2: if AMPL promised the $10,000, that promise is fresh consideration
    moving from AMPL (strong on that view); if the $10,000 was Pierre’s, AMPL needs its own consideration and the 27
    February advance is past (Roscorla 237 footnoted on the words ‘the past advance’, its own marker, with Pao On 629
    to 630 on the exception carrying a second marker: one source for one proposition, R-R(3); the counted form is
    ‘AMPL’s own promise suffices; if Pierre promised, the past advance,[fn] under Pao On, suffices only where the
    parties ’understood that the act was to be remunerated’[fn]‘; from v11 the doctrine is NAMED once in the course’s
    term, past consideration (RG2A: ’What is the rule with respect to past consideration? Are there any exceptions to the
    general rule?’; S2A PDF page 20, ‘Past consideration’; S2A PDF page 23, ‘Exception to past consideration rule’; the
    v11 form ‘the past advance, past consideration,’ +2: UPGRADES_r1 U7; R-X(4)); v5’s ‘if AMPL, that promise suffices;
    if Pierre, the past advance’ elided the verb of each ‘if’ clause and read as note form, an R1 lapse under the type
    added in v6: CRIT_round5 M7, adopted under R-S(2)). (2) Pao On’s FIRST condition met on its fact, as the Board
    itself runs each condition on its fact (‘The act must have been done at the promisors’ request … All three features
    are present in this case. The promise given to Fu Chip under the main agreement not to sell the shares for a year
    was at Lau’s request’, Pao On extract, [1980] AC 614, 629 to 630): the advance was made at Rocky’s request, ‘Rocky
    asked’ (the v11 form ‘the past advance, made at Rocky’s request,’, +4: UPGRADES_r1 U32, folded into this item
because the ‘similar shape’ below is a guarantee given after an act done at the guarantor’s request); the third
condition is credit to state; and the SECOND condition as the contest: nothing narrated shows that the parties
understood on 27 February that the advance was to be remunerated by a benefit from Rocky, whereas in Pao On the
understanding existed ‘at the time of the main agreement’ (the comparison named: similar shape, different timing;
credit: the two reasons, Pierre’s ‘expected’ narrated only on 8 April and AMPL’s return being Grace’s interest). (3) A
conclusion in the tier for the strength on the alternative that Pierre promised (‘likely’), written ‘if Pierre promised’,
never ‘on that branch’ (R7), WITH AMPL’S FALLBACK PUT in a clause (CRIT_round5 M6, adopted in the ‘put’ form
under R-S(3)): AMPL’s entry into the Loan Agreement at the same meeting, tied by Pierre’s expectation to ‘payments
under the Loan Agreement’, is the course’s second candidate for consideration moving from AMPL
(ISSUES_SETTLED S-13, moderate), and a verdict that AMPL ‘gave none’ without naming it overstates the tier and
leaves a C unit’s counter unput; the counted form is the absolute clause ‘its entry into the Loan Agreement
remaining arguable consideration’ after ‘AMPL likely gave none’ (the noun ‘consideration’ added in v8, +1, so that
the clause says what the entry is arguable as: CRIT_round7 m7, adopted; v7’s ‘remaining arguable’ is not deducted,
the sentence being about consideration throughout); and, from v11, the fallback MET, not only put, or ACCEPTED
with the bargain requirement applied (UPGRADES_r1 U2, adopted as core and as a grading fix, §2 already listing
‘the fallback limb of II.I3’ among the units where a paragraph with no lean and no method is Good while v10’s
‘remaining arguable consideration’ gave that limb neither): the bargain test asks whether AMPL’s entry was ‘really
done in consideration of’ the undertaking (‘it should be made to appear that the statement or announcement which is
relied on as a promise was really offered as consideration for the doing of the act, and that the act was really done in
consideration of a potential promise inherent in the statement’, CB2A 101, Australian Woollen Mills 456 to 457, the
Court), and Rocky’s own words name another price, ‘I will do that if you also promise to pay $10,000 to me directly’;
the v11 form adds ‘, though Rocky priced his undertaking at’$10,000’’ after ‘remaining arguable consideration’ (+7),
on its own marker to Australian Woollen Mills 456 to 457. Accepting the fallback with the bargain requirement
applied (AMPL may read ‘also’ as ‘in addition to entering the Loan Agreement’) is equally Excellent (S-13,
moderate). Meeting it in the fuller form (the critic’s +12, the entry not ‘really done in consideration of’ the
undertaking stated in terms) is credit.
Credit. AMPL’s fallback met or accepted in a fuller form than core (3) requires: its entry into the Loan Agreement is
arguably ‘really done in consideration of’ the undertaking (or, if AMPL was already bound to Grace from 27
February, a promise to perform a duty owed to a third party: Pao On 632), a detriment sufficing though the benefit
went to Grace; against which the express words price the undertaking at $10,000 and AMPL had already advanced
(S-13; either conclusion Excellent if the bargain requirement is applied and the pricing point met or accepted);
Lampleigh’s ‘request’ and Bowen LJ’s ‘implication that at the time it was rendered it was to be paid for’ (the extract;
cited as quoted by Lord Scarman without an AC page pin); the recital of consideration in the guarantee clause as
further information (O-3); the AWM creditor-forbearance example labelled an analogy; the consistency points (T7:
Rocky cannot say his undertaking was given only for past consideration while claiming the $10,000 was given for it;
T17), each stated once; the further information that would reverse it (evidence of an understanding on 27 February).
Strength and tilt. Two limbs, each with its own rating (corrected to ISSUES_SETTLED S-12 and S-13: CRIT_round5
M6; R-S(3)): the past advance as consideration, unproven under Pao On’s second condition (moderate to strong
against AMPL; S-12), a contrary conclusion on that limb capped at Very Good; AMPL’s consideration on the Pierre
alternative taken as a whole, once the fallback is live, MODERATE (S-13), so that a conclusion that AMPL gave
consideration which rests on the fallback with the bargain requirement applied and the pricing point met or
accepted is Excellent, and ‘AMPL gave none’ is Excellent only with the fallback put and met (from v11: U2). v5’s
single ‘moderate to strong against AMPL’ line rated the whole question by its first limb and tilted the unit against
AMPL beyond the settled ratings, which on the Pierre alternative was bad advice to Rocky (told that AMPL has
nothing to enforce). An answer that uses Pierre’s ‘expected’ as evidence, marked ‘arguably’, that an understanding
existed on 27 February (the narrator’s past tense permits the inference) is that capped contrary conclusion; an
answer that places the understanding on 8 April misapplies the second condition’s timing (the condition’s own words
do not state the time; the timing is read from the Board’s application, ‘at the time of the main agreement’) and is
Fair on the brief’s ladder (a criterion misapplied, not law wrong), as the parallel item at II.G was moved in v7 (Part 9
item 37(d)); v7’s Poor (CRIT_complete_bias_2 B-7) is withdrawn to that extent, Poor being kept for the textbook (D1)
and for Asquith LJ cut (D2), a Pao On pin or report error being VG with D11 from v10 (CRIT_round7 m14, adopted;
Part 9 item 38; CRIT_round9 m5; R-U(6)). Not academic: live whenever the $10,000 is not AMPL’s promise.
Citation. Pao On v Lau Yiu Long [1980] AC 614, 629–30 and 632 only, as the Board (no judge) or ‘(Lord Scarman for
the Board)’, the course extract being headed ‘Judgement delivered by Lord Scarman’ and Glidewell LJ at 14 naming
him as ‘delivering the judgment of the Judicial Committee’ (R-P; CRIT_round3 M7, adopted: naming him is not
deducted). Roscorla v Thomas (1842) 3 QB 234, 237; 114 ER 496 (the QB pin after the QB report, before the ER
parallel, so that it does not read as an ER pinpoint: CRIT_fidelity_3 m5, adopted; the ER pin is not in the course
materials); naming Lord Denman CJ, who ‘delivered the judgment of the Court’, is accepted (R-P). A Pao On pin
other than 629–30 or 632, or a wrong report, is a D11 deduction. Marker placement: the Pao On marker on the
timing sentence sits directly after ‘at the time of the main agreement’, the words it supports, and the fallback clause
that follows (‘its entry into the Loan Agreement remaining arguable consideration’) carries no marker, because Pao
On does not support it and it states an arguable fact, not a rule (CRIT_round6 m3, adopted at no cost; v6’s fn 48
closed the sentence and so appeared to cite Pao On for the fallback); a drafter who footnotes the fallback clause to
Australian Woollen Mills 456 to 457 on its own marker, the bargain requirement it invokes, is graded the same.
Drops. VG: the AMPL alternative not stated conditionally; the second condition applied without the ‘at the time’
comparison; the fallback (AMPL’s entry into the Loan Agreement) not put on the Pierre alternative, or put and
neither met nor accepted (U2); the first condition stated without its fact (‘Rocky asked’: U32); ‘AMPL gave none’
    stated as clear; Pao On cited as [1980] AC 615 (the slide misprint), with the D11 deduction, a citation slip graded
    once, as his marker graded a citation slip VG (‘the reference should not include ss 28 and 32(1)’), R8 then capped by
    R-C at the lowest citation grade (CRIT_round9 m5; R-U(6): v9’s Poor triple-counted it). Good: ‘Pao On applies’ or
    ‘past consideration fails’ bare. Fair: Pao On’s three conditions recited and declared met without the ‘understood … at
    the time’ requirement applied. Fair also: Pao On said satisfied by an understanding formed on 8 April (the timing of
    the second condition misapplied: CRIT_round7 m14). Poor: Pao On cited to the textbook (D1); Asquith LJ cut (D2).
    Omitted. Credit wrong: the fallback stated without the bargain requirement; Lampleigh given an AC pin (D11). The
    doctrine applied without its course name, past consideration, is graded at R7, not here.
    Marker comments. ‘Apply Pao On’s conditions to the facts; the second is the contest, and the timing is the
    difference.’ ‘Ensure your citation precisely supports the proposition: 629–30 for past consideration, 632 for the third-
    party duty.’

II.I4 Timing on both readings of the 12 months; the arguable March instalment; consequences conditional on
II.F2 (5.0; C; floor 68)

    Facts (deciding). ‘repayment of the Loan’; ‘repayable at the expiry of 12 months’; ‘8 February 2025’. Supporting:
    ‘when Grace had not yet repaid the Loan’; ‘I have until 8 April 2025’; ‘you won’t see a cent before then’; ‘AMPL
    required the $1,150,000 as soon as possible’ (motive; cannot accelerate the term: Ermogenous [25]); AMPL called on
    9 March 2025, ‘arguably’ the day after a monthly date if the instalments fell on the 8th (an inference: R-M).
    Core. (1) Whether the call was premature turns on the 12 months, cross-referred as ‘the 12 months (G)’ within the
    same Part (R-Q; never a bare ‘turns on G’), not on any rule of guarantee law (credit: the clause as described
    concerns ‘repayment of the Loan’, and the Loan was ‘repayable at the expiry of 12 months’, quoted again here). (2)
    Both readings applied: from 27 February the call was not premature; from 8 April it was premature as to principal
    (v12: v11’s ‘which only postpones liability’ is a guarantee-law statement and is removed from the core and from
    credit under R-Y(4), the answer not arguing guarantee law) (credit: if Grace does not repay by 8 April 2025 the 12
    months will then have run on either reading, and if Rocky then pays, the $10,000 condition fails: T13, II.H). (3) On
    the 8 April reading the bad news in a clause: the payment record ends at ‘8 February 2025’, which makes an unpaid
    March 2025 instalment arguable (the hedged form is required: ‘appropriate monthly repayments of the interest’
    between 8 April 2024 and 8 February 2025 ‘proves that interest was paid monthly, as the Loan Agreement required,
    in that period, and no more’ (R-M), so ‘payments end at ’8 February 2025’’ or ‘Grace stopped paying after 8
    February’ states more than the problem proves and is a D4 fault: UPGRADES_r1 U16; ‘interest is shown paid only to
    ’8 February 2025’’ is equally Excellent; the uncertainty and its effect being stated in this ‘arguable’ clause, ‘whether
    the March 2025 instalment was paid’ is not an R6 further-information item: U3) (credit: that it engages the oral
    content, ensuring ‘all payments … promptly’, and not the clause’s principal-only content as described; whether it was
    paid as further information). (4) The client’s position stated conditionally on II.F2: if AMPL promised the $10,000,

    words for v9’s ten: v9’s ‘exposure and claim both lie against him’ stated unhedged a consequence II.I3 leaves
    arguable on a moderate point (S-13), which §1 grades VG as overstatement, so the identity row’s ‘stated’ in v9
    certified an Excellent the tier rule did not give: CRIT_round9 M1; R-U(2); ‘both’ is written with its nouns, and no
    pronoun has a wrong or unclear antecedent (R1 as amended by R-V(1) and CRIT_round10 m3; CRIT_round3 m11);
    II.I1: AMPL is then a non-party to the undertaking, the clause alone cannot bind Rocky, and AMPL can enforce only
    the clause, on II.I2’s assent and II.I3’s consideration; credit: the chain through II.I3 stated, AMPL likely having given
    none on that alternative (II.I3), its entry into the Loan Agreement remaining arguable consideration).
    Credit. Grace’s ‘you won’t see a cent before then’ as the second fact supporting the March default; AMPL’s need as

    Strength and tilt. Timing open (S-1), carried from II.G. ‘The call was premature’ or ‘the call was valid’ stated as

Part 7. Global rows (20%), scoring rule and automatic deductions

Global-row cap (R-C). A global row cannot be graded above the lowest grade, among the units, of the core item it
summarises: R2 at most the lowest application grade (the authority applied by its own criterion; the comparison named at
K and C units); R4 at most Very Good if any unit is Omitted and at most Good if a claim is missed; R6 at most the lowest
further-information or strength grade; R8 at most the lowest citation grade; R10 at most the lowest consistency grade; R1,
R3, R5, R7 and R9 are graded independently. Reason: his real marker left the global rows ungraded in Part A, so the
global block must compound unit failures, never offset them.

R1. Style, clarity of expression, conciseness, grammar & spelling, persuasiveness of argument: his voice (4.0)

Measured against his LAWS1061 Part A (the authority) and VOICE.md (the measurement), with python3 -I voice_stats_v2.py
on the body (headings, footnotes and the count line are stripped by the script). - Excellent requires all of: flowing multi-
clause sentences joined by semicolons (the next step) and colons (claim, then proof); whole-answer measures within the
range of his Parts I to VI (mean sentence length 17.8 to 24.7 words; sentences under 12 words 9.1% to 36.4%, all verdicts
or one-line frames and never two in a row inside a paragraph; semicolons 0.85 to 2.02 per 100 words; colons 0.81 to 2.57
per 100 words; sentences containing a semicolon or colon 36.4% to 72.7%); the ranges apply to the whole body, not per
unit, so K and C units are written in his 20 to 25-word sentences and the one-sentence L forms are balanced by short
verdicts (the appendix shows the balance: mean 24.0; 10.0% under 12 words; 1.04; 0.90; 41.7%; the mean sits near the
top of his range, so a drafter who lengthens sentences further falls out of it: every fix that adds a clause to an existing
sentence pushes the mean up, so additions go in as new sentences or by splitting a long one, never as a further clause:
CRIT_round4 m14); a short counter-frame (‘AMPL would say nothing bound until execution’) and an element sub-verdict
(‘AMPL being ’owned and controlled’ by Pierre supplies consent’; ‘His payment therefore likely does not meet it’) count as
his frames and verdicts under VOICE.md F9 (his Part A: ‘On the other hand, only SPC knew its finances and that GR
would rely’), so a drafter does not merge them to be safe and fall below the 9.1% floor (CRIT_round4 m14, adopted); the
rule-then-proof join written as a colon, because the L form (‘fact, rule, so result’) produces semicolons and ‘so’ but few
colons; every semicolon joins a step to the step it follows from (a sentence of three or more semicolon-joined clauses each
a conclusion without its reason is conclusive and an R1 lapse); his signposts (‘Firstly’, ‘Secondly, however,’, ‘Decisively,’,
‘Nor does … assist:’, ‘… is distinguishable:’, ‘… is analogous because …’, ‘The strongest counter is …’, ‘On balance,’, ‘the
better view is that … likely …’) used where they fit and never at a required rate: the requirement is ‘no signpost he never
uses’, ‘Whether … turns on’ as an opener in at most about 1 in 6 sentences, ordinals where three or more factors are
weighed as separate sentences (his stacked-facts sentence, VOICE.md 2.3 examples 14 and 15, weighs three or four
quoted facts inside one sentence without ordinals and is equally his form), valence tags only on factor sentences
(CRIT_round3 m9, adopted in part: the appendix uses ‘Decisively’, ‘On balance’, ‘the better view’, ‘Yet’, ‘whereas’,
‘distinguishable’ and no ordinals, which is within his usage); no signposts he never uses (‘Moreover’, ‘Furthermore’,
‘Additionally’, ‘Here,’, ‘In this case,’, ‘Notably’; ‘Here,’ and ‘In this case,’ as sentence openers only, a mid-sentence
locative ‘here’ not being a lapse: I.F2 Credit; CRIT_round8 m3); no direct question with a question mark in the body (his
Part A has none; a question is put as a ‘whether’ clause: CRIT_round4 m9); none of this sheet’s analytic words (‘narrator’,
‘narrated’, ‘narrative’, ‘the facts narrate’: R-R(1)); NO META LANGUAGE AND NO SLIDE CITATION (R-Z(1), (2), (5)):
body and footnotes never mention weeks, classes, lectures, slides, the course, the readings, the syllabus, the rubric or the
assignment, and never frame anything as ‘outside the scope’ (or ‘outside Weeks 1 to 4’), the answer being advice to
Rocky; one such instance, or one slide citation, caps this row at VG, and two or more cap it at Good, whatever the rest of
the row earns (the same instances are graded at R8; §1); parenthetical cross-references (‘(D, E)’, ‘(D)’, ‘(II.G)’, ‘(G)’) are
not in his register (his Part A carries none, writing ‘likewise’ and ‘again’), so at most about one per Part is his form, the
rest written as ‘a question for intention and certainty’ or ‘the 12 months’ where words allow; the counted model spends
four because the count forbids the longer forms, and they are counted words, not free (CRIT_round4 m19, adopted as the
rule); ‘both’ written with its nouns, and no pronoun whose antecedent is wrong or unclear, that is, whose nearest
antecedent is a different person or noun from the one meant (‘Rocky’s detriment’, never ‘his’ after an ‘If Pierre’ clause:
CRIT_round4 M3, m17); a pronoun whose antecedent is right and clear is never a lapse, wherever the antecedent sits (‘it
is likely an offer’; ‘meet it’; ‘Rocky likely gave none’ after a sentence about consideration), v10’s wider wording (‘written
with its … antecedent in the same sentence’) being withdrawn because the counted model itself breached it without any
loss of clarity (R-V(1); CRIT_round10 m3); the scope of the question never restated at length (a conciseness fault here, not
at P: CRIT_round4 m17); verdict tiers matched to contest (Part 1); fact quotations short (median about 3 words), mid-
sentence, inside his own grammar, the legal word outside the quote; no sentence beginning with a quotation; quoted
words in the body at most 20% of the body and no single quotation over 20 words (R-G; the appendix spends 17.3%, 249
words against a ceiling of 299, so drafters have about 50 words of headroom and must paraphrase credit facts:
CRIT_round6 m13 corrects v6’s ‘about 40’); an accurate paraphrase of a judge’s hedge (‘could read as’, for Lord Diplock’s
‘might have been understood’) is accepted where the quoted words would cost the count; no sentence whose only
reasoning is a quoted phrase followed by ‘so’ or ‘thus’ and a result; present tense for law, past for facts; third person; zero
em dashes, en dashes as punctuation, double quotation marks, contractions, first person, metaphor or figurative idiom,
rhetorical or corporate framing (‘It is important to note’, ‘Ultimately’, ‘Crucially’, ‘Overall’, ‘In conclusion’), intensifiers
(‘clearly’, ‘very’, ‘significantly’), distanced hedges (‘It could be argued that’, ‘perhaps’); no doctrinal initialisms; defined
short names only for parties and documents (‘AMPL’, the ‘Loan Agreement’, ‘Loan’); no semicolon after a subordinate
clause or before a fragment; no comma splice; NO NOTE-FORM ELLIPSIS, that is, no clause whose subject or finite verb
must be supplied from an earlier clause (‘if AMPL, that promise suffices’; ‘given for its ’12 months’‘), the Instructions
marking ’the quality of the writing and the clarity of expression’ and a UNSW marker reading such a clause as note form
(CRIT_round5 M7, adopted; R-S(2)): each instance is a lapse under the VG line below, two make VG, and the counted
model carries none. THREE FURTHER LAPSE TYPES from v11 (UPGRADES_r1 U29; the Instructions’ criterion ‘the
quality of the writing and the clarity of expression’; four of six Stage-5 round-1 cold readers flagged instances in forms
this sheet had prescribed): (a) ZEUGMA, one verb governing two nouns it fits differently (‘claim and likely exposure lie
against him’: a claim lies against a person, an exposure does not); (b) WRONG COLLOCATION, a verb or noun that cannot
take the object it is given (‘AMPL enforces its own money’: money is not enforced, a loan or a contract is); (c) an
ANSWER-EXTERNAL DEICTIC, a pointer whose referent is outside the answer or cannot be found from the sentence (‘the
guarantee clause just named’, where nothing in the answer has just named it). Each is a lapse counted with the others
under the VG line; v10’s claim that the counted model carried none of these was untrue for the three forms quoted, each
now corrected at its unit (I.F3, II.F1, II.I4). Hedge density (CRIT_round5 m7, noted): the model carries ‘likely’ at about 12
per 1,000 words against his 7.0, ‘arguably’ at 5.5 against 3.2 and ‘though’ at 5.5 against 3.2 (8 in 1,442 words is 5.5 per
1,000, as the v10 appendix printed: CRIT_round10 m10), because the tier rule and R-M drive them; none is a checked
range, but ‘likely’ is for the moderate-to-strong tier only and a strong point takes the unhedged ‘therefore’ form, so a
drafter does not hedge a strong point to be safe. Colon margin (CRIT_round5 m8, noted): the model’s colons (0.90 per 100
words) and semicolons (1.04) sit near the floors of his ranges (0.81; 0.85) because the L form yields ‘so’ rather than a
colon, so a drafter writes an L unit’s rule-then-application join as a colon where it reads naturally (‘Acceptance requires
an external manifestation of assent: ’Confirmed’ …‘) and does not remove one to be safe. Cross-reference parentheticals
(’(F)‘,’(II.G)‘) count as words and are not free. - VG: one measure outside his range, or up to two lapses (eg a single one-
clause run; one stacked-assertion sentence); one meta reference or one slide citation (R-Z(5)). Good: a pattern of one-
clause runs, or two measures outside range; a quotation mosaic (quoted words over 20%); two or more meta references or
slide citations, in any combination (R-Z(5)). Fair: condensed, note-like or relabelled prose; repetition across Parts. Poor:
prose that reads as generated (metaphor, rhetoric, stacked assertions without reasons). - Marker comments. ’Avoid one-
clause sentences in runs; join steps with semicolons and colons.’ ‘No metaphor or rhetorical framing.’ ‘This is advice to
the client: never refer to weeks, slides or the course.’ ‘Quote facts briefly and keep the legal characterisation outside the
quotation.’
